# Implementation notes

Status: **working log, for review with the project owner.** Decisions made
while implementing (`I…`), and considerations to discuss. Design decisions
stay in the design documents ([README.md](README.md)); a note here that
changes the design says so, and the design gets the change once the owner
agrees. Decisions taken with the owner and open decision points are in
[05-decision-log.md](05-decision-log.md).

Benchmarks are in [02-benchmarks.md](02-benchmarks.md). Tests:
`cargo test --workspace --release`; the crash test's seed count is
`HS_CRASH_SEEDS` (300 by default; a 5,000-seed run, 30,000 crashes,
passed on 2026-10-20).

## Where things stand (2026-10-20)

Built, on branch `impl/hs0-hs1`:

| Milestone | Built | Not yet |
|---|---|---|
| HS0 | Engine (general objects, group commit, deferred writes, checkpoints, recovery; dense block maps and fuzzy checkpoints, D43), device back ends (memory, pread/pwrite, io_uring `O_DIRECT`, fault injection), crash tests, benchmarks | NVMe passthrough back end and the G5 comparison with SPDK (needs a spare namespace, I11) |
| HS1 | Placement, wire protocol, reactor, cluster map and map service, storage daemon with replication and group commit, client, `hyperstord` and `hyperstor-meta` binaries, end-to-end tests and benchmark; the replicated map service: 3 or 5 members over Raft (`glidex-raftstore`), a stored map, leader redirects and the takeover wait (I62–I67) | `.meta` header objects and volume leases (06-metadata.md §4.2, §9) |
| HS2 | Liveness by heartbeats, degraded acting sets, backfill with a whole-object copy (including a node that returns with stale data: reset, refilled, stand-ins dropped), map leases with fencing and pushes (D42), peer reports (D40), cache pools without expiry or eviction (D45, I40), failure end-to-end tests, a real multi-process failure check (`kill -9` of a node: writes go on with two copies); delta recovery and light scrub by content digests (I57, I58) | the PG log (09-replication.md §8.3), deep scrub, blocklist and forced takeovers (06-metadata.md §9), throttling |

### Review

The points raised here were reviewed with the owner on 2026-10-20; the
answers, and the two points still open (map leases, checkpoint spikes)
with their trade-offs, are in [05-decision-log.md](05-decision-log.md). Settled
points: D38 (I14), D39 (I19), D40 (I21, I25), D41 (I12, I23, I24), D42 (I20).
D45 is partly built (I40).

## Engine (HS0)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I1 | **A device is split into shards, one per reactor**, each with its own log ring, allocator and index; a PG lives in one shard. | No locks or cross-core traffic on the I/O path (08-engine.md §6.1), and recovery runs per shard in parallel. | Load across shards depends on PG placement; moving a PG between shards of one device (a local backfill) is not built yet. |
| I2 | **Commits are batch-synchronous (group commit).** `Shard::commit` resolves a batch of lists, writes new data extents and the log record together, flushes once, and returns. The reactor calls it with everything that arrived since its last pass. | The simplest design that is correct and still amortizes the flush over a batch. | The reactor waits during the flush. On NVMe with power-loss protection a flush costs microseconds; on drives without it, milliseconds. A completion-driven engine (submit, return to polling, finish on completion) is the next step once the reactor carries real traffic (HS1). |
| I3 | **The index lives in memory; a checkpoint is the whole index serialized** into freshly allocated extents, named by the shard's superblock. The on-device copy-on-write B+tree of 08-engine.md §7.1 is deferred. | Small and easy to verify; recovery is load plus log replay. | Open: 05-decision-log.md, point 5 (fuzzy checkpoint now, paged index next). |
| I4 | **One flush per commit round**, not a flush of new data and then of the log record. Recovery checks the last commit group's new data against its checksums, and drops a record whose data didn't reach the media, with everything after it. Only blocks the replayed index still maps to the same place are checked. | Halves flushes per commit. A dropped record was never acknowledged: rounds are sequential, so only the last group can be incomplete. | Correct only while rounds don't overlap. If commits are pipelined later, recovery must check every group not known to be flushed. |
| I5 | **The log's checksums are seeded with a random salt that changes every time a shard is opened**, and opening always ends with a checkpoint under the new salt. | Stale blocks from an earlier pass of the ring or an earlier format, and tenant data that happens to look like a record (deferred writes carry tenant bytes into the log), never pass as records. | The salt is 32 bits and CRC32C is not a MAC: forging a record still needs the salt guessed, and a crash at the right moment. A keyed MAC per record would close it at some CPU cost. |
| I6 | **Revisions come from a shard-wide counter** and are never reused, even across delete and recreate. | Compare-and-set on revisions can't suffer ABA. | — |
| I7 | **A write is never deferred onto blocks allocated in the same round.** | Recovery's data check (I4) compares those blocks with the checksums their `Map` logged; an in-place write in the same round would contradict it and drop an acknowledged group. | — |
| I8 | **Data offsets and lengths are 4 KiB aligned.** | Matches the device and the checksum granularity. | S3 objects and file tails need padding by the caller (the S3 gateway, the filesystem client), with the logical size kept separately (`Truncate`). |
| I9 | **`Truncate` keeps the bytes of a partial last block** past the new size. | Avoids a read-modify-write. | A later extension shows the old bytes. Filesystem semantics need zeroing there (00-filesystem.md). |
| I10 | **Assertions are checked against the object before the list**, not at their position in it. | Simple to reason about: a list is validated whole, then applied, so a refused list never leaves a trace. | — |
| I11 | **NVMe passthrough (`uring_cmd`) is not implemented yet.** The block back end (io_uring, `O_DIRECT`) serves every device. | The development host has one NVMe device and it holds the root filesystem; passthrough needs a namespace of its own. | G5 is only half run (02-benchmarks.md); passthrough and its benchmark need a test host with a spare namespace. |
| I12 | **Write data is copied once** into an aligned buffer before it goes to the device. | Network buffers aren't aligned yet. | Zero copy comes with registered buffers shared by the reactor's network and disk paths (08-engine.md §6.2). |
| I14 | **The deferral threshold is a per-shard setting** (`Shard::set_defer_max`). Storage nodes turn deferral off unless told the device is a hard disk (`hyperstord --hdd`). | On flash, deferral writes small overwrites twice; redirecting them reached 1.6× the 4 KiB write throughput on dev-1 (02-benchmarks.md). | **Settled: D38.** |
| I13 | **A checkpoint must fit in 200 extents** (named in one superblock). | Keeps the superblock one block. | On a badly fragmented shard a checkpoint can fail with `NoSpace`; a chained extent list removes the limit. |

### Bugs the tests found

Across the sessions, tests found these bugs before anything else did:

1. Engine: `[Delete, Touch]` deleted the object (crash test).
2. Engine: recovery checked freed and reused blocks, dropping an
   acknowledged group (crash test).
3. Placement: positional EC placement moved twice the necessary shards
   (movement test, I16).
4. Daemon: a write could be acknowledged without a target that had just
   finished backfilling (cluster test, 2 in 25 runs; I26).
5. Reactor: a task that yields in a loop starved all I/O, because each
   pass ran ready tasks until none were left (the returning-node cluster
   test hung). Each pass now runs only the tasks ready when it starts;
   `a_yielding_task_does_not_starve_io` guards it.

7. Engine: a deferred write lost when an absorb moved its block in the
   same round, and a deferred write replayed onto a remapped block
   (crash test, once checkpoints were paged; I149).

Found in review while designing leases:

6. Map: a PG whose acting members were all down was reset to an empty
   acting set, so when the members returned, backfill from the empty
   primary would have wiped them. Such a PG now stays down with its acting
   set until a member returns (`a_pg_whose_copies_are_all_down_stays_down`).

### Details of the engine bugs

The randomized crash test (`crates/hyperstor-engine/tests/crash.rs`) found
two engine bugs before any other test did:

1. `[Delete, Touch]` in one list deleted the object; `Touch` must recreate
   it. Fixed by tracking whether the object is alive through the list.
2. Recovery's data check (I4) looked at blocks that a later part of the
   same group had freed. Those can legitimately be reused (by the next
   round's data, or by the checkpoint an earlier recovery wrote), so an
   acknowledged group was dropped. Fixed by checking only blocks the
   replayed index still references.

## Placement (HS1)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I15 | **The placement hashes are spelled out** (SplitMix64 mixing, FNV-1a for names), with pinned test values, instead of `std`'s hashers. | Every node and client must compute the same placement from the same map, across Rust releases and architectures. | Changing them is a format change: it would move every PG. |
| I16 | **EC shard positions are kept in the PG table, not recomputed.** `place` chooses the members; `assign_positions` keeps each surviving member at its shard index and fills vacated positions with new members. | A stateless positional choice (CRUSH-style `indep`, tried first) moved 2.1× the necessary shards when a device was removed, because changed positions collide with others. With kept positions, only shards whose device left the set move (2,068 of 30,000 in the test, against 3,176). | The metadata service must keep positions per EC PG in its acting set (07-placement.md §5.3 already keeps acting sets). |
| I17 | **Weights are hierarchical**: a device's weight counts for its host and rack, and choices go rack, host, device. | Load follows capacity at every level, which is what keeps devices evenly full. | Removing one device moves 1.44× the theoretical minimum of PGs in the test (its host's share drops, so some PGs that used the host's other devices move too). CRUSH behaves the same. A flat choice over devices would move less but couldn't keep copies in distinct hosts by weight. |

## Map, protocol, reactor, daemon, client (HS1)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I18 | **(Superseded by I62–I67.)** **The map service is a single in-memory map** (`hyperstor_meta::serve`), not yet the Raft group of D2/D20. | HS1 needed a map to route by; Raft through `glidex-raftstore` needs that crate extracted from glidex first (a glidex change). | Until then the map service is a single point of failure for map changes (not for I/O, D23). |
| I19 | **Each reactor listens on its own address, recorded per device in the map.** | No cross-thread hand-off of sockets, and tests can bind ephemeral ports before the map exists. | **Settled: D39.** |
| I20 | **(Superseded by D42.)** **Storage nodes poll the map every 200 ms**; replicas refuse `Repl` for a PG epoch they don't have. | Simple, and it bounds how long a stale primary can find willing replicas. | Open: 05-decision-log.md, point 3. |
| I21 | **A write fails if any acting member fails** (`Unavailable`), and the client retries until the map service has marked the node down and the PG's acting set has shrunk (I25). | No in-sync shrinking on a primary's say-so: one authority (the map service's liveness) decides who is out. | **Settled: D40** (nodes report each other; to build in HS2). |
| I22 | **A read of a chunk never written returns zeros** from the primary. | Thin volumes. | — |
| I23 | **Payloads are copied into each outgoing frame**, once per replica. | Simplest correct path. | Zero copy with registered buffers and vectored sends (`SEND_ZC`) is the next CPU win (02-benchmarks.md, end to end). |
| I24 | **The reactor's executor is a simple FIFO of ready tasks**, woken through an eventfd from other threads, with an `AsyncCancel` sent for operations whose future is dropped. | Small (about 400 lines) and enough for the data path; buffers stay owned by the reactor until the kernel completes, so cancellation is safe. | No adaptive polling yet: an idle reactor sleeps in `io_uring_enter`, which costs QD1 latency when requests cross cores (02-benchmarks.md, end to end). |

## Failure handling (HS2, first part)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I25 | **Liveness by heartbeats to the map service** (every 150 ms from each reactor; a node is down after a timeout, 600 ms in tests and 2 s in the benchmark). Each PG has an `up` set (placement) and an `acting` set (who has every acknowledged write). When a node goes down, `acting` keeps its live members in order, so the new primary is a surviving member that has every acknowledged write. | Writes are acknowledged only when every acting member has them (§8.1), so any surviving member is complete. Keeping order avoids an election. | Detection by the map service only: a node that can reach the map service but not its peers isn't detected (06-metadata.md §4.1 asks peers to report each other; not built). |
| I26 | **Backfill**: the primary resets the target's copy of the PG, then pushes every object whole, each read taken only when no commit is queued and sent on the same connection as live writes. Live writes also go to the target meanwhile (best effort). If any write or push to the target fails, the backfill starts over. When it finishes, the target becomes *required* for every write before the primary waits out in-flight writes to it and reports `BackfillDone`; the map service then adds it to `acting`, and once all of `up` is there, `acting = up`. | Ordering on one connection makes each pushed object exactly the primary's state at that point, with later writes following. Making the target required before reporting closes a race the cluster test found (2 failures in 25 runs before the fix, 0 in 30 after): a write could be acknowledged while its copy to a target that had just joined `acting` failed. | Objects are pushed whole, holes included (reads materialize zeros), so a backfill writes every byte of each chunk; an extent list from the engine would send only what's there. No throttling or priorities yet (09-replication.md §8.5). A node that returns with old data gets a reset and a full copy, not a delta from the PG log (no PG log yet). |
| I27 | **Storage nodes refuse `Repl` and `Push` for PGs they are in neither `acting` nor `up` of**, and for PG epochs they don't have. | A node that is no longer a member must not accept writes. | — |

## Binaries

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I28 | **`hyperstord` and `hyperstor-meta` take flags and a plain-text cluster file** (`hyperstor_meta::parse_cluster`), no configuration library. | Enough to run a cluster by hand; no extra dependencies. | glidex will drive these (16-glidex.md §17); the cluster file is a stopgap until the map service has its management API (§15). `hyperstord` creates sparse files for shard devices; on ext4 the first write to each block then costs a journal update. Use block devices, or files written once in full. |

## Leases (HS2, D42)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I29 | **Leases come from the map service**, with every heartbeat answer, and run from when the node *sent* the heartbeat; the map service measures expiry from when it granted the lease plus a quarter. Storage reactors send heartbeats every 100 ms. | The node's lease always ends before the map service believes it has, whatever the network delay; the quarter covers clock *rate* differences between hosts (no synchronized clocks needed). | Defaults: lease 0.6 s, liveness timeout 0.8 s (D48; until 2026-10-21, 2 s and 3 s; the timeout must be at least 1.25 × the lease; `hyperstor-meta` refuses otherwise). With D40, detection can drop below the timeout; the lease remains the floor of failover time. |
| I30 | **A fence is acknowledged only after the device's in-flight writes for the fenced PGs are answered.** | A write accepted under the old role and answered after the new map took effect could otherwise be acknowledged without reaching the new acting set. | — |
| I31 | **The RPC connection moved to its own crate**, `hyperstor-rpc` (re-exported as `hyperstor_client::rpc`). | The map service now calls storage reactors (fences, pushes) and couldn't depend on the client crate, which depends on it. | — |
| I32 | **Test hook `NodeConfig::meta_blocked`** cuts a node off from the map service in both directions (heartbeats, map fetches, pushes, fences) while clients and peers can still reach it. | Lets the cluster test reproduce the partition leases exist for. Removing the lease check makes that test fail with a stale read, which shows the test checks what it should. | Test-only; a fault-injection layer for the network (drops, delays, partitions between any two nodes) is the general form, for the HS2 fault rig. |

## Dense maps and fuzzy checkpoints (D43)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I33 | **Fuzzy checkpoints** (08-engine.md §7.3): one starts when a quarter of the log is used; each commit round writes a slice (64 KiB, eight times that once half the log is used); it finishes at once if the log reaches three quarters first. The blob is a stream of objects; a directory, named by the superblock, lists the blob's extents (no limit on how many). Recovery replays from the checkpoint's start over the fuzzy image. | Removes the checkpoint pause (02-benchmarks.md: worst commit 3.8 ms whole, 0.15 ms with 64 KiB slices). Replay over a fuzzy image works because every log record sets state absolutely; the one exception, deferred writes onto blocks a later trim unmapped, is skipped during replay. The crash tests run with 1 KiB slices, so about 400 of each run's crashes land mid-checkpoint; a planted bug (log head at the end instead of the start) fails them at once. | Checkpoints still rewrite the whole index (2 GiB per TiB with dense maps), only spread out. Paged checkpoints (05-decision-log.md, point 5, option B) would write only what changed. |
| I34 | **Dense maps switch by estimated memory**, with hysteresis: to dense when runs cost more than 8 bytes per block of span, back to runs below 60% of that (a contiguous chunk is about 51%). A dense map is only reconsidered after a change of at least an eighth of its span. 32-bit physical blocks: a shard over 16 TiB keeps runs for blocks beyond. | Measured: every chunk was dense after only 5% random overwrites, so dense is the steady state for VM disks; the threshold matters less than first thought. Reconsidering after every write scanned 1,024 slots and made commits 13× slower; the size condition removes that. | — |
| I35 | **Run overhead calibrated to 128 bytes** (was an assumed 96), from the process's growth per run in the aging benchmark. | Decides when a chunk goes dense. | — |
| I36 | **Benchmarks for decisions are a repeatable suite**: `hyperstor-bench decisions` and `redb`, run through `scripts/collect-decision-data.sh`, which records the host; the procedure is the agent skill `.claude/skills/collect-decision-data`. | The owner decides from data; the data must be repeatable, with the host and the exact commands. | — |
| I37 | **`Shard::stats()` is constant-time; index-wide totals are `index_stats()`.** | The decision-data run caught a 3.5× regression in replicated writes: with dense maps, `stats()` walked every block map, and the daemon called it after every commit. | Benchmarks belong in every session for exactly this reason (the skill's step 2). |
| I38 | **Adaptive polling** (`hyperstor_reactor::set_spin`): after activity, a reactor polls (entering the kernel without waiting, so network completions arrive) for the window before sleeping. `hyperstord` defaults to 200 µs (`--spin-us`); everything else to 0. | QD1 latency −15 to −23%, 4-reactor write p99 −80% (02-benchmarks.md, adaptive polling). | The window is a CPU cost at low load; see 05-decision-log.md point 16. |
| I39 | **Peer reports** (D40): every storage reactor pings, every 100 ms, the nodes it shares a PG with, and reports one silent for the peer timeout (`hyperstord --peer-timeout-ms`, default 0.3 s since D48; 1 s before) to the map service; a node reported by two distinct nodes within half the liveness timeout is marked down and gets no lease until the reports stop. | Measured: failover 3.0 → 2.5 s at the default lease, 0.78 → 0.55 s with a 0.5 s lease (02-benchmarks.md, failover). The cluster test fails with peer reports turned off. | "Two distinct nodes" doesn't yet require different failure domains (the cluster file's racks); with one rack per site, any two nodes count. Pings scale with the number of peer nodes, not PGs. |


## Cache pools (D45, first part)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I40 | **A cache pool is a third pool kind** (`pool N name cache copies K` in the cluster file; tag 2 in the map codec). Its acting set is always its up set: no backfill, no stand-ins. A read of an object the shard doesn't have answers `NotFound` rather than zeros. A storage reactor that sees a cache PG at an epoch it hasn't served before deletes everything it holds for that PG, committed before the request goes on. | A node returning with an old copy must not serve it: a write made while it was away went to another node, and placement can hand the PG back. The cluster test writes different data while the holder is down and fails if the old copy is ever read back; it caught that the first version queued the deletes without committing them, so a read on the new epoch still saw the old copy (reads don't pass through the commit queue). | Not built: expiry, eviction under space pressure, least-recently-read tracking. With `copies` > 1, any change of membership empties every copy, including those that stayed; keeping survivors would need a per-PG "last written epoch". The purge costs one delete per object of the PG on the first request after a change. |

## Administration (AD0 and AD1, first part)

Built on branch `impl/admin-ad0-ad1`: the cluster API on the map
service, `hyperstorctl`, and `hyperstor-install` with host checks and an
all-in-one trial cluster (19-administration.md §23).

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I41 | **The cluster API runs in `hyperstor-meta` on its own thread** (`--api ADDR`): a single-threaded tokio runtime serving connectrpc (D51), with each call sent to the map service's reactor over a cross-thread channel (`Meta::admin`) and answered on a one-shot. Reads answer from the published map; `SetDeviceIn` changes the working map, which the publisher fences and publishes as for any other change. | The map service's state is `Rc` on its reactor, and the data path has no tokio (D3); a channel keeps the control path off the reactor's core (D58) without locks around the map. | Settled: two ports are fine (D69). |
| I42 | **The API takes mutual TLS** (`hyperstor-meta --api-tls DIR`) and authorizes by the client certificate's identity: any identity of this cluster may read except `host`; only `admin` and `mgmt` may change the cluster; another cluster's certificate is refused. Without `--api-tls` it is plain HTTP and allows everything, for loopback and tests. The device in/out request went only into the API: an earlier draft also added it to the D5 protocol and was removed. | D53, D56 and §16's roles. The D5 port faces the storage network, which shouldn't carry administrative changes. The end-to-end test checks a node's certificate is refused a change, a host's a read, a stranger's the handshake, and plain HTTP everything; making `authorize` allow all fails it. | The D5 ports (map service, storage nodes) still have no TLS (D25). |
| I43 | **Health is computed by the map service** (`hyperstor_meta::health`) from the published map: a PG counts once, under its worst state: inactive (no primary, or fewer than `min_size` copies), backfilling, degraded (fewer than `size`), else clean. Inactive is `error`, the rest of the states and any node down or device out are `warn`. | 15-operations.md §16's levels, from what clients see; the API, `hyperstorctl` and its exit status share one definition. | Fixed in the API rather than the map: a node the map service hasn't heard from since it started is a `warn` reason ("not heard from since the map service started"), and every node reports `last_heard_ms`; the map still counts it up for the grace period (I21), which placement relies on. |
| I44 | **`hyperstorctl` is written by hand over the generated client**, not generated from the protos as D55 says. | Three RPCs; a generator and its CI check ("every RPC has a command") cost more than they save at this size. | When to switch: at the next service, or when the RPC count passes a handful. |
| I45 | **`out` and `in` mark devices in or out of placement** (`SetDeviceIn`, by device or every device of a node) and return at once. | The map service already moves PGs and backfills on any map change; this exposes it. | With as many failure domains as copies, an out device's PGs have nowhere to go and run degraded until it is back in: that is servicing, not draining. `--wait` polls until nothing backfills or is inactive; a real drain should be an `Operation` (§23.3) and should refuse when it would leave PGs degraded. The map isn't persisted (I18), so a map service restart brings every device back in. |
| I46 | **`hyperstor-install` takes a subcommand** (`check`, `install`, `uninstall`), unlike glidex's flag-only installer, and for now only `--all-in-one`. It takes binaries from its own directory rather than building, writes files by write-and-rename and only when they differ, keeps the cluster id across runs, restarts only the services whose binary, unit or configuration changed, and with `--root` writes under a directory without touching users or systemd. | Run without arguments it must print usage (the docs check does that on CI); installing on a bare run would be dangerous. Roles, devices and joining (§23.4.2) need join tokens and identities. `--root` makes the whole install testable without root. | Run as root on dev-2 on 2026-10-21, at the owner's request: every service and the timer came up under systemd; health ok; `io-test` 64 MiB on the replicated pool (115 MiB/s write, 251 MiB/s read, 1 GiB sparse files) and on the cache pool; stopping `hyperstord@2` gave warn with I/O going on, starting it again brought ok; `out`/`in --wait` on node 1, metrics, audit lines in the journal, the TLS and socket paths, and a rerun that changed nothing all worked. Installing on block devices (`--devices`) is not built: it formats them, so it wants a confirmation flag and a test host. |
| I47 | **The end-to-end test uses the binaries built beside the installer**, so `cargo test -p hyperstor-install` alone can run stale `hyperstor-meta` or `hyperstord` binaries. | Cargo builds every workspace binary only with `--workspace`, which CI and CLAUDE.md use. A mutation check of the device out/in path at first passed for this reason, then failed as it should once the workspace was rebuilt. | Resolved by the end-to-end suite (I54): it reads cargo's dep-info for each binary and refuses one older than any source it was built from. |
| I48 | **Certificates come from `hyperstor-pki`** (rcgen and rustls with the ring provider): a root CA (10 years, signs intermediates only), an intermediate (1 year), leaves (30 days) whose URI SAN is `hyperstor://<cluster>/<role>/<id>`, renewed by the installer after two thirds of their life. `--all-in-one` makes the root, the intermediate, the map service's certificate (`meta/0`, for 127.0.0.1 and localhost) and an administrator's (`admin/local`) in `/etc/hyperstor/pki`; the administrator's key is readable by a new `hyperstor-admin` group. | D53's two levels, so rotating the intermediate changes no trust store; one identity format for every role; the provider is explicit because connectrpc pulls in rustls with its default provider too. | On one host the root key stays on disk (root-only), which D53 wants offline for real clusters: `cluster init` should export it encrypted and delete it. No revocation list yet (D56's denylist in the map). Renewal is a daily systemd timer that reruns the installer (`hyperstor-renew.timer`), which is convergent; D56's renewal by the map service over the existing session is for multi-host clusters. |
| I49 | **Storage node metrics** (`hyperstord --admin ADDR`, D58, D59): each reactor counts requests and their bytes by kind (client write and read, replication, backfill push) as they arrive, and client writes answered with an error (refused at once or failed later), with relaxed atomic adds, times client writes (arrival to answer, after every replica) and reads into fixed histograms (16 µs to 65 ms, powers of two), and copies its engine's counters (objects, free space, log use, commits, checkpoints) and its map epoch into gauges once a second; a plain thread serves them as Prometheus text with `/health`. | No system call or lock on the I/O path; `stats()` is constant-time (I37). Measured against main: counters cost nothing measurable, histograms at most ~1% at QD32 reads (02-benchmarks.md). The end-to-end test checks every write that arrived was answered once, timed or counted as an error; it first expected exactly the I/O test's bytes, which failed on CI where a write refused for want of a lease was retried and counted twice. | Per-pool and per-volume series (15-operations.md §16) with §23.3's top-k cap aren't built; the admin port is plain HTTP and unauthenticated. |
| I50 | **Multi-host install from a shared cluster file, before join tokens:** `hyperstor-install install --cluster FILE --role meta|storage|both [--node N] --map ADDR`. The meta role installs the map service listening on ADDR, the CAs and the API; a storage role installs node N with one reactor per address the file lists for it. The all-in-one became one case of the same plan. | The installer can set up real multi-host clusters with what exists today (a static cluster file, I28); D57's join tokens need the map service to issue certificates and change the map at run time, which needs the map persisted (Raft, I18). The end-to-end test installs a map service and three storage nodes into four roots and manages them. | Replaced by `--join URL --token T` (§23.4.2) once the map can grow at run time; until then adding a host means editing every copy of the file and restarting the map service. Storage hosts get no certificate: the D5 ports have no TLS yet (D25). |
| I51 | **The API audits and rate-limits** (§23.9.4, §23.3): every change and every refused call is a JSON line on the map service's standard error (time, principal, operation, target, result), which systemd keeps in the journal; reads that succeed aren't recorded. Each principal (certificate identity, or peer address without TLS) gets a token bucket of 200 calls per second, bursts of 1000; over it, `resource_exhausted`. | Who changed what is the first question after an incident; journald is already there and needs no new storage. The limits are §23.3's defaults. The end-to-end test finds the administrator's change and the node's and host's refusals in the log; a unit test checks the bucket. | §23.9.4 also wants an append-only audit object in `.meta`, which doesn't exist yet; journald alone can be rotated away. The limits aren't configurable. |
| I52 | **Break-glass local socket** (§23.6, D55): `hyperstor-meta --api-socket PATH` serves the same API on a Unix socket (mode 0666, checked per request); a small hyper loop tags each request with the caller's `SO_PEERCRED`, and root, the service's own user and members of `hyperstor-admin` (from `/proc/<pid>/status`) get every right, audited as `local:uid=N`. `hyperstorctl` uses it by default on the map service's host when the installed configuration names it and no `--api`/`--tls` is given. | It must work when certificates, the network or a future IdP don't, and the kernel's word on who is calling needs nothing to be valid. Allowing the service's own user lets the end-to-end test reach it without root. | The client speaks h2c over the socket (connectrpc's Unix transport); the server answers HTTP/1 too, so `curl --unix-socket` works. A caller's groups are read from `/proc` at each request, which a caller could race by changing groups between connecting and calling; it only matters for callers that drop privileges, and root is allowed anyway. |
| I53 | **A reused gid can grant `hyperstor-admin`.** On dev-2, `glidex-users` (gid 985) had been created and later deleted; `groupadd --system hyperstor-admin` took 985 again, and a login session started before the deletion still held 985, so it passed the local socket's group check. | The kernel's credentials are what the socket trusts (I52), and they are right: the session does hold gid 985. | Group ids are reused by design. Options: have the installer refuse a gid that running processes hold without a name, or create `hyperstor-admin` with an explicit unused gid; or accept it and document that sessions should log in again after groups are deleted. Unprivileged users (checked as `nobody`) are refused and audited. |
| I54 | **The end-to-end suite is its own crate, `hyperstor-test`**: a harness (`Cluster::all_in_one`, scratch roots, port ranges per process and call, `hyperstorctl` over TLS, socket or plain HTTP, issued identities, metrics and audit readers) and tests by area: `install` (checks, convergence, bad flags, multi-host), `manage` (status and tables, I/O on every pool, out and in, a killed node), `security` (roles, strangers, plain HTTP, break-glass socket, audit), `metrics`, and `installed`, ignored by default, which checks a host where Hyperstor is really installed. `scripts/e2e.sh [--installed]` runs it. | One place for every operator-level regression, run by `cargo test --workspace` on CI, and reusable against real hosts. Binaries are checked against cargo's dep-info so a stale build fails loudly (I47). A Unix socket's path is limited to 107 bytes, which a checkout path can exceed, so sockets live in a short directory under the system temp directory. Five runs (two on 2 CPUs) passed; making `authorize` allow all fails `security`. Test ports are taken below the kernel's ephemeral range (`ip_local_port_range`): on CI, a port picked in that range was taken by an outgoing connection before the dashboard, started last, could bind it; services' output now goes to log files, and a service that exits before listening fails the test with its log. | The installed-host tests need root or `hyperstor-admin`; a CI runner that installs Hyperstor would run `scripts/e2e.sh --installed`. Each fixture starts its own cluster (about 1.5 s); tests in one file run in parallel. |
| I55 | **An interim read-only dashboard, `hyperstor-mgmt`** (§23.7 before AD2): one page and script served from the binary, reading the cluster API as its own `mgmt` identity and each node's metrics port, refreshing every 2 s; health, I/O, space, pools with PG states and PG lists, nodes with heartbeat, I/O, p99 latency, space, log use and map epoch. Without people's login it refuses non-loopback addresses, answers only loopback `Host` names on its port (DNS rebinding), only GET, with a CSP that allows no inline script and data only via `textContent`. The installer runs it on the map service's host (8852) with its own certificate and restarts it on renewal. | The owner asked for something to look at before AD2; it becomes AD2's shell once `glidex-auth` brings login (D52). The end-to-end test checks the page, its data, a killed node, the protections and the loopback rule. | Space isn't in health (D27 not built): the dashboard and Grafana warn at 85 %/95 % from metrics; on dev-2 the 1 GiB trial devices reached 94 % from repeated `io-test` runs, since nothing deletes test volumes. The installer used to restart every service when any unit changed; now each changed binary, unit, option file or certificate restarts only its own services (a `--root` run reports what it would restart). |
| I56 | **Grafana and Prometheus files in `deploy/`**: a scrape job reading the installer's `/etc/hyperstor/prometheus-targets.json`, and a dashboard (16 panels, a data source and a node variable) for import or provisioning. | §23.7's longer history. The suite checks every metric the dashboard queries is served by the nodes. On dev-2, Prometheus 2.53.0 (podman) scraped the installed cluster under load and all 19 queries returned data; Grafana 11.1.0 loaded the provisioned dashboard and ran a query through its data source. | No alert rules yet (nearfull, write errors, a node behind on map epoch would be the first). |

## Delta recovery and scrub (HS2)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I57 | **Delta recovery compares content digests instead of replaying a PG log** (09-replication.md §8.3, §8.5). `Shard::digest` hashes (FNV-1a, 64 bits) an object's size in blocks, each mapped block's number and CRC, its attributes and its key–value table, all from the index; a block of zeros counts as a hole. A `Digests` request returns a member's `(key, digest)` list of a PG, in the frame's payload. Backfill asks the target for it instead of resetting it, then pushes objects whose digests differ and deletes for keys the primary lacks, each read and sent after the queue drains, on the connection live writes use. A target that doesn't know `Digests` (a rolling upgrade, 15-operations.md §18.4) answers "unsupported request" and gets a reset and a whole refill, as before. | No new persisted state, so nothing new to crash-test, trim or keep in step with the data; it also covers a member that was away longer than any log would reach, and copies that diverged for any reason. Measured: a node that returns after 1% of a 256 MiB volume changed is sent 2 MiB instead of 192 MiB, and is clean in 0.14–0.18 s instead of 0.46–0.48 s (02-benchmarks.md, rejoin). The cluster test fails if every object is pushed; another restarts a node as an old one and checks it is refilled whole; a randomized test (writes, node stops and restarts against a model; 23 seeds passed) fails on all three default seeds if changed objects aren't pushed. | Cost grows with the objects in a PG, not with what was missed: both sides walk the PG's index entries (in memory today; pages of the B+tree after D43). A PG log would make a short absence cost only its writes, and remains the way to go if PGs grow to millions of objects. 64 bits of FNV over CRC32Cs can collide in principle; a collision leaves one stale object until a write or a deep scrub finds it. |
| I58 | **Light scrub repairs by majority** (09-replication.md §8.5). Each primary compares every acting member's digests of its clean PGs once per `--scrub-hours` (default 24, 0: off), one PG at a time spread evenly over the interval, looks again at keys that differ after 200 ms (a write in flight isn't damage), and for a difference that persists pushes its copy (or a delete) to the members that differ when a majority of copies agree with it; otherwise it counts the object as unresolved and logs it. Counted in `hyperstor_scrub_objects_total`. | Digests can't say which copy is right; a majority can, and two copies can't decide. Pushing from the primary reuses backfill's ordered push, so a repair can't overtake a live write. The cluster test overwrites a replica behind the primary's back and fails if the repair isn't sent. | When the primary is the copy outvoted, nothing is repaired: the primary would need to pull, or hand primacy to a member that agrees. Light scrub is paced only by spreading PGs over the interval; it reads no data, so it has no byte rate. |
| I59 | **(Superseded by I69.)** **A pushed copy's size is rounded up to whole blocks**: `Push` carries the data from offset 0 to the size rounded up, and no size. The digest therefore compares sizes in blocks. | Client writes are whole blocks today, so the sizes agree; comparing byte sizes would make every pushed copy of an object with a partial last block differ forever. | Byte-exact sizes (S3 objects, files) need `Push` to carry the size and the target to truncate; to do with the S3 gateway (HS12). |
| I60 | **Deep scrub is every seventh scrub pass, and the first** (09-replication.md §8.5: light daily, deep weekly). `Digests` takes a `deep` flag: each member, in a task of its own so replication on that connection goes on, reads every object of the PG (the engine checks each block's CRC) at most 64 MiB/s per device, pausing between objects, and marks objects that fail. A marked object's digest is `DAMAGED` (0, never a healthy digest) until a push replaces it; light scrub's majority rule then repairs it, and a damaged copy never counts toward a majority (each gets a value of its own). | No new flag or schedule to configure, and the repair path is light scrub's. The cluster test flips a byte of a replica's data under the engine, where the index (and the digest) still look right, and fails if deep reads don't mark it. | A damaged primary is reported, not repaired (as in I58), and a client read of it fails meanwhile instead of falling back to a replica. Its cost on I/O (02-benchmarks.md, deep scrub against I/O), with a deep pass every 3.5 s: 4–6% of throughput and up to +45% p99 for QD32 reads. Reading in 256 KiB pieces to bound each synchronous read made it worse (−12% throughput), so the cost is the extra device and CPU work, not one long stall; deep scrub should run in the `bulk` class (D46) once it exists, at a rate measured on dedicated drives. **Found while testing:** damage in the data of a device's *last* commit group isn't silent: on reopen, recovery checks that group's data and drops the record as torn (I4), so the object is missing, and light scrub restores it. |
| I61 | **Peering by digests** (09-replication.md §8.4). Before a primary serves a PG at a new epoch, the first request to it compares every acting member's digests with its own and pushes its copy (or a delete) where they differ; requests arriving meanwhile wait (up to 2 s, then `Unavailable`, and the client retries). A member that doesn't know `Digests` (rolling upgrade) is taken to agree, as before; an object whose own copy is damaged is left to scrub. Counted in `hyperstor_scrub_objects_total{kind="peering"}`. | Every acknowledged write is on every acting member, so the new primary's copy has them all; what differs can only be a write that never completed, and making every copy the primary's means a read can't see it and later not see it. The cluster test diverges the third copy and kills the primary, and fails if peering pushes nothing. | Not the log-based peering of §8.4: the new primary's copy wins even where another member holds a later, unacknowledged write, which §8.4 would keep ("the longest log"). Both are allowed; this one never keeps a write the client wasn't told about. A read served by the old primary *before* its write was replicated could still be undone (reads don't wait for replication), which only replica-granted read leases or reading after commit on all members would close. Each failover pays one digest round trip per PG on its first request (02-benchmarks.md, failover). |

## The replicated map service (HS1, D20, D71–D74)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I62 | **The published map is rows of one table**: `head` (the map without its PGs) and `pg/{pool}/{pg}` for each PG. The leader saves a map by writing, in one write set, the head and the PG rows whose contents differ from what the store holds, compared inside the transaction, and deleting the rows of PGs it no longer has. The schema raises the entry limit to 64 MiB (glidex `Schema::MAX_WRITE_SET_BYTES`). | One row for the whole map exceeded the store's 4 MiB entry at the scale envelope (65,536 PGs is about 4.2 MB of JSON; 06-metadata.md §4.3), and replicated megabytes for every node failure. Per-PG rows make an entry about the size of what changed, and keep each change atomic; only the first map, or a new pool, is written whole (8.3 MB for 65,536 PGs, measured by the store test). | Write sets are JSON with base64 values (glidex's format): about twice the binary size. A binary write-set format would be a glidex change. |
| I63 | **Members talk Raft over TCP with mutual TLS** (`replica.rs`): a request is `kind, length, body`, one connection per member, one request at a time on it, reconnected once if the peer closed it. Each side checks the other's certificate: the chain to the cluster's root, the server's IP address, and an identity of role `meta` in this cluster. The certificates are the members' `api` ones (`--api-tls`); the installer issues them for the member's addresses. | The transport the crate needs is two calls (append and vote) and a snapshot; TLS reuses certificates every member already has. | Snapshots travel whole in one frame (a map is megabytes); a streamed transfer is needed if maps grow far beyond the envelope. One request at a time per member serializes append and vote. |
| I64 | **Raft timing: 50 ms heartbeats, elections after 200–400 ms**, a log of 1,000 entries kept past the snapshot. | No lease is renewed while there is no leader and leases are short (D48), so the election must fit within about half a lease. Measured with 400 ms leases: the longest gap between writes across a leader's death was 0.37–0.46 s (02-benchmarks.md). | False elections under load on real networks: part of G16. |
| I65 | **The cluster file seeds the store and grows it**: a new group's leader commits the file's map first; afterwards a leader, once elected, adds the nodes, devices and pools the file has and the map lacks, and nothing else. | Keeps I50's way of adding a host (edit every copy of the file, restart the members) until join tokens (D57); removals go through `out`/`in`, so a stale file can't undo them. | The members themselves are fixed by `--member`: replacing a member's host or growing from 3 to 5 needs a Raft membership change (`ClusterNode::set_voters`), not built yet. |
| I66 | **Raft has a runtime of its own** in `hyperstor-meta` (two tokio workers on a `hyperstor-raft` thread), beside the cluster API's single-threaded one. Maps flow to the reactor over a cross-thread channel; commits flow back with a one-shot reply. | The store's write path waits with `block_in_place`, which a single-threaded runtime refuses; and keeping Raft apart from the API means API load can't delay heartbeats (G17). | — |
| I67 | **Bugs found while building it, fixed in `glidex-raftstore`** (glidex PR): a stopped member kept its store open for ever (the store held the member through its replicator, which held the store); and the write-set limit was fixed at 4 MiB for every application. `ClusterNode::shutdown` now puts the store in a stopped mode that refuses writes and drops the replicator, and each schema can set its limit. | Found by the group restart test (a restarted member couldn't open its store) and by sizing a large map. | — |

## Object operations (HS1, `.meta` groundwork)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I68 | **Atomic operation lists on named objects** (`Exec`, `ReplExec`, `Lookup`): a client sends a list (the engine's operations, plus `AssertExists`, `AssertAbsent` and `AssertAttr`) to the PG primary of the object's name (FNV-1a of it). The primary holds the key, waits until every queued commit is done, checks the assertions against the object as it is, then queues the effects and sends them to every replica with no wait in between, as writes go. Replicas apply effects only. `Lookup` reads size, attributes and a range of the key–value table from the primary. | Headers, lineages and directories (06-metadata.md §4.2) need compare-and-set. Engine revisions differ between copies (I6), so assertions are on attributes the caller keeps (a header's version), and the primary alone decides them. The cluster test races 16 clients over 20 rounds, and fails every time without the lock. | An error after the primary accepted the list (a replica failed) leaves it unknown whether the list applied; the header layer must read back and compare. The lock waits for every queued commit, not only the key's: fine for metadata, not for data. |
| I69 | **Refill, repair and peering push whole objects** (`PushObject`): the exact size, the data with its block CRCs, the attributes and the key–value table, the table in several lists if large. A node from before it (a rolling upgrade) still gets data-only pushes. | A data-only push lost the attributes and table of a `.meta` object (the cluster test shows the returning copy empty), and rounded sizes up to whole blocks (I59). | — |
| I70 | **Volumes are named by a 128-bit id, not by name**: the caller chooses it (glidex: the disk's UUID) or the client draws it at random; the name is shown and not unique. The header is `h/{id}` in `.meta`; each data pool has 64 directory objects `d/{pool}/{id mod 64}`, a key–value entry per volume. | A unique name needs one object every create of the pool goes through, or a name index kept in step with the headers; an id spreads headers over the `.meta` PGs and keeps a create to two operation lists. glidex already has a UUID per disk. | Whether people want unique names (an index object per pool, checked at create). |
| I71 | **A create carries a token** (`by`: the client's instance and a random number), asserted absent with the header. A create that gets a conflict reads the header back: its own token means its earlier attempt applied and the answer was lost. | I68 leaves the outcome of a list unknown when a replica fails after the primary accepted it; a plain retry would report the volume as existing. | — |
| I72 | **Order of changes**: create writes the header, then the directory entry; delete marks the header `Deleting` (compare-and-set on its version), deletes every chunk, then the directory entry, then the header. Listing skips an entry whose header is gone. Expand is a compare-and-set of the size; shrinking is refused. | Each step that stops half-way leaves a state the next attempt finishes: a header with no entry works and is listed after another create; a `Deleting` header can't be attached and its delete is resumed. | A sweep for headers left without entries, with scrub (HS2). |
| I73 | **`.meta` is pool 0, added by the map service** (`size` min(3, nodes), `min` 2 from 3 copies, else 1, 256 PGs); a cluster file that declares pool 0 is refused. The cluster map moved into its own crate, `hyperstor-map`, which `hyperstor-meta` re-exports. | Every cluster needs it, and a fixed number lets clients find headers without a lookup. The split lets the map service use the client library (for the volume API) without a dependency cycle. | — |

## Volume leases and the blocklist (HS1, HS2)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I74 | **A lease is the header's `lease` attribute plus an expiry in the primary's memory.** Attaching writes the holder (instance, mode) to the header through the primary, as an operation list does; a renewal writes nothing and sets the expiry to 4 s from when it arrives (D75). A holder renews all its leases on one primary in one `LeaseRenew`. A primary that has no expiry for a holder, or has one from another PG epoch (it was primary before, and the PG moved away and back), gives it a full lease from when it first sees it. Detaching removes the attribute. | Renewals then cost no write and no replication; a new primary can't know when the holder last renewed elsewhere, so it waits a whole lease. The cluster test checks that a holder keeps its lease across its header's primary failing, and fails when a new primary gives no grace. | Expiries of headers this device no longer serves stay in memory until the next grant; bounded by the volumes it was primary of. |
| I75 | **A storage node takes a map lease only with a map at least as new as the one published when the lease was granted**; a `Blocklist` is answered once every device of an up node has acknowledged the push of that map, or 1.25 leases after it was published. | 06-metadata.md §9 needs "every node holds epoch N or has lost its map lease". A lease granted before epoch N runs out within 1.25 leases; one granted after can't be used without epoch N, so no node keeps serving the blocklisted instance. | — |
| I76 | **Storage nodes refuse a blocklisted instance on every request**, by the instance its connection named at `Hello`, and check again after the last wait before queueing a write or an operation list. Peers send no `Hello` (instance 0) and are never refused. The blocklist is in the map: format 2 of its encoding, kept at format 1 while the list is empty so nodes from before it read the map. Entries last an hour (`BLOCKLIST_FOR`); the leader prunes them. | The second check means a write queued after the node installed epoch N is refused, and one queued before is ordered before every write of the new holder. Mutation-checked: without the checks, the old holder's writes keep landing after the takeover. | Settled: anyone who can reach the map service's port can blocklist an instance, as anyone who can reach a storage node can write, until the security work of AD (D78). |
| I77 | **Two ways to take a lease from another instance**: `take_over` waits until the lease has expired, then blocklists the holder (06-metadata.md §9's order), and leaves a holder that keeps renewing alone; `preempt` blocklists first, so the holder's renewals are refused, then waits out its lease. The client marks a lease lost only when a primary says so or a node refuses it as blocklisted; it doesn't expire leases on its own (D76). | `take_over` is for a holder that died or is cut off (a VM re-placed after its host failed); it can't break a healthy holder by mistake. `preempt` is the operator's (and NVMe's preempt, 11-frontends.md §11.2). | Settled: glidex's driver preempts when it re-places a VM from a fenced node (D79). |
| I78 | **An attached volume can't be deleted**: the client refuses, and the delete's first change asserts that the header has no `lease`, decided by the header's primary as leases are. `ro` leases are refused until snapshots exist (D18, HS4). | A deleted volume's data would vanish under a running VM. A holder that died keeps its lease until taken over, so an operator deletes it with `force` (D80): `force_delete_volume` preempts the holder and deletes the volume holding its lease, the delete's first change asserting that lease. A delete gives up after 10 conflicting changes, as an expand does. | — |

## Joining hosts, certificates and operations (AD0, AD1)

Numbered from I80, leaving a gap after the volume-lease work
(I74–I78) was in progress beside it.

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I80 | **(Superseded by D81: tokens are replicated.)** **Join tokens were held in memory by the member that made them**: only the secret's SHA-256, the role, the expiry and used/revoked flags; the last day's expired ones are forgotten. A restart of that member revokes them all. A token is `hsj1-<root SHA-256>-<32 random bytes>`, hex; its id is the first 12 hex digits of the secret's hash. | Tokens live an hour and are used once, so losing them on a restart costs an operator one `token create`; keeping them in the replicated store would put secrets' hashes in the Raft log and every snapshot, and the store's write path is busy with the volume work. Failing closed is the safe direction. | Whether tokens should survive restarts and be usable on any member (commit them through Raft) once AD2's tokens for people need the same store anyway. |
| I81 | **(Settled as D82.)** **A token is burnt by its first join, successful or not**, except when the member asked doesn't lead (it did nothing; the token stays usable). Storage tokens can only be made on the leader: a follower refuses `CreateToken --role node` with `not_leader` and the leader's map address. | Retrying a failed join with the same token would let whoever saw a failure retry with it; a not-leader refusal reveals nothing and changes nothing. Tokens are per member (I80), so a storage token made on a follower could never add a node. | Forwarding joins to the leader instead, which needs a member-to-member call path. |
| I82 | **(Settled as D83.)** **The cluster API accepts TLS clients without a certificate**; every call but `Join` then refuses them with `unauthenticated`. Before, the handshake itself required one. | A joining host has no certificate yet; a second, unauthenticated port would be another listener on the management network (D59) to secure. The refusal moved from the handshake to the first call: the API still answers nothing without a certificate. | — |
| I83 | **A joining host pins the root by the fingerprint in the token, before it sends the token**: its TLS client accepts the server only if the server's chain contains a certificate with that SHA-256 and the server's certificate verifies under it (with the address checked). Servers therefore send the root at the end of their chain (`api.crt` ends with it). | Sending the token to an impostor would let it join a rogue node with it. Without the root, the host can't verify the chain the usual way; matching the fingerprint, then verifying with the pinned certificate as the only anchor, is what D57 asks. An impostor copying the root into its chain fails the verification (tested). | — |
| I84 | **A joining storage host gets the next node number and is added down**, with one device per address, `class nvme`, weight 1; it comes up on its first heartbeat, so nothing is placed on it before it runs. A host joining again with the same addresses gets its node back; addresses another node holds are refused (`node_conflict`). `.meta` grows to min(3, nodes) copies as hosts join. | Numbers are the map's to give, so two hosts can't pick the same one; down until heard keeps PGs off a host that isn't running. A reinstall shouldn't add a second node with the first one's addresses. `.meta` is sized when the map is made (I73), which for a cluster that starts empty is one copy. | Whether the operator should choose node numbers or racks from the token instead of the host. |
| I85 | **A PG that never had a copy starts where placement wants it** (`acting = up`), like a new PG. Before, a pool made before any host joined kept its PGs empty for ever: an empty acting set looked like "every copy is down", which waits for a member to return. | Found by the join test: a cluster that starts with no storage node never served. An empty acting set holds no data, so there is nothing to protect. | — |
| I86 | **Map service members after the first join with `meta` tokens** and get the root's certificate, the intermediate CA (certificate and key) and the cluster file over the pinned TLS session; the root's key stays on the first host (D70). Before, each member's install made its own root, so members' Raft TLS couldn't work across hosts. Members are still listed at install (`--members`); changing them at run time is I65's. | Every member signs joins and renewals (§23.8), so each needs the intermediate; sending it over a session pinned to the root and authorized by an admin's single-use token is how glidex servers get their CA. | Intermediate rotation: the first host renews it at 9 months, but the other members don't fetch the new one yet. |
| I87 | **The intermediate's key reaches `hyperstor-meta` as a systemd credential** (`LoadCredential=intermediate.key:…`), read from `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY`, else from `--ca DIR` (tests, hosts without systemd). The file stays readable by root only. | The service runs as `hyperstor`; making the key group-readable would expose it to every process of that user. `LoadCredential` hands it to this service alone; an encrypted, TPM-sealed credential (G18) is the next step. | — |
| I88 | **Renewal signs the caller's own identity and addresses**, taken from the certificate it connected with, for whatever key the request carries. A joined storage host's daily installer run renews at two thirds of the life; a map service member renews its own leaves locally with the intermediate. | The caller can't ask for more than it has; a new key each time limits what a stolen old key is worth. | Without a denylist (D56), renewal lets a stolen certificate be renewed for ever: the revocation list is the next piece. |
| I89 | **The cluster API listens on the map service's own address** for installs from a cluster file or a join (it was always `127.0.0.1`), port `--api-port`; the all-in-one keeps loopback. `hyperstorctl.conf` and the dashboard follow it. | Joining hosts must reach it, and D59 puts it on the management network, which is the map service's address in these installs. | — |
| I90 | **Drains are operations held in memory**, the last 100: `DrainNode` takes the node's devices out like `out`, and a task on the API thread checks the published map every 500 ms until every moved PG is settled (`acting` = `up`) and none has a device of the node. A drain whose devices come back in fails; a cancelled one brings them back in. `--dry-run` recomputes a copy of the working map. | Operations need no persistence while drains are their only kind: the map holds the state, and a drain's progress can be read again from it. | Persisting operations once long ones that change data (rebalances, pool deletes) exist. |
| I91 | **Every API error carries a stable reason** (`hyperstor.v1.ErrorInfo`, wire-compatible with `google.rpc.ErrorInfo`, domain `hyperstor`); `hyperstorctl` prints it as `(reason)` and exits 4 on `permission_denied` or `unauthenticated`, 3 on `unavailable` (which includes `not_leader`). A member that doesn't lead now answers `unavailable`/`not_leader` instead of `not_found`. | Scripts need something steadier than messages (glidex grpc.md G-D5); a distinct exit code for "not allowed" lets them tell a missing permission from a missing device. | — |
| I92 | **`hyperstorctl` is checked against the protos, not generated from them**: a test reads every `rpc` in the proto files and fails when one has no row in `COMMANDS` (the command that calls it, or the installer for `Join` and `RenewCertificate`). | D55's goal is that the CLI never falls behind the API; a coverage test gives that with hand-written commands, whose two-word names and table output a generator would make worse. | Generating the commands once the API has many more calls. |
| I93 | **(Resolved by I109.)** **`buf` wasn't run yet.** The protos are compiled by `protox` in `build.rs` (no `protoc` or `buf` needed), which also checks the JSON names; `buf lint` and `buf breaking` (§23.5) need the `buf` binary in CI. | Not available on the development host; downloading a binary into CI is a change for its own pull request. | Adding `bufbuild/buf-action` to CI. |
| I94 | **The bootstrap token file is the lowest-numbered member's** (`DATA_DIR/meta/bootstrap-tokens`, mode 0600, rewritten whole and synced on each change): before the store holds a map, it alone makes tokens, and only `meta` ones; others refuse with `not_formed`. A map service of one is its own first member, and has a map within a second of starting: `hyperstorctl` retries on `not_formed` in the tests, as an operator would. | The lowest number is the member that forms the group (I62), so it's the one an operator installs first; storage tokens need the map anyway. | — |
| I95 | **Token writes run on the API thread's blocking pool** and wait for Raft's commit, as a map commit does on the replica's runtime; a refusal (used, revoked, expired) writes nothing, because the check and the change are one write closure that returns early. | The API's runtime has one thread; a write that waits for a majority must not stop the other calls. | — |
| I96 | **`hyperstor-meta` logs who leads** (`member N leads (term T)`, `no leader (term T)`) to the journal. | A CI run timed out waiting for a leader with nothing in the members' logs to say why; operators need the same line. | — |
| I97 | **The end-to-end tests hard-link the built binaries into each scratch root** (`hyperstor_test::link_binaries`) before installing, and the join tests take turns. | Each root got about 460 MB of binary copies (release builds keep debug info); two tests installing eight roots at once on CI starved the map service's fsyncs: a member stayed at term 1 for 40 s. The likely cause; not reproduced on the development host, and three CI runs passed after. | Whether release builds should ship split debug info, which would also shrink installs. |
| I98 | **A root backup is a text file**: a header in the clear (`hyperstor root backup v1`, the cluster, the root's fingerprint, the KDF and its salt, the nonce), authenticated with the ciphertext, then the data in hex. The passphrase needs 12 characters and comes from a file or `$HYPERSTOR_BACKUP_PASSPHRASE`; the file is created 0600, never overwritten, and read back and opened before the key is removed. | Text can be printed or kept in a password manager; the clear header tells backups apart without the passphrase, and authenticating it stops a backup being relabelled for another cluster. Opening before removing means the key never leaves on a backup that doesn't open. | Point 46: Shamir splitting for sites with several administrators. |
| I99 | **Test ports are reserved across processes**: `free_ports` holds a lock file (`flock`) per port in the system's temporary directory for the rest of the test process, and skips ports another process holds. | A port free when checked was taken by another checkout's tests before ours bound it, or while a map service restarted on it ("Address already in use"); several sessions run the suite on the development host at once. Only processes with this harness honour the locks. | — |
| I100 | **A reactor's listeners wait up to 5 s for their address** (`hyperstor_reactor::net::bind_retrying`, for the map service's port and each storage node reactor's). A killed or stopped process's io_uring is torn down by the kernel in the background, and a pending accept keeps its listening socket open meanwhile: for about 100 ms after the process was reaped, binding the address fails, with nothing in `ss`. | Found by the join test, which restarts the map service at once ("Address already in use", reproducible on two CPUs). `systemctl restart` does the same; `Restart=on-failure` would have recovered a second later, after a failed start. | — |

## The rest of HS2: failures

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I101 | **Down and out as built** (07-placement.md §5.3): placement keeps the devices of a down node in `up`, so its PGs run degraded and nothing is copied; after `--down-out-secs` (600 by default) the leader marks them out (`auto_out` in the map, encoding format 3) and they are placed elsewhere; when the node returns they are marked in. Operator `in`/`out` overrides both. Cache pools follow live nodes at once (nothing is copied to them). Health counts a PG waiting for a down member as degraded, not backfilling. The leader keeps when each node went down in memory: a new leader starts the interval again. | A short reboot moves no data. Before this, a down node's PGs were placed elsewhere at once. The cluster test checks both; it fails if placement drops down nodes again. | A node that flaps (up for a moment, down again) restarts the interval each time. |
| I102 | **A failed device** (09-replication.md §15): an I/O error in a commit poisons the engine's shard, and so does one in a read; the reactor then answers every request `Unavailable` (clients retry) and its heartbeat loop reports `DeviceFailed`. The leader marks the device failed and out (`failed` in the map) and places its PGs elsewhere at once; its node stays up. An operator's `in` clears it. | Waiting for the down-out interval would leave the PGs a copy short for 10 minutes for a device that won't come back. | One bad sector fails the whole device; no SMART or error-rate threshold yet. |
| I103 | **Slow and unreachable replicas** (D91 as built): a primary that hasn't had an answer to a replicated change within 2 s of sending it (`REPL_TIMEOUT`), or can't connect to a member at all, answers the client `Unavailable` and reports `MemberSlow`, once per PG epoch. The leader takes the member out of that PG's acting set if the reporter is the PG's primary at that epoch, at least `min_size` copies remain, and no peer has reported the primary silent; the member stays in `up` and is backfilled back. | Unreachable counts too: a partition between a primary and one replica is invisible to liveness and peer reports (one reporter), and stalled the PG's writes for as long as it lasted. Ignoring a doubted primary keeps a primary that is the one cut off from dropping healthy members before it is marked down. Fencing the dropped member may stop its node's map lease for one lease if its disk hangs. The timeout is a coarse deadline, woken by one ticker per reactor every 50 ms: a reactor timer per write (an io_uring timeout) cost about 3% of write throughput (02-benchmarks.md). | A slow *primary* is not handled: its clients wait for it, until its map lease lapses. |
| I104 | **A primary repairs its own copy from a member** (`Fetch`, 09-replication.md §8.2, §8.5): a read whose block fails its checksum, or a scrub whose majority outvotes the primary (`Pull`), fetches a member's copy and replaces the primary's. The repair holds the key: operation lists wait, and a write to it is answered `Unavailable` (rare; retried); it first waits until every write accepted for the PG has been answered, so the member has them all. Replicated pools only: an EC member holds another shard. | §8.2 and §8.5 asked for both; I58 and I60 had left a damaged or outvoted primary reported, not repaired. The cluster tests damage a primary's block under its engine, and overwrite its copy, and fail without the fetch. | — |
| I105 | **Scrub results reach cluster health**: a primary reports `ScrubReport` (objects left unresolved in a PG) when the count changes, and the leader keeps them; `GetStatus` warns with the inconsistent PGs. | Unresolved differences were only logged on the node. | Kept in the leader's memory: a new leader learns them again at the next scrub of each PG (a day by default). |
| I106 | **The fault rig** (D92 as built): `hyperstor_daemon::faults` has `Partitions` (node pairs cut both ways, consulted before every request to a peer) and `FaultyDev` (a device whose every call is delayed, blocking its reactor as a hung disk does, or fails with `EIO`); the cluster harness wires them to every node. `random_faults_lose_no_acknowledged_write` runs writes while nodes stop and restart, node pairs are cut, and devices hang, against a model in which a failed write may or may not have landed, then heals everything and checks every chunk and every copy. 20 seeds pass; it fails when backfill and peering push nothing. | Runs in CI on every pull request and replays a failing seed exactly (`HS_SEED`). | Not yet modelled: partitions between a client and a node, between map-service members, and devices that return wrong data (deep scrub's test covers that). |

## Revocation and protos (AD0)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I107 | **Leaf certificates get random 16-byte serials** (top bit clear). rcgen's default is a hash of the public key. | Revocation names certificates by serial; two certificates for one key (a renewal that kept its key) would share one. | — |
| I108 | **The API records the certificates it signs** (joins, renewals) in an `issued` table, by the leader and best effort: a renewal through a follower is signed and not recorded. Certificates the installer makes on the map service's host (`api`, `admin`, `mgmt`) aren't recorded; `hyperstorctl cert show --file F` gives their serial. A store that can't be read counts as "not revoked". | The list is for finding serials and keeping revocations only as long as needed; refusing renewals on followers would let certificates expire on hosts whose `--join` address isn't the leader. Failing open on a read error keeps an unreadable store from locking operators out of the API (the local socket is the other way in). | Settled: fail open (D93). |
| I109 | **`buf lint` (STANDARD) and `buf breaking` (FILE, against the base branch) run in CI**, `buf` 1.75.0 (1.47.2 until 2026-11-08), the release's Linux binary downloaded and checked against its SHA-256 (it ran through `npx` at first); `buf generate` runs the web UI's `protoc-gen-es` with `bun`, and CI fails when the committed TypeScript differs from its output; `buf.yaml` is in `crates/hyperstor-api/proto`. GetOperation and CancelOperation keep returning the `Operation` itself (AIP-151), excepted from two naming rules in `admin.proto` only. Resolves I93. | No third-party action to trust; the pinned version makes runs repeat. The breaking check fails on a renumbered or removed field (checked). | — |
| I110 | **A map backup is the replicated tables' rows** (`HSBACK01`, the cluster id, the map's epoch, the time, then each table's rows, then a CRC32C), from `Db::dump`, Raft's own bookkeeping left out. The leader checks every second whether the store's revision moved, and writes when it has and `--backup-every` has passed since its last; files are written beside, synced and renamed, mode 0600. Installs set `--backup-dir DATA/meta-backups`. | The rows are what Raft replicates, so a backup restores to exactly what the group held; a fixed format with a checksum makes a damaged file fail loudly. Writing only on change keeps an idle cluster's directory still. | — |
| I111 | **A restore writes the backup with the group's first commit**, in one write set, so it reaches the Raft log; it is allowed only for a group of one with no store yet, and its map is the member's seed, so the first publish changes nothing. Other members come back through member changes (D87). | Rows written straight into a member's store would never reach the others: a fresh group's log starts empty and its followers catch up from the log. A group of one has no others. | — |
| I116 | **A member's Raft record is `RAFT_ADDR/MAP_ADDR`**, so redirects and member lists find members added at run time; groups formed before (the Raft address alone) fall back to the member list each member was started with. Member changes run on the Raft runtime (`MemberOp`); `AddMember` waits up to 120 s for the learner to catch up, then replaces the voters (joint consensus). Storage nodes keep their installed member list (any member redirects them). | The map address has to come from somewhere every member agrees on; the Raft record is replicated with the membership itself. | Pushing the member list to storage nodes with the map. |
| I117 | **A forced start alone revokes the other members by identity**, not by serial: every `meta/<id>` certificate issued before then (its start, less the 5-minute backdating) is refused, kept 31 days. Members that joined before the group had a leader have no recorded certificate (I108), so revoking by serial would miss them, and an old member with a longer log could win the survivor's vote and overwrite its log. Checked by a test that fails without it. | It is D85's "new generation" without a new wire format: Raft between members already checks the revocation list. | — |
| I118 | **An identity revocation covers certificates issued at or before it**, to the second. Certificates carry whole seconds; a member's certificate issued in the same second as the forced start (a fast test) passed the stricter "before", voted for its old group's term, and made the survivor follow it: openraft then panicked applying entries its log lacked. Found by running the recovery test 20 times. | A member that joins afresh gets its new certificate long after a forced start, which is done by hand, offline. | — |
| I119 | **A forced start alone asks the other members for an answer** (a map-protocol ping, one second), not just a TCP connection: a stopped reactor's port accepts for a moment after its process exits (I100), which refused a legitimate forced start. Members also log every member connection they refuse, by address. | The check exists to avoid two groups; a socket the kernel still holds is no member. | — |

## ublk devices (HS1)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I112 | **`hyperstor-ublk` serves one queue** on one reactor thread, which also runs the client that holds the volume's lease. Each tag is a task: fetch, serve through the attached volume, commit with the next fetch. Reads and writes copy between the request's buffer and the client's (the driver copies to and from the page cache already, so zero copy needs `UBLK_F_SUPPORT_ZERO_COPY` or `USER_COPY`, later). Flushes are answered at once: the device declares no volatile cache, since a write is answered once every copy has it. Discard and write-zeroes aren't offered. | Enough for tests and the NVMe-oF stopgap. Several queues need one client instance shared by every queue's reactor, so the lease and the blocklist cover them all; the client library has one instance per client today. | Several queues, zero copy, discard (a delete of whole chunks), and resizing a served device after `expand`. |
| I113 | **Shutting down in order**: stop the device (the driver completes every pending fetch as aborted), let every tag's task end, unmap and close the character device, detach, then delete the device (`DEL_DEV` waits until the character device is closed). An `attach` that is killed leaves the device, which `hyperstor-ublk delete` removes, and the lease, which runs out (another client takes the volume with `--take-over`). | `DEL_DEV` before the close hangs; found while writing it. A shard out of space now answers `NoSpace` (it was `Io`), which the device returns as `ENOSPC`: found by fio on a volume larger than its cluster. | Whether `hyperstor-install` should install it on client hosts. |

## The vhost-user service (HS3)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I158 | **`hyperstor-vhost` as built** (D116, D117): one reactor thread runs the cluster client, the control socket, and every attached disk: a socket `<dir>/<volume id>.sock` (mode 0660, `--group` its group), one frontend connected at a time, each enabled queue a task on the kick eventfd. A frontend that disconnects leaves the volume attached for the next (a hypervisor restarting); `detach` cuts the frontend off, removes the socket and gives the lease up; SIGTERM detaches everything. `attach` of a volume attached here answers with its socket, so glidex can retry. The reactor gained `fd::{readable, read, write}` for eventfds and sockets. | The queues do their I/O through the same client that holds the lease, as `hyperstor-ublk`'s tags do; one thread is enough to prove the protocol and the I/O path. The test (`vhost::a_volume_is_served_over_vhost_user_blk`) plays the hypervisor through `vhost`'s frontend, guest memory in a memfd and a split ring laid out by hand, with and without `EVENT_IDX`; `vm::virtual_machines_boot_with_a_volume_as_their_disk` (with `HS_VM=1`) boots the host's kernel with a busybox initramfs under Cloud Hypervisor, two queues, four writers at once, then under QEMU on the same volume, which reads back what the first wrote.
