Mutation checks: a missing call, untranslated ring addresses and misplaced write data fail the tests; two safeguards don't (waking a stopped ring's task, which otherwise only leaks it, and re-checking the ring after re-enabling notifications, a race neither test hits). | Several reactors with each VM's queues on its NUMA node (D26), and a state file so a restarted service keeps its leases (§11.1, G6); today each start is a new client instance, so a service that crashed must `take_over` (up to 4 s). |
| I159 | **virtio-blk as offered**: `VERSION_1`, `EVENT_IDX`, indirect descriptors, multiqueue (`--queues`, default 1), flush (answered at once: writes are acknowledged once every copy has them), 4 KiB blocks (the guest's logical block size, so requests are whole blocks), up to 128 segments, `GET_ID` (the serial glidex passes), and write-zeroes, done as writes of zero blocks in 1 MiB pieces. Not yet: discard, packed rings, the config-change event on expand, inflight tracking, adaptive polling and zero copy (§11.1). | Discard can't just unmap the head's blocks: a read would fall through to a snapshot's (08-engine.md §7.2); it needs a zeroed marker in the head version. `virtio-queue` has no packed rings. The rest are listed in §11.1 and come with the VM boot tests and measurements. | Discard and write-zeroes without allocating need the engine's zeroed marker; until then `mkfs` discards nothing, and write-zeroes allocates. |
| I160 | **Preempting a volume blocklists the whole service it was attached through**: the blocklist is per client instance (06-metadata.md §9), and one `hyperstor-vhost` is one instance, so every disk it serves is cut off, not only the one taken; it must restart to attach again. The test shows it. | D79's case is a fenced node, where every VM goes anyway. | D119: glidex preempts only a fenced node's disks; a planned move detaches on the old node first. Per-volume identities on shared connections (with D33's delegates) if operators need to take one disk off a live node. Resolved by D150 (2026-10-29): a preemption fences only the volume's lease. |
| I161 | **A ring stopped by `GET_VRING_BASE` doesn't wait for its requests in flight**: their completions are dropped, and the frontend gets the next index it would have. | Enough for a VM stopping or a hypervisor reconnecting, where the guest is gone or restarts its queues. | Live migration and a backend restart under a running guest need inflight tracking (`INFLIGHT_SHMFD`), not offered. |
| I167 | **hyperstor's side of glidex's driver** (D122, D123): `CreateToken` takes role `mgmt` with a `name` (1–32 of a–z, 0–9, -), kept in the token record as a trailing field old records lack; `Join` with it issues `hyperstor://<cluster>/mgmt/<name>` (no addresses); every `Join` answer names the identity it issued (`identity`), which `enroll` prints rather than reading it back from the certificate (CodeQL counts certificate contents as sensitive), and answers with the map service's addresses; `RenewCertificate` already serves any identity. `hyperstorctl enroll --file TOKEN --out DIR` does the join for scripts. `ExpandVolume` is a new cluster API call (`hyperstorctl expand-volume`). `hyperstorctl write-volume` writes a file at an offset, holding the volume's lease, in runs of non-zero blocks (`--sparse` skips zero ones); `read-volume` reads a range without a lease; both reach the map service from the API's status, or directly with `--map`. | glidex's workers then need only the map service's addresses and `hyperstorctl`, and the API identity stays in glidex's store. Test: `enroll::a_management_client_enrolls_and_manages_volumes` (enroll, refusals, single use, API calls as the identity, expand and no shrink, a sparse image round trip, a region edited and written back, refused while attached elsewhere, whole blocks only); dropping the name check or `--sparse` fails it. | Per-volume allocated bytes for glidex's `disk.stored` aren't reported yet. A guest learns of an expansion only after its next attach (no config-change event, I159). |

## Snapshots in the engine (HS4)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I114 | **`Op::Absorb(src)`** in a list on object `dst` takes every block `src` holds that `dst` doesn't, then deletes `src` (D104). It resolves to a list on `src` (`Forget` the moved runs, which unmaps without freeing; then `Delete`, which frees only the blocks `dst` overrides) and `Map`s on `dst` naming the same physical blocks, all in the same log record, so it is atomic and replays absolutely (I33): recovery rebuilds free space from the index, so a block named twice during replay is never handed out. **`Shard::read_layered`** reads each block from the first of several objects that holds it, and **`Shard::versions`** lists a name's versions, for reads at an epoch through a chunk's versions and a clone's parents (08-engine.md §7.2). | No data is copied to delete a snapshot. The crash test applies `Absorb` among its random operations against a model (2,000 seeds pass); it fails if `Forget` frees, if the source's list isn't logged, or if overridden blocks move. | — |
| I115 | **Branches, chains and what a snapshot deletion may do.** A chunk's versions in a branch are named by the chunk with the branch appended (branch 0 keeps the bare name, so volumes from before read as before) and versioned by epoch; a read carries layers, (branch, newest epoch) pairs, and the primary reads each block from the newest version of the first layer that holds it. The header (format 2) has the head epoch, the branch, the chain (the branches it reads through, parent first) and the snapshots, each with the chain it was taken with. The lineage object `l/{lineage}` gives out branch numbers and records every branch other than 0: its volume, its chain, and the epoch it was flattened at. A snapshot is **in use** while a branch record that still reads its chain (not flattened, or with snapshots of its own from before the flatten), the volume's chain, or another snapshot's chain holds it. **Deleting** snapshot `s` merges its versions above the previous cut point into the next newer version only if no cut point lies between (a live snapshot, a chain through the branch, the head); with no newer version and no cut point above, they are deleted; otherwise they stay, read by the later cut point. A delete marks the snapshot deleting before it looks for dependents, and a clone or rollback records its branch before it checks the snapshot again, so a race leaves one of them refused. | Found by the model test: a snapshot's chain can't be derived from the volume's current one once a rollback has moved it, and merging into the next version loses blocks a snapshot between them reads (a targeted test fails if the merge ignores it). The model test runs snapshots, clones, rollbacks, flattens, deletions and writes against a model, checking every head and snapshot (over 90 seeds of 30 or 60 steps pass). | One snapshot deletion per volume at a time. Lineage records of branches no longer read are kept until their volume is deleted. |
| I122 | **Quiesce as built** (D103): a holder keeps a `Watch` per attached volume on its header's primary, answered on a header change, on a pending quiesce, or after 20 s. `Snapshot` carries the header's compare-and-set: with a lease holder, the primary asks it to quiesce through its watch; the holder stops new writes, waits for its writes in flight, and sends `Quiesced`; then the primary applies the change and answers the holder, which reads the header (the new epoch) and resumes. A holder that doesn't drain within 2 s gets the snapshot abandoned (`Busy`, a new code clients don't retry). | Snapshots are crash-consistent and never change once taken: a test writes without pause while snapshots are taken, reads each again later, and fails if the holder doesn't pause and move to the new epoch. A dead holder whose lease stands blocks snapshots until it is taken over, as 10-volumes.md §10.3 implies. | — |
| I123 | **A rollback is a new branch**, as 10-volumes.md §10.3 says: instant, and the old head's versions above anything still reading them are deleted at once. Each rollback adds a level to the chain (at most 16, then flatten). | Instant, and every snapshot of the old head keeps reading what it did. | Settled as D106 and built (I124). |
| I124 | **A reset in place** (D106): a rollback to the newest snapshot of the head's branch, whose chain is the head's, moves the head epoch on and records the epochs from the snapshot to the old head as skipped (`skip` in the header); reads take their layers as (branch, oldest, newest epoch) ranges around the skip, so the reset is instant for readers. The skipped versions are then deleted (`DropVersions` takes a range) and the record cleared; anything that needs a branch's versions whole (a snapshot, a deletion, another rollback, a flatten) finishes a pending trim first, so a reset that stopped half-way is finished by the next operation. A rollback to an older snapshot, or after a flatten changed the head's chain, stays a new branch. | An agent VM reset after every task stays on one branch: a test resets 20 times and checks the chain stays empty and no versions are left. The model test found the case of a flatten between snapshot and reset (the head's chain no longer the snapshot's), now a new branch. | — |
| I125 | **Consistency groups as built** (10-volumes.md §10.3): `Quiesce` puts a volume's holder on hold with a token (it drains, then writes wait) until a `Snapshot` with the same token, a `Release`, or 5 s; a group writes its `g/{id}` object as preparing, holds every member, takes each member's snapshot tagged with the group under the hold, and marks the group complete. On any failure the snapshots taken are deleted, the holds released and the group removed; `delete_group` removes a group's snapshots and the group. | Holding every member before cutting any is what makes the cut one moment: a test checks writes wait during a hold until it is released, or until it ends by itself. A test of a VM's two disks written in order checks each group is consistent; it can't by itself tell groups from separate snapshots taken quickly one after another, so the hold is tested directly. | A group whose client died after cutting some members leaves them tagged with a group that never completes, until `delete_group`; no background cleanup yet. |
| I126 | **Snapshots in the cluster API and `hyperstorctl`**: `ListSnapshots`, `CreateSnapshot`, `DeleteSnapshot`, `CloneVolume`, `RollbackVolume`, `FlattenVolume`, `CreateGroupSnapshot` and `DeleteGroupSnapshot`, run by the map service as a client of `.meta` like the volume calls (D77), audited; refusals because something depends on a snapshot have the reason `in_use`. | Operators can see and manage snapshots before the web UI; the end-to-end test drives every command through the installed binaries. | — |
| I134 | **The read cache as built** (D102): each `Client` keeps an LRU of 4 KiB blocks in memory (256 MiB by default, `set_read_cache_bytes`, 0 turns it off), keyed by lineage, the lineage's random `incarnation` in the header (copied to clones; a recreated volume gets a new one), branch, epoch, chunk and block. `ReadAt` answers (`ReadAtOk`) give each block's source version; only blocks from a version other than the volume's head (and not holes) are cached. An attached volume remembers each block's source until it writes the block or its header changes; a read that overlapped a write records nothing. When the cache holds blocks of the lineage, a read whose sources aren't known first asks `Sources` (the same as `ReadAt`, without the data), and is served from the cache if every block's version is in it; otherwise it reads as usual. Headers without an incarnation don't use the cache. | A version that isn't the head never changes (versions are never rewritten, epochs never reused, a merge only adds blocks a version lacked), so entries never need invalidating; the per-volume map is what can go stale, and only the holder's own writes or a header change move it. The `Sources` probe lets clones of one image share what one of them read, at one small round trip per miss, asked only once the lineage is cached. | Reads that mix cached and head blocks read everything from the network; serving the cached part and fetching the rest is left for when a measurement shows it matters. Not yet measured on hardware; the second tier on NVMe (D32) comes later. Forgetting sources on a header change isn't caught by a test: no operation that changes what an attached volume reads is allowed while it is attached. |
| I148 | **Paged checkpoints as built** (D105, D47): the index is cut into pages by key range, each about 128 KiB (`set_checkpoint_page`). Every change marks the page holding its key; a checkpoint takes the marks at its start and writes only those pages, each to fresh blocks, cutting a page in two once it passes the size and dropping one left empty (its range joins the page before). The directory, named by the superblock, lists every page (bound, length, checksum, extents) with the revision, group and log position; the pages a checkpoint replaced are freed once the superblock names it. A device whose checkpoint is one blob (format before this) opens as before, and its first checkpoint writes pages and frees the blob. Recovery reads every page in one batch and checks each object against its page's range. | A page nothing changed since the last checkpoint began holds what replay from the new start needs, since every change after that start is in the log; so checkpoint traffic follows the share of the index that changes, not its size (02-benchmarks.md, paged checkpoints). Key-range pages keep a PG's objects together; dropping empty pages and cutting full ones keeps pages near their size without a merge pass. The crash tests run a third of their seeds with 256-byte and 2 KiB pages, so pages are cut, dropped and kept across crashes. | Uniform random writes still touch every page each quarter of the log (no gain there; 02-benchmarks.md). A volume's chunks spread over all PGs, so one busy volume dirties pages across the shard; a page per PG range is what a busy volume touches. Small pages that stay small aren't merged; nothing has needed it yet. |
| I149 | **Two replay bugs, found by the crash test once checkpoints were paged** (both from deferred writes meeting a fuzzy checkpoint, since #25): (1) a deferred write and an absorb that moves the same block, in one round: the checkpoint page, written after, has the block in the absorbing object, so replay skipped the write as a hole and the block kept old data under the new checksum; (2) replaying a `Map` over a page that already has it freed the block it maps, cancelling its pending write, which hid a deferred write landing on a block remapped later. Fixed: a deferred write's record names its physical blocks (new record tag; old records replay as before); on replay, a write whose object no longer maps that block is held tentatively and kept only if, after replay, some object maps the block with the write's checksum; a `Map` never frees the blocks it maps. The write's fields are boxed in the logged operation, which keeps it 56 bytes (unboxed, 80 bytes made recovery 12–17% slower; 02-benchmarks.md, paged checkpoints). | The checkpoint's timing decides whether these appear: paging moved the crash points enough to hit both (seeds 212 and 1455). Unit tests now pin each case (`a_deferred_write_follows_its_block_to_another_object`, `a_deferred_write_to_a_block_since_remapped_is_dropped`); the crash test also catches them, over 3,000 seeds. | — |
| I150 | **Checkpoint pages stay 128 KiB** (measured 16–128 KiB, 02-benchmarks.md, checkpoint page size). | A volume's chunks spread over every PG, so a busy volume's chunks fall in different pages whatever their size: from 128 to 32 KiB, a tenth of the chunks written costs the same bytes per write, while uniform writes pay 12% more at 32 KiB (padding and headers). Only 16 KiB pages, about two chunk maps each, write 40% less for a busy volume, and they cost uniform writes 24% more. | For pools where a few volumes take most writes, 16 KiB pages are the better choice; a per-device setting would allow it. The on-device index (D43) would write only the changed nodes and remove the trade. |

## People's logins, first part (AD2)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I120 | **`hyperstor-authd` builds `glidex-authd`'s server from a pinned glidex revision** (glidex `main` at 6500c1f, after glidex #163, #164 and #171), with hyperstor's defaults when `/etc/hyperstor/authd.json` is absent (user `hyperstor`, group `hyperstor-users`, `/run/hyperstor-authd`); a file present is read as glidex-authd reads its own, starting from glidex's defaults. The installer writes the file, the socket and service units and `/etc/pam.d/hyperstor` (Debian and Ubuntu includes) on the map service's host. | One audited PAM helper for both projects (D99); writing the file makes the values explicit wherever it is installed. | The PAM file for Fedora and RHEL (`system-auth`), as glidex's installer writes. |
| I121 | **mgmt's rows: keys up to 256 bytes, values up to 64 KiB, lists of 1000 at most**, `put` with an optional "only if absent"; reads from the member's applied copy, writes through the leader. Only `mgmt` certificates may call `MgmtStoreService`; the calls aren't audited one by one. Backups include the table. | Accounts, sessions and tokens are small; "only if absent" makes creating a user or a claim race-free. mgmt audits what people do; auditing its own bookkeeping would double every line. | — |


## People's logins, second part (AD2)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I127 | **Roles are groups of actions, evaluated in Cedar** (glidex-auth's engine, D96): every RPC of the protos is an action `Service.Method`, plus mgmt's own (`People.*`, `Dashboard.Read`), each in one group (read, operate, store, secure, own) or machines-only (joins, renewals, mgmt's rows), which no person gets. `viewer` reads; `operator` and `storage-admin` add to it; `security-admin` stands apart and doesn't read storage. A test fails when an RPC has no action. | Deny by default; a new RPC can't be reachable by people before someone decides who may call it. A security administrator who can't touch data separates the duties, as glidex does. | Settled 2026-10-22: security administrators don't read storage status. Whether operators should create volumes stays open. |
| I128 | **Step-up for what is hard to undo**: deleting volumes, snapshots, group snapshots and members, rolling a volume back, join tokens and API tokens, revoking certificates, map backups, people's roles and accounts need a login of the last 10 minutes (`base.step-up`, a forbid). API tokens are never fresh. Refusals say `reauth_required`. | A stolen token or a session left open can't destroy data or grant itself more; glidex uses the same rule and window. | The window (10 min, glidex's); whether rollbacks belong here. |
| I129 | **The cluster API through mgmt is a pass-through**: `POST /api/hyperstor.v1.<Service>/<Method>` is authorized as that action, then sent as it came (Connect, gRPC-Web, JSON or protobuf; protocol headers only) over mutual TLS as mgmt's identity to each `--api` member until one answers other than 503. Answers up to 256 MiB. The map service audits the call as `mgmt`; mgmt audits it as the person. | One API and one client library for the CLI, the UI and scripts; mgmt doesn't need to know each call's messages. | Settled for now (2026-10-22): the map service audits these calls as `mgmt`. Naming the person there too comes later. |
| I130 | **The first security administrator claims the cluster** with a code mgmt prints to its log while no one is one: 18 random base62 characters, valid an hour, stored hashed, replaced at each start, gone once used. Logging in with it adds `security-admin` to that person. | Someone with access to the host's journal is already trusted with the host; no default password to change. | — |
| I131 | **Logins only through `--authd`; the read-only dashboard stays without it**, loopback only. Beyond loopback, mgmt needs `--authd` and `--web-tls` (`web.crt`, `web.key`). The installer adds `--authd` and keeps `127.0.0.1`; it issues no web certificate yet. | A browser can't trust the cluster's CA without people installing it; until there is a way to bring one's own certificate, SSH tunnels are the safe default. | Settled 2026-10-22 as D107: all three, in time. |
| I132 | **Cookies and writes**: `hs_session`, `HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict; Path=/` (and `Secure` over HTTPS); a write with the cookie needs the session's CSRF value in `X-Hyperstor-CSRF` (compared in constant time), and a write with an `Origin` other than its own is refused. Bearer tokens need neither. A first login makes an account with no roles. | Two independent defences against cross-site writes, as glidex's UI; tokens aren't sent by browsers on their own. | — |
| I133 | **`serde_json` keeps insertion order in the whole workspace now**: `cedar-policy-core` turns on its `preserve_order` feature, and cargo unifies features. `hyperstorctl -o yaml` sorts keys itself; `-o json` follows the protos' field order instead of sorting. | Found by `hyperstorctl`'s YAML test; output that people diff should not depend on a dependency's features. | — |
| I135 | **mgmt names the person to the map service**: each call it passes on carries `X-Hyperstor-On-Behalf-Of: user:NAME`. The map service believes it only from mgmt's certificate, refuses it from anyone else and refuses a malformed one, and then audits and rate-limits the call as `<mgmt's identity> for user:NAME`. mgmt drops a browser's own such header. | The owner's question on I129: both audits name the person, at the cost of one header; mgmt's key can already change the cluster, so a signature would add nothing. | — |
| I136 | **An identity provider's person is named by the email it vouches for**, lowercased (≤ 64 characters), and tied to the provider's issuer and subject (`oidc/<sha256>` rows): a changed email keeps the account; an unverified or missing email gets none. Roles come from security administrators, as for passwords; the claim code and step-up work through the provider. | Emails are what people and administrators recognise, and can't collide with local user names; the subject is what providers promise is stable. | Mapping the provider's groups to roles; naming by `preferred_username` instead. |
| I137 | **OIDC logins are begun with `POST /auth/oidc/start`** (→ the provider's URL), bound to the browser by `hs_oidc` (`HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax`, path the callback, 10 min), and finished at `GET /auth/oidc/callback`, which sets the session and sends the browser on with 303. Failures are a plain page (403) and audited. The client secret is read from a file at each use. | A claim code in the body, never a URL (history, logs, `Referer`); `Lax` because the provider's redirect is a cross-site navigation, while the session cookie stays `Strict`. | — |
| I138 | **`hyperstorctl --mgmt URL`** goes through mgmt as a person, with an API token from `--token-file` or `$HYPERSTOR_TOKEN` (never an argument), under `/api`; `http://` only to loopback; over HTTPS it trusts the host's CAs and `--mgmt-ca`. | The CLI with people's roles and audit, for operators without the administrator's certificate; tokens stay out of process lists and off the wire in clear. | A device-flow `hyperstorctl login` instead of pasted tokens. |
| I139 | **mgmt's refusals are Connect errors** with the reason as an `ErrorInfo` detail (and as `"reason"` for browsers); `hyperstorctl delete-volume` now shows a refusal's reason and exit status (4 when not allowed) like the other commands, instead of always 1. | One way to read a refusal, whether mgmt or the map service refused it. | — |
| I140 | **hyperstor's UI builds `@glidex/ui-kit` from a copy**, unchanged, in `crates/hyperstor-mgmt/ui-kit`, at the glidex commit its `UPSTREAM` file names (the one the Rust crates pin); `scripts/ui-kit-sync.sh REV` replaces it, and CI fails when it differs from glidex at that commit. | The kit is TypeScript source glidex doesn't publish, and bun can't take a package from a repository's subdirectory; a copy checked against its source builds offline and can't drift unseen, like cargo's pinned git crates. | Publishing the kit (a registry, or a repository of its own) once a third app wants it. |
| I141 | **The web UI as built** (D97, D100): React, Vite, TypeScript and Tailwind with Connect-ES over the Connect protocol (JSON) to mgmt's `/api`, its TypeScript generated from the protos and committed (CI checks it is current). Pages: overview, nodes and devices (out, in, drain with its preview), pools and PGs, volumes with snapshots, clones, rollback, flatten and group snapshots, operations, the map service's members, security (people and roles, your API tokens, join tokens, certificates). Every action shows its `hyperstorctl` command; what your roles don't allow isn't shown (the server decides). No audit page yet: audit has no API (AD3). The map backup stays with `hyperstorctl`, a file to keep. Light only, as the kit's dialogs. | §23.7's pages for what exists; Connect's JSON is what mgmt passes through and what `curl` sees. | A theme switch; charts from sampled counters (§23.7). |
| I142 | **The installer installs the UI's built files** from `--ui DIR` (else `hyperstor-ui` beside it) to `/usr/local/share/hyperstor/ui`, exactly the new build's (old hashed assets removed), and adds `--ui` to mgmt's options when they are there; a run with neither keeps what is installed (the renewal timer's runs); `uninstall` removes them. | D100: the UI updates without a new binary, and bun is needed only where it is built. | Shipping the built UI in release archives as `hyperstor-ui`. |
| I143 | **Browser tests** (`crates/hyperstor-mgmt/e2e`, Playwright, Chromium) run the built UI against a cluster `ui-e2e-cluster` installs from the release binaries, with the made-up PAM helper and an identity provider that logs any browser in; their results are written as `cargo test` prints them (`browser::…`), so the tracking check counts them. | glidex's practice (D97 asked for it); the claim, roles, CSRF and the provider round trip are checked in a real browser, not only over HTTP. | — |
| I144 | **mgmt on several addresses** (D110): `--listen` takes a list (`0.0.0.0:8852,[::]:8852`, the IPv6 socket `V6ONLY`, through `glidex_tls::bind`); a host without IPv6 serves IPv4 only. Beyond loopback the host's own names and addresses (`glidex_tls::LocalNames`: host name, FQDN, interface addresses) join the `Host` allowlist, and so do the Tailscale names a certificate is held for. `HS_WEB_TLS_POLL_MS` shortens the certificate check from a minute, for tests. | glidex's listeners and allowlist, shared rather than rewritten; a test needn't wait a minute for a reload. | — |
| I145 | **mgmt's web certificate**: identity `mgmt/web`, from the intermediate, for the names and addresses `LocalNames` finds when the installer runs (recorded beside it, re-issued when they change, renewed by the timer); the root isn't in its chain (browsers that import the root get it from their store). mgmt re-reads it, so a new one restarts nothing. | D108; the addresses a host has at install time are what people use, and a change is picked up by the next run (daily). | A host whose address changes often (DHCP): the timer's day of `421` and warnings until the next run. |
| I146 | **The installer's Tailscale grant** is glidex's (glidex's tailscale-tls.md §4.6): `TS_PERMIT_CERT_UID=hyperstor` in a marked drop-in, written only when no one holds it, kept when it is already `hyperstor`, and refused (nothing changed, mgmt not told to fetch) when someone else holds it; `tailscaled` is restarted before mgmt. `--no-tailscale-cert` and `uninstall` remove only the marked drop-in. | `tailscaled` grants one user; on a host with glidex-ui, the first to ask keeps it. | One grant for both services (a shared group); see G24. |
| I147 | **The end-to-end suite keeps each test's mgmt on loopback** (the installed `0.0.0.0` becomes `127.0.0.1`) and speaks HTTPS to it, verified against the cluster's root; one test (`web::`) runs the installed every-address options. The browser tests ignore certificate errors: they test the UI, not the CA. | A test's cluster isn't reachable from the network for the minutes it runs; the HTTPS path is exercised everywhere. | — |
| I151 | **A returning node was reported silent again** (D40): a node's peer check remembered when it last heard each peer and never forgot one, so a peer that came back after being down (or shared a PG again) was reported at once, by both other nodes of a three-node cluster: marked down a second time, its lease revoked, its PGs moved away and back (about 180 map epochs on the trial cluster). Fixed: a peer that leaves the set starts afresh. The map service now logs every node going down or up, with why (no heartbeat for N ms, or reported silent by which nodes), and a failed end-to-end test prints its cluster's logs. | Found while looking at CI's intermittent failures (`manage::io_on_every_pool`, `manage::a_killed_node_is_reported_and_comes_back`); `manage::a_returning_node_goes_down_once` failed 3 of 3 without the fix. Those CI failures came at the first I/O after a fresh start, which this doesn't explain; one loaded CPU here showed no false downs, so the logs will say on CI. | Whether D48's timeouts (peer report after 300 ms, lease 600 ms) are too tight for small or overloaded hosts, once CI's logs show a cause. |
| I152 | **The trash as built** (D54): a trashed volume is header state 2 with the time it went (appended to the header; older readers stop before it), still in its pool's directory, not listed, not attachable (storage nodes grant no lease on a header that isn't active), no snapshots taken. A trashed snapshot has its time set, isn't listed and can't be cloned from or rolled back to; it is marked first and then checked for dependents, as a delete is. Trashing is refused as deleting is (attached unless forced; something reads it), so a purge can always go on. Restore clears the mark; a volume whose name another active volume of the pool took comes back as `NAME-restored`. Purge is the delete of before; a group's object goes with its last snapshot. | §23.9.3, with the fewest new states: the trash is a mark, and purging reuses the delete that resumes after a crash. | Whether trashing a volume should also be allowed while a clone reads it (purging only when none does). |
| I153 | **The purger** (on the API's thread since I165) runs on the map service's leader every minute (`HS_TRASH_SCAN_MS` for tests): for each data pool, its volumes' headers are read and what has been in the trash past the pool's grace is purged, audited as `map-service/purger`. Grace periods and `trash_on_full` are rows of a new `settings` table of the replicated store (in map backups), not the map: only the map service needs them. | Simple and restartable: a purge that stops half-way is found again next minute. | Reading every header each minute costs one lookup per volume: an index of trashed items if pools hold many thousands. |
| I154 | **Roles for the trash** (D114): reading the trash and its settings is `read`; restoring, and taking snapshots alone or in groups, are `operate`; purging and setting the grace periods are `store` with step-up. The delete calls keep their step-up. | §23.9.2's table, settled by the owner. | — |
| I155 | **The audit log as built** (D112, D113): one `.meta` object a day, `audit/YYYY-MM-DD` (UTC), its records key–value entries keyed by the time counted down (newest first in a lookup), a per-process tag and a sequence number; the value is the JSON line journald gets. `audit/days` indexes the days. Every member writes its own records, batched each second (up to 500 per operation list); a queue of up to 10,000 waits while `.meta` can't be reached, and a dropped count is recorded when it overflows. hyperstor-mgmt ships its records through `AppendAudit` (mgmt's identity only; lines checked to be audit records). Reads filter by time, principal (contains) and operation, paging with `DAY:KEY`. The leader drops whole days past the retention (today counts as kept) hourly. | §23.9.4: append-only in the replicated pool, no Raft growth; journald stays the first copy, so a `.meta` outage loses nothing that matters. | A filter that skips whole days by principal (an index) if logs grow large; records' order across members is by their clocks. |
| I156 | **Reading the audit log is for administrators**: the API takes the `admin` or `mgmt` identity (not storage nodes or hosts); through mgmt it is `security-admin`'s (`secure`), and setting the retention needs step-up. | §23.9.2: storage and security apart, so no one covers their own tracks. | — |
| I157 | **A cancelled drain answers once its map is published**: bringing the devices back in changes the working map, and the cancel now waits (up to 5 s) for the published map to reach that epoch, so what is read next shows them in. | `operations::drains_run_as_operations` failed on CI three times: `nodes` read right after the cancel still showed a device out. | — |
| I163 | **Operations as built** (D120): rows of a new `operations` table of the replicated store (in map backups), keyed by a random 16-hex id, each the operation the API shows plus what it needs to run again (a drain's node, devices and PGs; a flatten's volume; a purge's volume and epoch; volume ids as hex, since serde can't hold a `u128` in a tagged enum). Without a store (no `--data`) they are kept in memory. Any member answers `GetOperation`, `ListOperations` and `WatchOperation` from its copy; only the leader writes. Every 2 s the leader resumes the running operations it isn't following (after a restart or a change of leader) and every hour removes those finished more than a week ago. | Each kind restarts safely from its row: a resumed drain takes the node's devices out again (the e2e test found that a drain changes only the working map, committed when published, so one taken just before the map service was killed came back undone, and the drain waited forever) and is followed from the map; a flatten copies what is left; a purge deletes what is left. The e2e test kills the map service mid-drain and the drain finishes after the restart. | A member that lost the lead in the middle of a flatten or purge is fenced by the next leader (D125, I168). A person's `in` during a drain that the leader hadn't yet seen before it stopped is undone by the resumed drain. |
| I164 | **Flatten and purge are checked, then run** (D121): `FlattenVolume` and `PurgeFromTrash` read the volume's header first and refuse at once what would fail (no such volume or snapshot, `not_found`; not in the trash, `in_use`), then record the operation and return it (a flatten with the volume as it is now). The call is audited as started with the operation's id, and its end under the same call name (`ok, operation ID done`, or why it failed). Their progress is 0 of 1 until done: the client library reports no progress per chunk. Only a drain can be cancelled; the others answer `failed_precondition` with reason `not_cancellable`, whatever their state. | What a person did wrong shows at once, not in an operation that fails a second later. Stopping a flatten or a purge half-way leaves nothing anyone wants. | Progress per chunk for large volumes (the client would report chunks done). |
| I165 | **The purger is on the API's thread now**, no longer the reactor's (I153): on the leader, every minute, it lists the trash through the map service and starts a purge operation, as `map-service/purger`, for each item past its pool's grace that has none running, nor one that failed in the last hour. | Its purges show beside the people's, with progress and failures kept a week; one place runs every long operation. The hour keeps a purge that can't finish from adding an operation every minute. | — |
| I166 | **Watching an operation**: `WatchOperation` sends the operation now, then each change (read every 250 ms), and again every 2 s so a proxy doesn't take the stream for idle, ending once it has finished. `hyperstorctl op watch` and `--wait` (on `drain`, `flatten-volume`, `trash purge`) follow it with the watch, a call whose deadline is `--wait` (else a day), started again if the member stops answering. Through `--mgmt` they ask `GetOperation` every half second instead: mgmt passes on whole answers only, so a stream would arrive when it ends. The UI polls the list every 2 s as before. | Server streams are in the protocol already; polling a row locally is cheap. | Streaming through mgmt (its responses would carry a streaming body), then the UI could watch too. |
| I168 | **Fencing as built** (D125): `Meta` bumps a lead generation whenever a member gains or loses the lead, and the admin loop's client (all volume work: operations, creates, snapshots) is made anew for each, so its random instance changes; the audit writer keeps its own. `Admin::Lead` reports the role with that client's instance, and `Admin::Blocklist` runs the existing forced-takeover blocklist (it waits 1.25 leases after the election, D73, then for every node to hold the entry). The runner fences once per lead (by when it began) before its upkeep resumes anything and before any new operation is created; the record is the settings row `operations/instance`. A map service of one without Raft has no other leader and skips it. The log says which instance was blocklisted. | The blocklist already stops every message of an instance at every storage node (`hyperstord` refuses all but pings), and its lifetime (an hour) is far longer than a cut-off run lasts: its first refused write ends it. Refusing to reuse a purged volume's id is unneeded: until a purge ends the header exists, so a create with that id is refused, and after it the old run is fenced. The e2e test restarts the map service mid-drain and checks the old process's client is blocklisted before the drain carries on, and that the new one still writes. | The first operation after an election waits for the fence, about a second (1.25 leases and the nodes' acknowledgements). A former leader cut off from every storage node for over an hour and then reconnected could write once more: its requests would have timed out long before. |
| I169 | **512e as built** (D126): `blk_size` 512 and the topology fields in the configuration space; `decode` wants whole sectors. Reads of a partial block read the whole blocks and return the part. A write takes a per-volume range lock on its whole blocks (a list of held ranges; waiters are woken on every release and look again), then writes whole blocks as they are, or reads the partial end blocks (one read when both ends are in the same block), merges and writes the span. Write-zeroes pieces now end on 1 MiB boundaries, so only a range's own ends can be partial. | Reads take no lock: a read racing a write may see either, as on any disk. The lock list is scanned per write; with at most a queue's worth of writes in flight, that is short. Tests: `requests_decode_to_whole_sectors_inside_the_volume`, and `a_volume_is_served_over_vhost_user_blk` with partial blocks read and written, eight writes into one block's sectors in flight at once (the guest helper's `write_together`; without the lock one is lost, checked) and write-zeroes of two sectors; the VM test checks the guest's 512/4096. | Unaligned random writes cost a read each; not measured yet. |
| I170 | **A joined member can run a storage node too**: `hyperstor-install install --join API --role both --node N` joins as map service member (its `api` certificate, the intermediate CA and the cluster file, as `--role meta`), then installs node N of that file, as the first member's `--role both` does. `--node` is checked before the token is sent, so a refused run doesn't burn it. | Found installing a pilot on three hosts, each a member and a storage node: `--join` took one role only, and a member that doesn't join has no CA. | Like the first member's, such a node has no certificate of its own, unlike a joined storage host's `node/N`: the storage network uses none yet. |
| I171 | **Two fixes from the first install across hosts**: (1) a join on a fresh host failed setting its key's group, `hyperstor`, which the install step creates later: the join now creates the service user first, and a member's join writes the intermediate CA and the cluster file before its own key, so a run that fails after the token is spent can be finished without one. (2) mgmt offered the first security administrator's claim code for 2 minutes only; the first member of a new group has no quorum until another joins, so none was printed. It now asks until the store answers (every second for 2 minutes, then every 30 s). | Found installing a three-host pilot (three members, each also a storage node). The e2e suite installs under `--root`, which skips users and `chgrp`, so it can't show (1); the pilot's hosts did. | A real-root install test (in a container or VM) would catch the first kind; `scripts/e2e.sh --installed` checks a host after the fact. |
| I172 | **A follower's status no longer warns about what only the leader knows**: nodes send heartbeats, and scrub its results, to the leader only, so `GetStatus` on a follower reported every node "not heard from since the map service started" and health `warn`. A follower now answers from the map (the leader's published one, which has nodes up or down), without heartbeat times or scrub results, and adds an `ok` note naming the leader for those. A member that knows no leader (before the first election, say) warns instead: nothing can change the map, and `hyperstorctl status --wait` must keep waiting (CI caught the first version, which answered `ok` there). | Found on the three-host pilot: `hyperstorctl status` on prism1 or gpu-dev2 warned while the leader said `ok`. | Forwarding `GetStatus` to the leader would give the whole answer on any member; the member would need to call the leader's API as a client. |
| I173 | **Restarts as built** (D143): `<dir>/state.json` (mode 0600, written beside and renamed after every attach and detach) holds the client instance and each volume with its serial. A start reads it before the control socket answers, attaches each volume, and on `held` by that same instance takes it over (≤ 4 s for the lease to lapse, then the old instance is blocklisted); anything else drops the volume with a line on stderr. SIGTERM no longer detaches; it removes the control socket and exits. With `--group`, the directory is made 0750 and given the group. | A state file in the runtime directory, not the data directory: after a reboot no VM uses the volumes, and their leases should lapse, not come back. Tests: `the_state_file_round_trips_and_bad_ones_say_why`; `a_restarted_service_takes_its_volumes_back` (two volumes back on the same sockets with their data; one taken over by another service while it was down is left to it). | Stopping the service for good without detaching leaves its volumes held (a plain attach elsewhere is refused until a takeover): the installer detaches them first; a person stopping it by hand must too. |
| I174 | **In-flight tracking as built** (D145): `inflight.rs` keeps libvhost-user's layout per queue in a memfd from `GET_INFLIGHT_FD` (or the frontend's from `SET_INFLIGHT_FD`). A chain is marked when popped (counter, then the flag); its completion records `last_batch_head`, goes on the used ring, then clears the flag and stores the used index. At each ring start, a buffer in use gives the next available index (used index plus the marked) and the heads to run again, sorted by counter; their segments are read from the descriptor table (and an indirect table) directly, as `virtio-queue` builds chains only from the ring. Host check G6: Cloud Hypervisor v53 reconnects to a restarted backend on its own and QEMU 8.2.2 with `reconnect=1`; both negotiate `INFLIGHT_SHMFD`. | Without the buffer the frontend's ring base after a reconnect skipped requests the old process never took, and Cloud Hypervisor's guest hung on them. Tests: `requests_taken_and_not_answered_are_found_after_a_restart`; the VM test restarts the service under each hypervisor while the guest writes 16 MiB in 4 KiB direct requests (both regions read back right); without the rerun the Cloud Hypervisor guest hangs (checked). | Packed rings, when offered, need their own layout. Completions of a ring stopped by `GET_VRING_BASE` are still dropped (I161), but they stay marked and run again at the next start. |
| I175 | **The installer and `hyperstor-vhost`** (D142, D144): `--vhost`, `--no-vhost`, `--vhost-user NAME` (repeatable; replaces the saved list) and `--role vhost`; `--map` takes several addresses only for `--role vhost`. Users are resolved to ids at install (`getpwnam`): `vhost.env` carries `--allow-uid` for each and `--group` for the first one's primary group, the unit `SupplementaryGroups=` with every one's. An unknown user fails the install and `check` (`vhost` line). A changed binary, unit or `vhost.env` restarts the service, which keeps its VMs' disks (D143). Uninstall and `--no-vhost` detach every listed volume, then disable the unit. | Ids, not names, in the arguments: `hyperstor-vhost` takes uids and gids, and a renamed user keeps them. Tests: `vhost_is_installed_for_its_users`, `the_volumes_hyperstor_vhost_lists_are_read`; install's `hyperstor_vhost_is_installed_for_its_users` starts the service from the installed `vhost.env` and attaches a volume through it. | Not checked under systemd on a real host yet (glidex's end-to-end run). |
| I176 | **`hyperstorctl --map` takes the map service's members** (`write-volume`, `read-volume`): `ADDR[,ADDR...]`, connected with `Client::connect_members`, so any member that answers serves; `member add` still takes one address. Found by glidex's first install against a three-member map service: glidex passes the members the join gave it, and a single address was all `--map` parsed, so making a disk failed with the usage line. | The members as glidex has them, not one picked: a member that is down must not stop disk creation. Test: `enroll`'s partition-table write names an address that doesn't answer, then the cluster's. | Single-member test clusters hid it; the multi-member suites don't use the data path's tools. |
| I177 | **`.meta` PG 0.63 stuck below min_size on a three-host pilot cluster, and pushes in parts** (D146): glidex's image loop (glidex #180) made about 660,000 refused, audited calls in two hours, so the day's audit object (`audit/2026-10-24`, one object a day) grew to about 220 MiB. When that PG's acting set shrank to its primary during node flapping, every backfill attempt sent the object in one `PushObject` frame, over the 64 MiB limit, and the receiver dropped the connection (`unexpected end of file`); attempts every 300 ms each digested and compared the object (0.25–1 s of reactor time), which most likely starved the node's heartbeats and kept it flapping. Now `push_parts` reads an object once and splits its table into parts of `push_part_bytes` (`NodeConfig`, default 16 MiB): `PushObject` with the first, `PushKv` (kind 67) with the rest; the receiver applies `PushKv` as `KvPut` lists after the object its connection's last `PushObject` replaced. Backfill, peer sync and scrub repair use it. | Found with the diagnostic build (I178) on that cluster. Test: `a_large_key_value_table_is_refilled_in_parts` (a replica away while a ~420 KiB table changes gets it back in about 26 parts of 16 KiB; the harness sets 16 KiB parts for every cluster test); sending only the first part leaves the replica with 75 of 2,049 entries (checked). | Not fixed here: `Fetch` still answers with a whole object in one frame (read repair of such an object fails); refused calls are audited without limit; a day's audit object has no size bound; comparing a large object's digests blocks a reactor; RPCs have no timeouts or keepalive (a node held hundreds of idle peer connections). |
| I178 | **A diagnostic build of `hyperstord`**: `HYPERSTOR_DIAG=1 cargo build` sets the `hs_diag` cfg (`hyperstor-daemon/build.rs`), and `diag!` lines (`diag.rs`) go to standard error, each kind at most once per 10 s with a count of those held back: every backfill attempt's steps and why it fails (no address, connecting, the target's answer to `Digests`, objects differing and how long comparing took, a push's answer, `BackfillDone`'s answer), attempts running over 10 s, and drains that wait. Without it the macro expands to nothing. | Backfill failures were silent, so a stuck PG showed only as a count in `status`. A separate build keeps production quiet and costs nothing there; clippy runs clean in both. | More of the daemon could say what it does under the same option (peering, heartbeats, fences). |
| I179 | **Large objects keep their digest until they change**: `Shard::digest` hashed an object's whole key–value table each time, synchronously; for the ~220 MiB audit object of I177 that took 0.3–1 s of reactor time (one target took 13.8 s to answer `Digests` under load), on the primary and both targets of every backfill attempt, every 300 ms, so heartbeats (600 ms leases) were missed and the nodes kept flapping even with parts (D146). Now a digest of an object with at least 1,024 entries is kept with the revision it was taken at (every list that leaves an object gives it a fresh revision from the shard's counter) and answered again while the revision is the same; revision 0 is never trusted; at most 4,096 are kept. | In memory only: a reopen starts empty. Test: `large_objects_keep_their_digest_until_they_change` (two objects alike; each kind of change sets them apart until both have it; delete and remake; reopen); answering a cached digest whatever the revision fails it (checked). | The first digest after a change still takes the reactor for as long; hashing in steps that yield, or keeping a running digest, would remove that too. |
| I180 | **A follower's Raft could deadlock on its own metrics**: the task that reports who leads read openraft's metrics (`watch::Receiver::borrow`, a read lock) for the whole of a `match`, and its arm for another member's leadership read them again to find that member's map address. When openraft queued a write between the two reads, the fair lock made the second read wait for the writer and the writer for the first read: that member's Raft stopped for good (its port took connections and never answered) while its cluster API still served. The leader's id is now copied out before the match. | Found on the three-host pilot: prism1's member deadlocked at its first start after a reboot; with gpu-dev2 down, prism2 had no majority for a day and a half, and when gpu-dev2's node failed it couldn't be marked down, so I/O to its PGs hung. `gdb` showed the worker parked in `RwLock::read` under `replica::run`. | Hard to test: it needs openraft to write between the two reads. A lint against holding a `watch` borrow across calls would catch the pattern. |
| I181 | **A leader that can't commit says so**: a map commit now times out after 5 s (logged with the epoch and why), the publisher tries again every half second instead of every 10 ms, and `GetStatus` on the leader is an error ("hasn't committed a map change for N s") until a commit succeeds. Before, the commit waited forever and nothing was logged: a stalled leader looked healthy while it couldn't act on a failure. | Same incident (I180): prism2 waited on one commit for 36 hours; after a restart, refusals were logged 400 times a minute. e2e: `members::a_leader_without_a_majority_says_so`. | A commit that times out may still be applied later; the publisher sends the same map again, which is harmless. |
| I182 | **A copy is fetched in parts** (#62): a primary pulling a member's copy of an object (scrub outvoted its own, or a read found it damaged) took it in one `FetchOk`, so an object whose key–value table exceeds the frame limit (the audit object of I177) could never be repaired. Now `FetchPart { first, kv_from }` mirrors the pushes of D146: the first part is the object with up to `push_part_bytes` of its table and where the rest starts; each further part is the entries from there. The fetcher applies `object_lists` for the first and `kv_lists` for the rest, all under the object's exec lock, so neither copy changes between parts. A member from before this answers that it doesn't know the request, and gets the whole-object `Fetch`, which stays as it was. | Test: `an_outvoted_primary_fetches_a_large_table_in_parts` (a primary's copy of a ~420 KiB table replaced by a one-entry one; scrub pulls the majority's in 16 KiB parts; every copy then holds the same 2,000 entries); following only the first part fails it (checked). | A copy whose later part fails is left half replaced and marked damaged until the next scrub or read repairs it; a member that changes leadership between parts is not detected beyond the PG epoch check. |
| I183 | **Flap damping and yielding as built** (D149): `liveness_tick` keeps each node's down transitions of the last 10 minutes and a hold (`flap_hold`: 2 s doubled per earlier down in the window, at most 60 s); a node that is alive again stays down until its hold passes, and the log's down line says how long ("held down 2 s"). `Meta::flapping` (nodes down twice or more in the window) goes to `GetStatus` beside `heard` and `inconsistent`. A new leader starts every node afresh, as it does liveness. In the daemon, `local_digests` (the `Digests` answer and scrub's own copy) and backfill's comparison work in batches of 64 objects with a yield between, so a PG of large objects no longer holds the reactor (and the shard) for the whole PG; an object written between two batches is compared as its batch finds it, which scrub's second look and backfill's live writes already allow. `push_parts` still reads one object synchronously: its parts must be one snapshot. | Tests: `a_flapping_node_is_held_down_longer_each_time` (the hold, its doubling and cap, the warning, ten quiet minutes, no lease while reported); ignoring the hold, or never doubling it, fails it (checked); `manage::a_returning_node_goes_down_once` checks the hold in the log of a real cluster. | One large object's first digest after a change still takes the reactor for as long as it takes; hashing it in steps would need the table walked across yields. A stalled reactor still misses pings: damping limits what that costs, it doesn't prevent it. |
| I184 | **Calls to peers end, and connections don't pile up** (#63): the daemon's `peer()` and the client's `conn()` checked their cache, awaited a connect, then inserted, so concurrent callers each connected and all but one connection leaked; and a call to a peer that stopped answering (without closing) waited forever. Now (1) both use `hyperstor_rpc::Pool`: one connect per address at a time, the others wait for it; (2) `Conn::call_within` gives a call a deadline (10 s, `CALL_TIMEOUT`) after which its pending entry goes and the connection is killed, since a peer that silent is broken; `try_within` only gives up (pings, 200 ms, which share the connection with replication); applied to backfill, scrub's light digests and repair pushes, fetches, and the client's map-service calls (which then try the next member); replication keeps D91's; deep scrub's digests, which read every block, have none; (3) every connection, accepted or made, gets TCP keepalive (10 s idle, 5 s interval, 3 probes) and `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` 30 s, in the reactor's `net.rs`; (4) a connect gives up after 3 s; (5) the server side keeps idle connections: a client may hold one quietly between calls, and keepalive ends the dead ones. | Tests: `connections_keep_alive_both_ways`, `a_call_past_its_deadline_fails_and_takes_the_connection_down` (keeping the pending entry, or not killing, fails it; checked), `a_connect_that_hangs_gives_up`, `concurrent_callers_share_one_connect` (100 callers, one connect; without single-flight, 100; checked). | The values (10 s, 3 s, keepalive and 30 s) are guesses to confirm under load; no cluster-level count of connections per peer is tested. |
| I185 | **The audit log bounded** (D147, D148; hyperstor #60, #61): refusals go through `audit_log::refused`, which counts them per principal, operation and connect error code (`permission_denied`, `resource_exhausted`, …) in windows of 60 s from the first: ten are queued for `.meta`, the rest only counted; the writer flushes the windows that ended every second as `Refused` records ("refused N more OP calls from P in a minute (CODE: last message)", with the operation and the last target as its target), and a refusal arriving after an unflushed window brings its summary. Every refusal still goes to standard error. Parts: the writer keeps, for at most four recent days, this member's part number and the bytes written to it (keys plus values); the first write of a day in a process lists `audit/parts` for that day and opens the part after its member's highest (a restart never appends to a part whose size it doesn't know); a part is listed in `audit/parts` and the day in `audit/days` before records go into it; a batch is split so no part passes 16 MiB (a single record always fits an empty part). A read lists the day's parts and adds the old object, reads each a page of 1,000 at a time from the cursor's key, and takes the smallest key in front; pruning deletes each part and its entry, then the old object, then the day's entry, and leaves the day listed if any delete failed. | The member is `--id`. Record keys stay unique across members by the process tag and sequence, so the merge needs no coordination and the cursor (`DAY:HEXKEY`) is unchanged. Tests: `repeated_refusals_are_summarised` (250 refusals of one kind over 25 s keep 10 and summarise 240; other operations and codes apart; a late refusal brings the last window's summary); `audit_parts_roll_over_merge_and_are_dropped` (in-process cluster: an old one-object day and two members with 1 KiB parts; every part within its size, a restart opens a new part, paging 7 at a time returns all 61 records once in order, filters work across parts, a dropped day leaves no parts, entries or objects); `a_refusal_storm_is_summarised_and_pages` (end to end: 15 refused calls from a node's certificate, all 15 in the journal, 10 plus one summary of 5 in `.meta`, paged through mgmt three at a time). Ten mutations (no rollover, reusing a part after a restart, skipping parts or the old object on read or prune, a repeated cursor key, keeping 11, an off-by-one window, losing a late summary, no merge order) each fail a test (checked). | Windows live in each member's memory: a restart starts counting afresh, and a storm spread over members is counted per member. Summaries record the last target only. mgmt's `AppendAudit` refusals are not summarised. |
| I186 | **Per-volume fencing and delegates as built** (D150, D151, D152): `Target` gains `lease` (protocol version 2, 32 bytes a target); a client's `attach`, `take_over` and `preempt` each pick a random identity, which the `Attached` handle sends on every request (`write_under`, `read_layers`), on its renewals (`LeaseRenew` entries carry it) and on its watch and quiesce. The header's `lease` attribute is now identity, instance and mode (17 bytes; the 9-byte form reads as identity = instance), and `Volume.holder` is the identity. Delegates live in `lease.delegates`, apart from `lease`, so an operation that asserts the holder (trash, delete) still matches while delegates change; `LeaseDelegates` sets the list, holder only. Storage nodes answer `Fenced` (code 13) for a blocklisted identity and `Blocklisted` for an instance; only client connections (instance ≠ 0) are checked, since a primary forwards the client's target to its replicas. `LeaseHeld` carries the delegates, so a take-over blocklists the identity and all of them in one map change (`Blocklist { ids }`), and breaks the lease only when all are listed. A renewal of a fenced identity is answered lost. Each lease records its holder's label (D153: `Lease.label`, kept in the `lease` attribute after the mode, up to 64 bytes, answered in `LeaseHeld`); `Client::set_lease_label` sets a client's, `attach_as` one lease's. The quiesce keeps the identities still to drain, the holder's and each delegate's. `hyperstor-vhost` saves each disk's identity in its state file and, restarted, takes each volume over from that identity alone; its `list` reports a disk whose lease was taken elsewhere as `lost`, and `attach` of one lets it go and attaches afresh (for D153: glidex starting the VM on that node again). | One mechanism for both: the blocklist already keys on 64-bit ids, so identities and instances share it with no map format change. Checking client connections only keeps a write in flight from being refused by a replica after its primary accepted it. | A client request with identity 0 (tools, the map service's own work) is fenced only by instance, as before: the identity protects against a stale holder, not a hostile one (mTLS decides who may connect, D25). Each preemption adds an entry for an hour (`BLOCKLIST_FOR`); thousands an hour would grow the map. A delegate's identity reaches a guest only once D18's guest library and the vsock service exist. |
| I187 | **Backfill no longer drowns pings** (pilot incident 2026-10-29, 02-benchmarks.md 2026-10-29): when a rebuilt host rejoined a three-host pilot cluster empty, every primary started a backfill for each of its PGs at once, each with up to 16 pushes in flight, on the one connection per peer (I184) that also carries replication and pings. Pings queued behind hundreds of MiB, peers reported the target silent, and the reports and map changes spread until no node was up and the PGs whose only acting copy was the new node's stopped taking writes. Now pings go on a connection of their own to each peer (`pings`, a second `Pool`), and a reactor's backfills to one target together keep at most 16 MiB of pushes on the wire (`BACKFILL_BUDGET`; one object may go past it): a backfill whose window is full, or that finds the budget spent, waits for its own answers and then for room. `manage::an_empty_node_is_refilled_without_flapping` reproduces the incident on one host. | A ping measures whether the peer answers, not how much data is on its way to it. The budget bounds what a replicated write waits behind, which pings no longer show; a cap on PG backfills did too but slowed delta refills, leaving PGs at one copy longer. | The budget is per reactor and target, not per link: two reactors refilling one host may queue 32 MiB. 16 MiB is about 140 ms of a 1 GbE link ahead of each write during a refill; not measured on one. Open: whether the budget should follow the link's speed. |
| I188 | **A returning node no longer flaps on a large table** (pilot, 2026-10-30, 02-benchmarks.md 2026-10-30): with I187 deployed, a node that returned after hours still went down and up three times in 15 s, and the other two were each marked down for 2 s. A diagnostic build found one object, a `.meta` audit day from before parts (I185) with a key–value table of 660,234 entries (215 MB), on the devices that were slow. (1) Opening such a device took 1.4–4.4 s, while the node's other device opened in under 100 ms and its heartbeats brought the node up; peers pinged the device still opening and reported the node silent. Now each device's reactor counts itself open (or failed) and the node heartbeats once none is still opening; and `Shard::open` copies the checkpoint's index only when there is a log to replay, which may need it again (1.42 s to 0.67 s on a copy of a pilot device). (2) Its digest, taken at once, held a reactor for 290 ms (270–460 ms logged on the pilot, past the 300 ms peer timeout when the reactor was busy otherwise); every node digested it after a restart or when backfilling to a returning node. Now `Shard::digest_begin` and `digest_part` take it in parts of 1 MiB with a yield between (216 parts, the longest 1.5 ms), the same FNV digest, so nodes of both versions agree; a change between parts begins it again, and after two restarts it is taken at once. (3) A node whose own reactor was held reported its peers, whose answers were waiting for it to read them, so two held primaries got a healthy node marked down. Now the peer watcher, finding its 100 ms tick late by 100 ms or more, counts every peer's silence afresh. Diagnostics: each device logs its open time, each reactor hold of 100 ms or more (`hyperstor_reactor_stalls_total`), commits and in-place writes that took that long, and each peer it reports. Tests: `a_node_is_up_only_once_every_device_is_open` (one device of a restarted node opens slowly: the node stays down until it is open, then stays up; fails without the wait); `held_nodes_report_no_healthy_peer` (two nodes' devices hold their reactors 600 ms per I/O while the third returns to be refilled: it is never marked down again; fails without the fresh count, which a held node alone with connections already open did not show); `a_large_digest_comes_in_parts_and_agrees` (parts of 1 byte to 1 MiB give the digest taken at once, across attributes and table, cached once done; a change between parts asks to begin again). | A node is up when it can answer for all its devices; a node that couldn't read its peers' answers has no evidence against them. The digest format stays, so a rolling upgrade needs nothing. | The audit day stays until retention (365 days) drops it; whether to split old one-object days into parts is open. Opening a device with a large index still takes the reactor for seconds before it serves, now with the node down meanwhile. A held node may report a peer that died during its hold one hold later. Peers ping one device per node, so a hung device (not a slow open) still gets its whole node reported. |
| I189 | **The PG grid as built** (D154): `health::pg_states` is the one place a PG's state is decided; the counts in `GetStatus` and the grid both use it, so they can't disagree. `GetPgGrid` returns `PgState` values (1 clean, 2 degraded, 3 backfilling, 4 inactive) in PG order, PG n at byte n, from the member's published map, and nothing but the epoch when the caller's `since_epoch` equals it. `ListPgs` takes an optional `pg` (`hyperstorctl pgs --pg N`; an unknown PG is `not_found`). In the UI, `PgGrid.tsx` polls every 2 s and draws on a canvas, about three cells wide for each one tall, each cell 2 to 16 px, outlining the picked PG. Tests: `api::tests::pg_grid_follows_the_epoch` (states, the unchanged answer, a PG below `min_size`, agreement with the counts), `manage::status_and_tables` and `manage::out_and_in_settle` (`pg-grid` all clean, then all degraded with a host out) and the browser test `the pages show the cluster` (Overview's counts, picking PG 1.0, listing all). | A byte per PG instead of two bits leaves room for more states (scrubbing, a primary on a slow device) without a new call; base64 in Connect's JSON adds a third, still about 87 KiB at 65,536 PGs. The states come from the published map, as `GetStatus`'s do, so the grid shows what clients see. | 19-administration.md §23.7 asks for a state map by pool and failure domain; this grid is by pool only. Grouping cells by host or rack, and a grid of more than 65,536 PGs (which would scroll), are left for later. |
| I190 | **People's audit records listed by time again** (pilot, 2026-10-30): `hyperstorctl audit list --since 30d` showed 5 records while the day objects held more; without `--since`, 22 records listed as 1970-01-01, every one from hyperstor-mgmt (logins, role changes, drains through the UI). mgmt wrote `time` as a JSON string (`"1793311482.737"`); the map service's `append` read either form, so the records were stored under the right key, but the read side (`matches`, which filters by `--since`, and the API's `time_ms`) read only a number, so they had time 0. Now `audit_log::time_of` reads both, everywhere; mgmt writes a number, as the map service does. Tests: `a_time_written_as_a_string_is_read` (a string time is read, kept and dropped by `since` as a number would be, and appended; fails without the fix); `every_change_lands_in_the_audit_log` now lists mgmt's records with `--since 1h` and checks none is dated 1970 (fails with the read side and mgmt's string as before: "No audit records"). | Records already stored keep their string times, so the read side has to take both; writing numbers keeps new records one form. | Other readers of journald's audit lines (outside hyperstor) may have taken the string form from mgmt. |
| I191 | **PG usage as built** (D155): `Shard::pg_blocks` sums the blocks of at most 4,096 objects of a PG from a key on, and the daemon's `usage` task calls it a slice at a time, yielding between slices, so I/O never waits behind a whole PG. `PgUsage { device, full, pgs }` carries `(pool, pg, bytes)` for the PGs changed since the last report, and `PgUsageOk { known }` how many PGs the leader holds from the device; another number than the device's own (a new leader, a PG gone to another primary, a failed report) makes the next report full, and a full report drops the device's PGs it leaves out. The leader's version starts at the wall clock's nanoseconds so it never repeats a former leader's. `GetPgGrid` asks the reactor (`Admin::PgUsage`) only when `since_usage` is set; on a member that doesn't lead it is `unavailable` with reason `not_leader`, which hyperstor-mgmt's proxy takes as a cue to try the next member, and the UI, if no member answers for usage, asks again for the states alone. The levels were first a log scale from zero; on the pilot every PG came out at 235 to 255, so they are now a linear share of the fullest PG (point 94). `HS_PG_USAGE_MS` shortens the 30 s for tests. Tests: `shard::tests::a_pg_is_summed_in_slices`, `tests::usage_reports_follow_the_primaries`, `api::tests::usage_levels_are_a_share_of_the_fullest`, `usage::the_grid_shows_where_data_is` and the browser test `the pages show the cluster` (the legend once reports are in). | A periodic sum, not a counter kept with every write: the index changes in many places (commits, replay, checkpoint load), and a counter that drifted would be wrong until restart; a sum reads each object's block map once every 30 s, in slices, and needs no I/O. A report is about 16 bytes per changed PG. | The bytes are the primary's copy: for an erasure-coded pool, one fragment, which is the same share for every PG, so levels are unaffected. A PG whose primary moved shows its old bytes until the new primary reports. |
| I192 | **Pool capacity as built** (D156): `Msg::DeviceSpace { device, total_bytes, free_bytes }` (kind 73, no protocol version change) is sent by each device's `space` task every 5 s, apart from the heartbeat loop so that a slow answer never delays a heartbeat, with `ShardStats::data_blocks` and `free_blocks` times the block size; the answer is `Noted`, and a report not taken (no leader yet) is sent again after 500 ms, and a follower answers `NotLeader` like the other leader-only reports. The leader keeps `Meta::space` (device -> (total, free)), cleared on a lead change as heartbeats are; `Admin::Space` reads it for the API. `api::pool_space` fills `Pool.total_bytes` and `free_bytes` (optional proto fields 10 and 11) after `status_for`, choosing devices as `hyperstor-placement`'s `Topology` does. Tests: `api::tests::pool_space_from_device_reports`, `tests::device_space_is_kept_out_of_the_map`, the follower case in `tests::members_redirect_and_a_new_leader_waits_out_old_leases`, the round trip in `hyperstor-proto`, and `manage::status_and_tables` (every pool has its space within 10 s). | A first version sent it from the heartbeat loop; in CI a report sent before the first leader was elected waited a whole period, and an answer slow to come could hold a heartbeat back. Free space is the allocator's, without the checkpoint reserve the engine keeps back, so a nearly full device reports a little more than it will take. | A device whose node is down keeps its last report, so a pool's space can be up to its last report stale; nothing says how old. A follower's `GetStatus` has no space, as it has no heartbeats. The web UI doesn't show the space yet. |
| I193 | **Space in the web UI and per device** (D156): `Device` gains optional `total_bytes` and `free_bytes` (fields 6 and 7), the device's own last report, raw (before any redundancy), filled by `pool_space` from the same `Meta::space`, so only the leader's `GetStatus` has them, like the pools'. `hyperstorctl nodes` adds FREE and TOTAL columns before STATE (scripts read STATE last). In the UI a `Space` bar shows used of total with what is free, on the Pools page (usable), the Nodes page (raw, per device) and under each pool's grid on the Overview; it turns amber from 85 % used and red from 95 %, the Overview's thresholds for its metrics tile, and shows a dash before a report. GetStatus is polled already, so the bars cost no extra calls. Tests: `api::tests::pool_space_from_device_reports` (a device's own report, none before one), `tests::tables_list_every_device_and_pool` (the columns), `manage::status_and_tables` (every device reports) and the browser test `the pages show the cluster` (a device's and a pool's bar). | Raw per device and usable per pool: a device's free bytes say when it fills, which with uneven placement comes before the pool's average does. The thresholds are the ones the Overview already warns at, so the pages agree. | The Overview's "used" tile still comes from the devices' metrics through mgmt (Prometheus), not from these reports; one source for both is for later. A report's age isn't shown (I192). |
| I194 | **A dropped Raft call no longer leaves its answer on the connection** (pilot, 2026-10-30): with one of three members unreachable, the other two had no leader for about 15 minutes; terms went from 521 to 5,232. Each kept connection to the other member always had one answer (188 bytes) waiting unread. `Tcp::request` kept a member's connection in its slot while sending a request and reading the answer; openraft drops a call that times out, and a call dropped after its request was written left the connection with that answer unread, so the next call read it, and every later call the answer to the one before. Now a call takes the connection out of its slot and puts it back only once its answer is read; a dropped call drops its connection, and the next call opens a new one. Restarting one member's map service cleared it on the pilot. Test: `a_dropped_call_leaves_no_answer_behind` (an echoing member answers one request after 200 ms; the call is dropped after 50 ms; the next three calls get their own answers; without the change the next call gets the dropped one's). | A connection is in step with its calls only if each call reads its whole answer; one that might not have is not worth keeping. | A call dropped while its connection is being opened or its request written also drops the connection, as it should. Answers are not tagged with a request number, which would have caught this as an error instead of a wrong answer. |
| I195 | **Two flaky CI tests explained** (hyperstor #85, 2026-10-30): (1) `drains_run_as_operations` failed when two drains started in the same second: `Ops::list` sorted by `started_unix` and then by the random id, so the newer one came second about half the time. An operation now keeps `started_ms` as well (absent, so 0, in records written before) and lists by it, falling back to `started_unix` in milliseconds. (2) `a_returning_node_goes_down_once` asserted that the dead node's peers report it (600 ms) before the map service's own heartbeat timeout (800 ms). On a slow runner both peers' reactors were held about 400 ms, and since I188 a peer whose tick ran late counts silence afresh, so the timeout came first ("no heartbeat for 876 ms"). The node still went down once and was held down 2 s. The test now accepts the timeout as the reason when a peer logged a reactor hold. Tests: `the_same_second_lists_newest_first` (two operations in the same second, their ids in the other order; fails without the change). | Order is what people read in `op list` and the UI, and a second is too coarse for operations started by a script. A held peer has no evidence against a node, so the map service's own timeout is the right reason then. | `an_empty_node_is_refilled_without_flapping` still fails at times on CI: a node is reported silent while writing its filler, before the test's kill, and the flapping warning lasts 10 minutes. Not explained yet. |
| I196 | **Feature flags and formatting as built** (D157): `device::Features` (`compat`, `ro_compat`, `incompat`) in `DeviceInfo` and `DeviceConfig`; `KNOWN_*` are all 0 for now. `device::info` tells three cases apart, by copy: no magic (`Blank`), a version other than 1 or 2 (`EngineError::NewerFormat`), anything else that fails (`Corrupt`); only two blank copies are `NotFormatted`. `device::open` refuses unknown incompatible and read-only-compatible flags with `EngineError::UnsupportedFeatures` before opening a shard. `Shard::open` without a valid superblock now returns `Corrupt` instead of `NotFormatted`: formatting writes every shard before the device superblock, so a device with its superblock and a shard without one is damaged. Nothing sets a flag yet; `DeviceConfig::features` lets tests format a device as a newer build would. Format now writes a shard's first superblock (number 1) in the slot its number names (B), as every later one is: it went to A, where the first checkpoint's superblock (number 2) then overwrote it, so a torn write there left no valid superblock, which read as blank before D157 and as damaged after it. | Before this, `hyperstord` (`ShardState::open`) would have formatted a device after a downgrade across a format change, or after both superblock copies or a shard superblock were damaged, losing its data. Version 1 is still written while no flag is set, so builds before D157 never meet version 2; once a flag is in use, rolling back below the build that introduced it is refused, not destructive, for builds from this one on. | A device can't yet be given a flag after it was formatted (no conversion tool); the first feature that needs one adds it, writing copy B, then A. |
| I197 | **A follower's status asks the leader** (owner's choice, 2026-10-31): what only the leader knows (how long ago each node was last heard from, scrub's unrepaired PGs, flapping nodes, each device's space, I192) goes to a follower's `GetStatus` from the leader: `Msg::LeaderState` (kind 74) over the map protocol, answered by `LeaderStateOk` (kind 75), or `NotLeader` from a member that doesn't lead. The follower keeps the answer 1 s (`LEADER_VIEW_FOR`), waits at most that long for it, and when it gets none (no leader known, the leader silent, a leader without this change) answers from the map alone as before (I172), with its note. `Admin::LeaderView` replaces `Admin::Space`; on the leader it is the member's own state.
