Tests: `tests::a_follower_asks_the_leader_for_its_view` (the view, the second's cache, no leader, `NotLeader`), the protocol round trip, and `join::map_service_members_join_the_first` (a follower's `nodes` shows every device's space and its `status` no note). | The leader-only state is small (a few numbers per node and device) and already in the leader's memory: one round trip on the members' own connection makes every member's answer complete for every client (hyperstorctl, mgmt, glidex) without telling them where the leader's API is, which members don't know (only its map address). The cache bounds the leader's extra work to one answer per follower a second, however many UIs poll. | Before, on the pilot, `hyperstorctl nodes` and `pools` on prism2 (a follower) showed `-` for all space. `GetPgGrid`'s usage (D155) is still the leader's alone: followers answer `unavailable`, which mgmt follows to the leader; the CLI doesn't yet. Seen once: `join::map_service_members_join_the_first` failed right after a release build on a busy host, which wait unknown; 6 runs since passed. |
| I198 | **Peer reports wait for a node's first heartbeat**: the map service ignores `PeerDown` about a node it hasn't had a heartbeat from since it started serving or took the lead. A node heartbeats only once every device is open (I188), and a device still opening answers no pings, so its peers' reports said nothing but that it was starting; the heartbeat timeout still marks down one that never comes up. Test: `tests::reports_about_a_starting_node_wait_for_its_first_heartbeat`. | Found as `manage::an_empty_node_is_refilled_without_flapping` failing on CI from 2026-10-30 (main's runs at 0582539, cf52abd and bcb0a0c): a device took 2.65 s to open, every node was reported silent and marked down once at start, and the test's deliberate kill of node 2 then made two downs, which is flapping (D149), so health stayed warn. Ignoring in the map service, not the daemon, keeps peer reports for a node the leader hears but its peers can't reach once it has started. | A node restarted before it was marked down still has its old heartbeat, so its peers' reports count while it reopens; that is correct (it serves nothing meanwhile) but can mark it down once. |
| I200 | **The refill test's flapping explained** (CI on `main` after #86 and #87, and #88 twice, 2026-10-30): `an_empty_node_is_refilled_without_flapping` (I195's open point) failed at its last check, "health ok", in every case with its own check passing: no node went down or up after the kill but node 2, once. The flaps came earlier, while the 384 MiB filler was written: on CI's runner, with the binary's eight tests each running a 3-node cluster at once, one commit held a reactor for 600–1,007 ms (a blank device took 2.8 s to open), past the 600 ms peer timeout, so peers rightly reported that node; D149 then warns "flapping" for 10 minutes, longer than the test waits. The engine's I/O is synchronous on the reactor (`UringDev` waits for its completions, `flush` included), so a commit holds the reactor for as long as the device takes. Not reproduced on the pilot host, even on two CPUs (8 of 8 passed, 19 s; CI takes 195 s): its NVMe flushes fast. Separately, I198 (#93) stopped peers reporting a node whose devices are still opening at start, another source of the same early flaps. The test now waits, before the kill, for every node to be up with no warning but flapping, and ends on the same condition. | The test checks the refill; a node held past the peer timeout while the filler was written is correctly marked down, and the assertion on the map service's log still fails on any flap after the kill. | A slow device still holds its reactor for a whole commit, pings included; taking engine I/O off the reactor (submit, then wait for completions as other work runs) would remove it, and is larger than this fix. |
| I201 | **Device weights as built** (D158): the leader's `Meta::auto_weights`, run on each `DeviceSpace` report, sets a device's weight to its data region in TiB when it differs by more than 1 %, except for devices in the map's new `fixed_weights` set. Changes for devices already weighted wait until every device that is in and on an up node has reported, then go in one map change, so a cluster upgraded from weight 1 everywhere, or any devices of one size, move no data; a device at weight 0 is weighted at once. `Meta::add_node` gives a joining host's devices weight 0, so placement skips it until its first report, sent as its reactor starts. `SetDeviceWeight` (`hyperstorctl weight --device D --weight W\|auto`, role `operate` in mgmt, audited) sets a weight and adds the device to `fixed_weights`, or removes it and takes the capacity last reported. `fixed_weights` makes the map format 4, written only while it isn't empty, as formats 2 and 3 were added; `Device.weight_fixed` (field 8) shows it, and `hyperstorctl nodes` marks such weights with `*`. | The map, not the replicated settings, holds the set: placement and its readers already take everything from the map, and the leader needs it on each report. Waiting for every live device keeps a rolling upgrade from moving PGs device by device between the old weights and the new. Weight 0 for a new host replaces a guess (I84's 1) that would have placed PGs on it and moved them back seconds later. | Format 4 is unreadable to builds before it: setting a weight needs every storage node and map service member on this build. Devices whose builds predate D156 never report, so the others wait and keep their weights. |
| I202 | **A pool's space under its domain rule as built** (D159): `api::pool_space` sums each failure domain's devices (`Domain::Device`, `Host` by node, `Rack` by the node's rack), then `per_shard` finds the largest `y` with `sum(min(b, y)) >= n * y` by capping the largest domains in turn, `n` being the copies or `k + m`; the pool's total and free are `y` (times `k` for EC), each from the domains' total and free bytes. Fewer domains than `n` count one shard each, as placement places them. | Exact for the domain rule with a sort and one pass; free bytes go through the same rule, since a domain that can't fill can't use its free bytes either. | Within a domain nothing is limited, as placement adds nothing there: a host whose devices differ fills them by weight. |
| I203 | **A device's identity is checked before it is opened**: `hyperstord` connects to the map service first and takes the map's `cluster_id`, then reads the superblock alone (`device_info`, before the shard is opened and its log replayed) and checks `DeviceInfo::belongs_to(me, cluster)`: the device id must be the reactor's (`node × 1000 + shard`), and the cluster the map's unless either is 0. A mismatch is `EngineError::NotThisDevice`, naming what the device holds; the reactor fails and the node exits. A blank device is formatted with the map's cluster (it was 0 before); a superblock with cluster 0, from builds before this, gets the cluster recorded by `record_cluster` (copy 1, flush, copy 0, flush; reading takes the first valid copy, so a crash leaves the old or the new). Tests: `device::tests::a_device_belongs_to_its_device_and_cluster`, `manage::a_swapped_device_is_refused` (nodes 1 and 2 with their device files swapped: node 1 refuses node 2's device, writes nothing, and both serve again once swapped back). | The superblock recorded the device but nothing read it, and the cluster was always 0: a drive moved from another host or cluster, or two swapped paths, was opened, replayed and served as this device's data. Checking from the superblock alone keeps a refused device unwritten; connecting first costs nothing, as a node can't serve without the map. | A wiped drive is formatted afresh; there is no `adopt` command for a drive deliberately moved with its data, which its PGs' backfill makes unnecessary. |
| I204 | **Devices added to running nodes, stage 1a** (D167; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.2.1; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): `ClusterMap::add_device` appends a device as the node's next shard (`node × 1000 + shard`, positional until explicit ids, D169), at weight 0, and counts it in the node's `reactors`; `Meta::add_device` checks the node, a free address and the class (1 to 32 of `a-z`, `0-9`, `-`, defaulting to the node's other devices' class), and recomputes; nothing moves until the device reports its capacity, when D158's rule weights it at once. The cluster API's `AddDevice` (role `operate`, audited; `not_found`, `device_conflict`, `invalid_argument`) and `hyperstorctl device add --node N --address A [--class C]`. A join with a node's addresses in order followed by new ones now appends them (it was refused); other overlaps still are. `merge_new`, which adds what the cluster file has and the map lacks when a member takes the lead, gave such devices weight 1 and left the node's count: they now start at weight 0 as joined ones do. Tests: `a_device_is_appended_to_its_node_at_weight_0`, `a_device_is_added_to_a_node_and_weighted_once_it_reports`, the join test (an append, and the overlaps still refused), and end to end `manage::a_device_is_added_to_a_running_node` (registered at weight 0 with nothing placed; node 0 restarted with one more `--shard`; weighted, PGs on it, health ok, data written before reads back; refusals; audited). | The map service is the record of devices (D72); weight 0 until a report keeps placement from guessing (D158). | The operator restarts the node with the new `--shard` by hand, and the installer rewrites `node-N.env` on its next run (1d gives it `--device`). Device ids are still positional (1b). `hyperstord` still formats any blank device it is given (1c). |
| I205 | **Device ids named, and a shard the map must have, stage 1b** (D169; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.2.3): `hyperstord --shard [ID@]ADDR=PATH:SIZE` and the cluster file's `[ID@]ADDR` name a device's id; without `ID@`, the position gives it as before. `hyperstor_map::device_ids` is the one rule for both (and the installer): a named id must be one of the node's (`N × 1000` to `N × 1000 + 999`), none twice. `ClusterMap::add_node_ids` takes ids with the addresses; `add_device` now gives the id after the node's highest, not `N × 1000 +` the next position. New: before it opens its device, a reactor checks that the map has its device on its node at its address, asking again every 200 ms for 5 s (a device just added is published once fenced), and otherwise stops the node saying which (`not in the map: add it first`, or `the map has it on node M at ADDR`); a `--shard` the map lacked used to run unseen. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::device_ids_are_named_or_positional`, `hyperstor_install::tests::named_device_ids_reach_the_node_options`, end to end `manage::a_shard_must_be_the_maps_and_can_name_its_id` (a device the map lacks stops the node and its file gets no superblock; device 2 at device 1's address stops it; device 2 by its id, without device 1, is served and weighted). | Ids that don't move when a device before them goes (D169), and a node that can't serve a device nobody placed. | Removing a device (stage 4) must keep its id taken: `add_device` takes the highest id plus one, so removing the highest would let it come back. |
| I206 | **The format rule, stage 1c** (D168; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.2.2): `hyperstor_engine::probe::signature` reads a device's first and last 128 KiB and names what it holds, from the formats' published layouts: Ceph BlueStore, an LVM label (`LABELONE` and `LVM2 001` in one of the first four sectors), LUKS 1 and 2, md RAID superblocks 1.1 and 1.2 at the head and 0.90 and 1.0 near the end, GPT (and its backup header in the last sector), XFS, ext2/3/4, btrfs, NTFS, swap, and an MBR or FAT boot sector; and a hyperstor device, with its ids, or one this build can't read. `hyperstord` formats only files and memory: a blank block device stops the node (`a blank block device; hyperstord formats files only`). `hyperstor-install format-device --path P --device D [--force-format]` formats a block device after the probe finds nothing; forced, it zeroes the first and last 128 KiB first, so no old signature is left for other tools; the cluster is the installed cluster file's, else 0, which the node replaces with the map's at its first start (I203). Tests: `hyperstor_engine::probe::tests::foreign_signatures_are_named_and_blank_devices_pass` (each signature, at each end), `hyperstor_engine::probe::tests::a_hyperstor_device_is_named_with_its_ids`, `hyperstor_daemon::tests::only_files_are_formatted_by_the_node`, `hyperstor_install::tests::format_device_refuses_foreign_signatures_unless_forced`. | A disk of another system has no hyperstor magic, and NVMe names can change at boot: formatting a disk is the operator's step, checked. | Signatures, not proof of emptiness: a disk with data and no known signature (the pilot's nvme6n1 reports nearly every block written) passes; ZFS and some others aren't checked. Not tested on a real block device or against `blkid` (G44's host). The installer's `--device` (1d) will call this. |
| I207 | **The installer adds devices, stage 1d** (D167, D168; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.2.1): `hyperstor-install install --node N --device PATH [--device-address IP:PORT] [--force-format]`, repeatable. For each device not yet recorded: a block device must be named by `/dev/disk/by-id/`; it is probed (I206) before anything changes, so a refusal leaves the map as it was; its address is `--device-address`, else the next port after the node's own that no other service of the install uses; it is registered by running the `hyperstorctl` beside the installer (`device add`, through this member's API with the administrator's certificate, or the leader's when the answer names it; an address already registered, from a run that stopped before saving, gives its device back); formatted with that id (a device already formatted as it is left alone); handed to the service user (`chown` now, and `/etc/udev/rules.d/60-hyperstor.rules`, matching the disk's by-id link, for later boots, reloaded when it changes); and recorded in `install.conf` as `devices = ID@ADDR=PATH`, which every run turns into a `--shard ... =PATH:0` after the node's own, so the node restarts with it and the daily renewal keeps it. `install.conf` gets the `devices` line only once there is one. `format-device` now takes `--id D` (it was `--device D`), so `--device` always names a path. Tests: `hyperstor_install::tests::added_devices_become_shards_and_udev_rules`, end to end `manage::the_installer_adds_a_device_to_a_node` (an LVM image refused with nothing registered; a blank disk added as device 1, in install.conf and node-0.env; a second run keeps it and adds nothing; node 0, restarted from its options, serves it: weighted, PGs on it, health ok). | The installer owns `node-N.env`, so a device lives in its settings or not at all; registering through `hyperstorctl` keeps one client of the API. | Only on hosts with the administrator's certificate (map service members, as all the pilot's hosts are): a storage-only host would join again with a token (D167), not built. Classes (`--class`) wait for stage 2. Not run against a real disk or udev yet (1e). |
| I208 | **Stray copies deleted** (D177): `Shard::held_pgs` lists the PGs a shard holds objects of, one index step per PG. Each reactor's `strays` task, every quarter of the grace (50 ms to 10 s), checks each held PG with `is_stray`: the map has the PG, this device is in neither `up` nor `acting`, and `acting` is `up` with every member live. A PG that has been a stray at one PG epoch for the grace (`STRAY_GRACE`, 60 s; `NodeConfig::stray_grace` for tests) is dropped by `drop_copy`: its keys deleted through the commit queue, 64 at a time with a yield between, stopping if the PG is no longer a stray at that epoch. Logged: `device D: deleted its copy of PG P.G (N objects), which devices [...] serve`. Not while the node is cut off from the map service or its device failed. A PG the map lacks (a pool deleted, PGs split: later stages of 24-pools-and-devices.md) is left alone. Tests: `hyperstor_engine::shard::tests::held_pgs_are_listed_once_each`, `hyperstor_daemon::tests::a_copy_is_dropped_only_when_its_pg_is_clean_elsewhere` (off both sets and clean; a member; a PG the map lacks; backfilling; a member down), `cluster::a_device_deletes_the_copies_it_no_longer_serves` (device 3000 out: after the backfill and the grace it holds no PG it left, the data reads back and the other copies agree; without the task it kept PGs 1.4, 1.5 and 1.15). | Space comes back after every rebalance; nothing a recovery could need is deleted. | The pilot's node 1 keeps its old copies until this is installed there, then drops them within a minute. Deleting a large PG commits many lists, under the commit queue's usual batching; not measured on the pilot. |

The leader keeps reports in memory until every device of a node that is up has one, then applies them all (a node from before can't read format 5); a new leader collects them again. `SetDeviceClass` in the cluster API (role `operate`, audited; `not_found`, `class_conflict`, `invalid_argument`) and `hyperstorctl device class --device D --class C\|auto`; `GetStatus` gives each device's `kind` and `class_fixed`; `hyperstorctl nodes` has a CLASS column, `+` for a class set. `AddDevice` with a class marks it set. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::kinds_become_classes_unless_an_operator_set_one`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::kinds_are_applied_once_every_live_device_has_reported`, the kind in `hyperstor_daemon::tests::only_files_are_formatted_by_the_node` (a file; this host's NVMe namespaces), end to end `manage::devices_report_their_kind_and_classes_follow_it`. | Classes say what devices are (D178); a map nodes can read until every node can read more. | A node from before that returns after kinds are in the map can't read it: upgrade it before it starts. Class sets in pools are stage 2b. |
| I210 | **Class sets, stage 2b** (D164, D172, D179; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.3.2, §27.4; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): `PoolInfo::classes` and `hyperstor_placement::Rule::classes` (empty: every device) replace the single class; placement takes a device whose class is in the set. A pool with one class keeps it in the field formats before 6 read; two or more need format 6, written only then, which nodes from before can't read (set them once every node runs this build). The cluster file takes `class a,b`. `Rule::no_hdd` keeps `.meta` off devices of kind `hdd`. `SetPool` in the cluster API (classes only, for now; group `store`, audited; `not_found`, `pool_conflict`, `invalid_argument`) and `hyperstorctl pool set --pool P --classes C,C...\|any [--force]`: refused for `.meta`, when the set's devices that are in span fewer failure domains than the pool's copies, and when its classes' kinds include `hdd` and `nvme` or `ssd`, unless forced or a cache pool; `file`, and classes no device of which has reported a kind, count as neither (D179). `GetStatus` gives each pool's `classes` and `mixed_kinds`; `hyperstorctl pools` and `status` end each pool's line with its classes (`any`, `!` for a forced mix). Pool space follows the set. Tests: `hyperstor_placement::tests::class_sets_and_no_hdd`, `hyperstor_map::tests::class_sets_round_trip_and_need_format_6_only_when_several`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::pool_class_sets_are_checked_and_placed_on`, end to end `manage::a_pool_places_on_its_classes_only`. | Pools choose their media (D164) without new pools yet (stage 3). | Relabelling a device can still make a pool's set mix kinds, or leave it too few domains: only `SetPool` checks. | I215 | **A forced pool delete** (D176; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.3.4; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): `DeletePool` with `force` starts an operation, `Work::DeletePool { pool }` (kind `delete-pool`), unless the pool holds nothing. It lists the pool's volumes with the trash; moves each that isn't trashed to the trash with `force` (its holder preempted, D80) and purges it with its snapshots, each audited (`DeleteVolume`, `PurgeFromTrash`, target `volume V (pool P deleted)`); passes again while any purge succeeds, so a clone goes before what it was cloned from; fails when a pass purges nothing (a clone in another pool depending on a snapshot here, say). Then it deletes the pool; the end is audited under `DeletePool`. Resumed, it lists what is left, and a pool already gone counts as done. Cancelling stops it between passes. `hyperstorctl pool delete --pool P --force [--wait SECS]` prints the volumes and the operation, and follows it with `--wait`. Tests: `hyperstor_meta::operations::tests` (the row survives encoding), end to end `manage::a_forced_pool_delete_takes_its_volumes_with_it` (a volume, its snapshot and clone, and a volume in the trash; all audited; the pool gone). | Tearing down a test cluster or a tenant's pool shouldn't need a call per volume (D176). | Nothing stops a volume being made in the pool while the operation runs; it is deleted with the rest if listed before the pool goes, and lost otherwise. |
| I216 | **Fullness thresholds, step 1** (D27, D182; branch `feat/fullness`): `ClusterMap::full_ratios` (thousandths, defaults 850/900/950, `FullRatios::check` for 50–99 % in order) and `ClusterMap::backfillfull`, the devices past it, are kept in map format 12, written only when the ratios aren't the defaults or a device is past `backfillfull`. (Built as format 9; renumbered when `feature/elastic-pools` merged, because its formats 9–11, removed ids, splits and cache generations, already ran on the pilot.) `SetFullRatios` in the cluster API (audited; operate role through mgmt) and `hyperstorctl full-ratios` set one or more; `GetStatus` gives them. Each commit round sets the shard's `full` ratio from the map; `Shard::plan` refuses with `NoSpace` a list that takes room (a new object, or a write to blocks the object doesn't hold, `Object::all_mapped`) when the used blocks plus the list's would pass it. An existing object's keys and attributes don't take room: leases and volume headers are kept so, and refusing them would stop overwrites. The leader marks a device in `backfillfull` from its `DeviceSpace` report when its used space passes the ratio and clears it a percent below, so a device at the line doesn't change the map with each report; primaries start no backfill to a marked device (a running one goes on). Not yet: pool states, health reasons and audit events (step 2), refusing new volumes on a full pool, and D183 in `hyperstor-vhost`. |
| I217 | **Fullness thresholds, step 2** (D27; branch `feat/fullness-states`): `hyperstor_meta::fullness` gives each reported device a level (`ok`, `nearfull`, `backfillfull`, `full`) against the map's ratios, and each pool the fullest of the devices placement may use for it (`api::pool_devices`, shared with D159's space), `backfillfull` counting as `nearfull`, unknown until every one has reported. `GetStatus` gives each device's and pool's `fullness` and a pool's `fullest_device`, and a health reason per device past a threshold (warn for `nearfull` and `backfillfull`, error for `full`); `hyperstorctl pools` and `nodes` and the web UI's Pools page show them. `CreateVolume`, `CloneVolume`, `CreateSnapshot` and `CreateGroupSnapshot` in a full pool are refused `resource_exhausted` (`pool_full`), from the leader's view, so a follower refuses too; with no leader to ask they go on. The leader audits each device's change as `DeviceFullness` by principal `hyperstor-meta`, from its reports and at once when the ratios change (a change lasting less than a report period was missed before the latter); changing the ratios re-marks `backfillfull` devices at once too. `.meta` shares the devices, so it is full with them: its existing objects' keys still change (I216), so audit records and leases go on. Not yet: D115's purge of a full pool's trash, D183 in `hyperstor-vhost`, and glidex refusing new disks on a full datastore. |
| I223 | **Peers ping each other over UDP, as built** (D185). (1) The reactor: `net::UdpSocket` binds (waiting up to 5 s while the address is in use, as `bind_retrying` does for TCP), waits on the ring until the socket is readable and then receives without blocking, and sends at once; a send that would block fails, as a lost datagram would. (2) Each device binds a UDP socket at its TCP listener's address; if that fails it logs it and its peers ping it over TCP. A task answers each 12-byte ping (`HSP?` and an 8-byte nonce) with `HSP!` and the same nonce, and records, for each of this device's own pings answered within 200 ms, when its peer answered; a pong whose nonce is not in flight is ignored. (3) `peer_watch` sends each peer a UDP ping with a fresh nonce (`random_u64`) every tick, unless the partitions test hook cuts them; it takes UDP answers newer than a peer's last into account at the next tick, so an answer from before a peer left changes nothing. It also pings a peer over TCP, as before, until the peer first answers over UDP, and logs once a peer that answers over TCP and not over UDP for 5 s. (4) `hyperstor-install check` lists a storage port in use for UDP as `PORT/udp`. The fault rig's `Pings` loses TCP pings to a node, or starts a node without UDP. Tests: `lost_tcp_pings_report_no_one` (every TCP ping to node 2 lost for 3 s: it is never marked down; ignoring UDP answers fails it); `a_peer_without_udp_is_pinged_over_tcp` (node 2 restarted without UDP stays up for 6 s, and when killed is reported by its peers within 2 s, before the map service's 3 s; without the fallback it fails); `a_node_cut_off_from_its_peers_is_reported` (a partition cuts UDP pings too: node 2 cut from both peers is marked down within 2 s; UDP pings that ignore the hook fail it); `net::udp_tests::datagrams_go_both_ways`. | A UDP ping that is lost costs its own 100 ms slot and nothing else. The socket shares the device's port number, so nothing new goes in the map or the options; the TCP fallback needs no coordination during an upgrade. | Not measured on the pilot yet: whether single silence reports under 1 MiB load stop (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-01). A firewall that drops UDP is logged per peer, not checked by the installer between hosts. A peer that answered over UDP once and then stops (a firewall closed later) is reported silent, not fallen back to TCP. |
| I225 | **A full pool purges its oldest trash** (D115, D27; 19-administration.md §23.9.3; branch `feat/trash-on-full`): the leader's upkeep loop, every 2 s, takes the pools past `full` (`api::full_pools`, shared with the refusals of I217) whose trash is set to `purge`, and for each starts a purge operation (`map-service/purger`, as D54's purger does) of the oldest item in its trash by `trashed_unix`, a volume or a snapshot alike. One at a time per pool: none while one of the pool's purges runs, and none for 10 s after (two device reports, D156), so the space the last one freed is counted before the next is chosen; an item whose purge failed in the last hour is passed over, as the grace purger does. The leader logs each choice with the pool's reason. `keep` (the default) does nothing; the pool stays full. `hyperstorctl trash settings` says "when full, the pool keeps it" or "purges the oldest first". Found while testing: trash times are whole seconds, so two items trashed in the same second tie and the lower volume id goes first; the test trashes them 2 s apart. Test: `manage::a_full_pool_purges_its_oldest_trash` (kept with `keep`; with `purge`, only the older of two is purged, which takes the pool below `full`). | Purging one item at a time, and only once the last one's space is counted, purges no more than needed: the trash holds what an operator may still want. A 2 s check costs one trash list only while a pool set to `purge` is full. | A tie on the second could purge a newer item first; a finer trash time would change the header format for little gain. |
| I228 | **`hyperstor-vhost` holds a write refused for space** (D183; branch `feat/vhost-nospace`): `device::held` runs a write (or a write-zeroes piece) and, when it fails with `Code::NoSpace`, tries it again every second until `--hold-full` (default 300 s since G46, 60 s before; 0–3600, 0 answering at once) has passed, then answers `IOERR`. Each try reads the data from guest memory again, which stays the guest's until the request completes, so a write that succeeds at once copies nothing more; the block locks (D126) are taken per try, not while waiting. The first write held on a volume and the last let go are logged, and each held write that fails, past the hold or for another reason (with the error). Test: `vhost::a_write_refused_for_space_is_held` (an overwrite isn't held; a new block held until the ratios are raised after 3 s, then written; with `--hold-full 2`, `IOERR` after 2 s). | virtio-blk has no "no space" status (D183); re-reading guest memory keeps the hot path free of a copy. | G46 measured Linux 6.8 under both hypervisors (02-benchmarks.md); the newest kernel and Windows are still to check. A held write lets a later overlapping write from the guest land first; guests don't issue overlapping writes without waiting for the first, as with any disk. |
| I224 | **Devices removed, stage 4a** (D175, D186; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.2.5; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): `RemoveDevice` (operate group, with step-up) works out the removal on a copy of the map: a pool using the device that would span fewer failure domains than before and than its copies is refused below its `min`, and without `force` below its size; a device whose PGs' copies (the primaries' usage reports, D155; a 1/k share for EC) would take it past 95% of its reported size (`FULL`, D27's threshold, not yet a pool setting) is refused, forced or not. `dry_run` returns the moves, the short pools and each receiving device's space after. Otherwise the device is taken out and an operation, `Work::RemoveDevice { device, pgs }` (kind `remove-device`), follows its PGs as a drain does (the follower is now shared, `follow_out`); once no PG lists the device and it is still out, `FinishRemoval` removes it from the map (`ClusterMap::remove_device`), with its node if it was the last, keeping both ids (`removed_devices`, `removed_nodes`, map format 9); `add_device` and `next_node_id` skip them. A PG whose `up` shrank because no other domain is left (forced) never backfills, so its `acting` would keep the device for good: `ClusterMap::release` sets such PGs' `acting` to `up` when the device goes out, and again at every check of the operation's progress (`Admin::Release`), since a later map change (a node that flaps, a weight) can leave one so. And `recompute` now gives a replicated PG whose acting set holds `up`'s devices in another order `up` itself: nothing being left to backfill, nothing else would, and a removal or drain waiting for `acting` = `up` waited for ever (CI found it: a node restarted slowly enough to be marked down left 45 PGs so; reproduced by holding the node down 3 s; `hyperstor_map::tests::acting_takes_ups_order_when_it_holds_the_same_copies`). The operation ends once the published map, which clients and the installer read, lacks the device: in CI the installer's check right after `device remove --wait` still found it. The removal tests wait for every PG clean (`Cluster::settled`), not for health `ok`, which such a restart turns to a flapping warning for ten minutes. `CancelOperation` brings the device back in, unless it has left the map. On the node, a device the map lacks deletes nothing as stray (`is_stray`), and a `--shard` for a removed device stops the node saying so. `hyperstorctl device remove --device D [--dry-run] [--force] [--wait SECS]`. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::removed_devices_and_nodes_keep_their_ids`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::devices_are_removed_once_no_pg_lists_them`, `hyperstor_meta::operations::tests` (the row), the daemon's stray test, end to end `manage::a_device_is_removed_once_its_pgs_have_moved` (data reads back after each removal; the stale `--shard` stops the node; a forced removal takes node 2 with it; ids not given again; audited). | Retiring hardware shouldn't need a new cluster (D163). | A drain on a cluster with no spare failure domain never finishes, for the same reason `release` exists; drains don't call it yet. The space check counts what moves now, not what a pool will grow by. |

With the administrator's certificate it reads the map (`hyperstorctl nodes --json`) and refuses a device the map still has; without it, it says it couldn't check. A node's last device is refused: the node left the map with it. Tests: `hyperstor_install::tests::removed_devices_get_no_shard`, end to end `manage::the_installer_drops_a_removed_device` (refused while in the map and for a node's last device; dropped after `device remove`; a later run keeps it out; the node runs without it). | An installer run, the daily renewal's included, must not put back a device the map has removed: the node would refuse to start. The record names `src`, not what it holds, so two things keep recovery exact. A commit round whose lists hold a `Take` first finishes a fuzzy checkpoint in progress: replay then starts before the `Take`, over pages that are all older, or after it; replayed over pages written on either side of it, it couldn't tell which state they hold. And the check that a group's data blocks reached the device (`first_unwritten`) follows a `Take`: a block mapped in the group under `src` is checked under `dst`. Both were found by the randomized crash test, which now mixes `Take` into its lists (two seeds failed before the fixes; 5,000 of each kind pass after, 60,000 crashes). `hyperstor_placement::key_pg(ns, name, count)`: a volume chunk's key (namespace 1, a 16-byte lineage and an 8-byte chunk, then maybe a branch) by its lineage and chunk, any other by its name (`object_pg`, moved from the client); the client now routes by the key it sends, so a device splitting by keys and clients agree. `hyperstor_daemon::split_lists(shard, pool, pg, count)`: a `Take` to the sibling's key for each object of the PG that belongs to it under the doubled count; empty once split, so a split cut short is finished by asking again. Tests: `hyperstor-engine` `take_moves_an_object_whole` and the crash tests, `hyperstor_placement::tests::keys_route_as_chunks_or_names_and_split_in_two`, `hyperstor_daemon::tests::a_pg_splits_into_itself_and_its_sibling`. | Splitting moves only metadata, as D173 wants; a checkpoint finished early when a split begins is cheap beside copying tables. | Nothing asks a device to split yet (5b). A commit holding a `Take` waits for the rest of a checkpoint in progress. Engines from before can't replay a log holding a `Take`; a node is upgraded before it splits, which 5b's protocol ensures. |
| I219 | **PG splits, stage 5b: the protocol and `pool set --pgs`** (D173, D187; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.5; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): a pool gets `placement` (PG `p` is placed as `p % placement`) and `split` (PGs `marked` now, `done`), in map format 10, written only while a pool splits or places by fewer PGs than it has. `SetPool` with `pg_count`, twice the pool's (not `.meta`, not while it splits), starts a split. The leader marks the clean PGs (acting = up, every member live) up to `SPLITS_AT_ONCE` (4) per pool; marking moves the PG's epoch. Each device in a marked PG's acting set pauses it (`Unavailable`, which clients retry), waits for its requests in flight, commits `split_lists` and reports `PgSplit` (message 77) at the PG's epoch, again if the epoch moves, and keeps it paused until the map stops marking it. The leader flips a PG once every member of its acting set has reported at its epoch (an older report is ignored): the sibling `pg + pg_count` joins the map with the PG's acting and up sets, both at a new epoch; the last flip doubles `pg_count` and ends the split, `placement` staying, so nothing moves (5c moves it). A marked PG's acting set only shrinks: `BackfillDone` is refused for it, since a member joining would hold the PG without what moved to its sibling; backfill tries again after the flip. Clients route by `PoolInfo::pg_of_key`: under the doubled count, but to the parent of a PG not flipped. A device catches up, at start and on each flip, by running `split_lists` on what it holds by an older count, dropping an object whose new key already holds a newer copy (backfill to a device that missed the split). `hyperstorctl pool set --pool P --pgs N [--wait SECS]`; `pools` shows a split's progress and a placement count below the PG count. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::a_split_flips_pg_by_pg_then_doubles_the_count`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::splits_flip_pgs_once_every_member_reports` (it found that a stale report reset the count of current ones), `hyperstor_daemon::tests::a_pg_splits_into_itself_and_its_sibling`, end to end `manage::a_pool_splits_its_pgs_in_place` (data written before and during the split reads back, also after a node restarts; each new PG on its parent's devices; refusals; audited). | Splits PG by PG as D187 settled; the pauses are bounded by the slowest member of one PG. | A marked PG all of whose members go down waits for them. Splitting `.meta` isn't offered: its count grows with nodes. A device catches up about 100 ms after it starts, not before it opens: one that missed a split isn't in the flipped PG's acting set then (a flip needs every acting member's report), so it serves none of its reads, only backfill, whose newer copies the catch-up keeps. |
| I220 | **PG splits, stage 5c: the new PGs move** (D173; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.5): a PG below the pool's placement count is placed as itself, one at or past it as its parent, `pg - full / 2` (`PoolInfo::placed_as`; `full` the count the pool has once its split ends). Not `pg % placement`, which for a count that isn't a power of two would place most siblings as another PG: raising the count by k moves exactly k PGs. After a split the leader raises it by `SPLITS_AT_ONCE` (4) each time every PG of the pool is clean (acting = up, every member live), from `advance_splits` in the liveness loop, so the moves go a few at a time under the backfill budget (I187); a split is refused until it has reached the PG count. Format 10 stops being written once it has. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::raising_the_placement_count_moves_only_the_pgs_it_passes`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::splits_flip_pgs_once_every_member_reports` (raised only while clean, a split refused until done), end to end `manage::a_pool_splits_its_pgs_in_place` (every PG placed on its own and clean). | The slow part, moving data, is spread and bounded, as D173 wants. | The step and the wait are fixed: no operator setting. |

The installer's trial sizes each pool at the smallest count, at least 64, that `autoscale_wants` leaves (3 nodes: vol 64, cache 256, from 64). The daemon's in-process clusters and the benchmarks turn it off for their pools: their counts are the tests'. Tests: `hyperstor_meta::tests::the_autoscaler_grows_one_pool_at_a_time` (unclean waits, one pool at a time, autoscale off skipped), end to end `manage::the_autoscaler_grows_pools_that_ask_for_it` (a pool of 16 PGs on three devices grows to 64, all placed; one made with `--autoscale off` stays until turned on; the trial's pools at their counts; audited). | Growth is what adding devices needs (D174). | An autoscaled split is logged by the map service, not audited: no person made it. The target counts devices of the pool's classes, so pools sharing devices each grow as if they had them alone. |

Map format 7 holds them, written only while some pool has one, as formats 5 and 6 are. `Meta::set_pool_device(pool, device, member, force)`: adding a device of the pool's classes only takes back its removal, and removing an added device only drops it; each is checked by `check_class_set` (failure domains, hard disks with flash unless forced); `.meta` refused. A pool with no classes is never counted as mixing kinds, as before kinds (D171). `SetPoolDevice` in the cluster API (group `store`, audited; `GetStatus` gives `added_devices`, `removed_devices`), `hyperstorctl pool add-device --pool P --device D [--force]`, `pool remove-device --pool P --device D`; `pools` shows `+D` and `-D` after the classes. Tests: `hyperstor_map::tests::devices_added_and_removed_change_placement_and_need_format_7`, `hyperstor_placement::tests::class_sets_and_no_hdd`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::devices_are_added_to_and_removed_from_a_pool`, end to end `manage::devices_are_added_to_and_removed_from_a_pool`. | One pool can have a device another of its class doesn't, without a class per pool (D180). | An edit isn't dropped when the device's class later changes: a device removed, then moved into a class the pool doesn't name, stays in `removed` until added back, harmlessly. The web UI shows neither classes nor these edits yet. |
| I211 | **Pools made at run time, stage 3a** (D163, D171; 24-pools-and-devices.md §27.3.1; branch `feature/elastic-pools`): `Meta::create_pool` takes a `NewPool` (name, kind `replicated` or `cache`, copies, `min`, PG count, chunk size, domain, classes, `force`): classes are required; a name is 1 to 64 characters without spaces, not starting with a dot, and not another pool's; `min` 1 to the copies; a chunk a multiple of 4096; a PG count a power of two, else `default_pg_count`: 100 per device of its classes per copy, at least 8, to the next power of two (6 devices, 3 copies: 256). Its classes are checked as `SetPool` checks them (`check_class_set`, shared). Its id is `ClusterMap::next_pool_id`, after every pool's. `CreatePool` in the cluster API (group `store`, audited) and `hyperstorctl pool create --name N --classes C,C... [--kind] [--copies] [--min] [--pgs] [--chunk] [--domain] [--force]`. Tests: `hyperstor_meta::tests::pools_are_made_with_their_classes`, end to end `manage::a_pool_is_made_on_its_classes` (a pool on class `file` gets 128 PGs and takes I/O; refusals; audited). | Pools as storage is bought (D163). | Deleted pools' ids aren't kept yet, so `next_pool_id` could give a deleted pool's back: stage 3c keeps them. EC pools aren't served. The PG count can't grow until stage 5. |
| I162 | **A full shard no longer poisons itself through its checkpoint**: a checkpoint that couldn't allocate failed its commit, which poisons the shard, so every volume on it got I/O errors (`ublk::a_volume_is_a_block_device_and_keeps_its_data`, filling a thin volume, failed 1 run in 4 with EIO instead of ENOSPC). Now commits keep room for a whole checkpoint free (every page and the directory, plus a quarter and 8 blocks for the index to grow): a list that would take space is refused (`NoSpace`), one that only frees (delete, truncate, zero, removing keys or attributes, absorb) goes through. A checkpoint that still finds no room is abandoned: its pages are freed, the page bounds are the last checkpoint's again, the pages it was to write are marked again, and the shard refuses what takes space; once the room is free it checkpoints at once, and while stalled a round takes no more log than the ring has left. A full shard opens without its fresh checkpoint, keeping its superblock's salt. | Found investigating the flaky ublk test (HS3's PR, #45). Both layers have tests that fail without them (`engine::a_full_shard_refuses_data_but_keeps_checkpointing`, `shard::tests::a_checkpoint_without_room_is_abandoned`); 3,000 crash seeds pass. | The reserve follows the last checkpoint's size, so a quickly growing index can outrun it; the stall catches that. The reserve is the whole index, about 2 GiB per TiB with dense maps, kept free on each shard. |
| I199 | **Commits wait for the device off the reactor's thread: stage 1 of point 114, a prototype** (05-decision-log.md, 2026-10-31). (1) The engine: `Shard::commit_begin` does a round's checkpoint work, plans its lists, applies them to the index and encodes the log record, and returns a `Round` whose writes the caller takes (`take_writes`); `Shard::commit_end` gets their outcome: the log advances and the extents the round freed return to the allocator, or an error poisons the shard. `Shard::commit` does begin, write, flush and end, so its callers, the crash test and the benchmarks are unchanged. A record that wraps the ring is copied into two buffers. One round is in flight at a time (asserted). (2) The reactor: `file::write_then_sync` submits a round's writes on the reactor's ring at once and, once all have completed, `fdatasync`; the ring keeps the buffers until the last completion (`Resource::Keep`), so dropping the future frees nothing the kernel still uses. (3) `BlockDev::raw_fd` names the descriptor of `FileDev` and `UringDev`; memory and test devices have none and still write in their own calls, holding the reactor as before. (4) The daemon: `ShardCell`. A commit takes the shard, and every other use waits (`get().await`) until it ends, since the index shows a round before its blocks are written; code between an `idle().await` and its next `.await` uses `borrow()`, which panics during a commit, so a missed wait fails in tests instead of serving unwritten blocks. `drained()` waited for an empty queue, which used to mean committed; a commit now leaves the queue when it begins, so it also waits for the one in flight. A round's writes and flush may take `NodeConfig::io_timeout` (30 s, `IO_TIMEOUT`, as point 115 recommends); then the round fails, the shard is poisoned and the device is failed as on an I/O error. The log says apart a commit's time on the reactor ("took") and its wait for the device ("waited N ms for the device"). The fault rig's `CommitDelays` makes a device's commits slow without holding the reactor (D92). Tests: `a_slow_commit_holds_no_reactor` (node 0's commits take 1 s each, past the 300 ms peer timeout and the 700 ms liveness timeout: node 0 is never seen down and the writes read back; with the delay holding the reactor instead, node 0 was seen down 11 times in the same test); `a_hung_device_fails_after_the_io_timeout` (a device whose commits never complete is failed after 800 ms, placed elsewhere, and its node stays up); `readers_wait_for_a_commit_in_flight`; `using_the_shard_without_waiting_panics_during_a_commit`; `writes_land_at_their_offsets`; `errors_are_returned`. `hyperstord` opens file devices, so every end-to-end test now commits through the ring. | The flush is what a slow device makes long, and a reactor held by it misses pings for every PG it serves. One round in flight keeps the order of rounds and the allocator as they were; requests wait for a commit as they did, so nothing reads a round before it is durable. | Points 114 and 115 were settled as recommended (D165, D166), and `hyperstord --io-timeout-secs` sets the timeout. Still on the reactor's thread: checkpoint work in `commit_begin` (a forced checkpoint is the longest), deferred in-place writes and every read (stages 2 and 3); the 1 s commits seen on CI (I195) may have been either, which the split log now tells. Requests to the shard still wait during a flush, so throughput is unchanged until stage 2. Not measured on the pilot (G43). |
| I230 | **Reads wait for the device off the reactor: stage 2 of D165** (D190; 05-decision-log.md points 164–167). (1) The engine: `Shard::read_begin` and `read_layered_begin` plan a read (blocks still deferred in memory are copied now) and return a `ReadPlan`, its device reads as (offset, offset in the buffer, length); `ReadPlan::finish` checks the CRCs; `Shard::read_end` ends it, also when abandoned. Each read notes the generation it began in; `commit_end` frees a round's extents at once when no read is in flight, else holds them under the current generation and starts the next, and `read_end` returns every held generation older than the oldest read still in flight. A write is not deferred in place while a read is in flight (it is redirected), since the read may have planned the old data from the device. `Shard::read` and `read_layered` still read in the caller's call, untracked: nothing else runs on the shard meanwhile. (2) The reactor: `file::read_into` submits a read's runs at once into one buffer, kept by the ring until the last completion (`Resource::Keep`), so a read abandoned on the timeout frees nothing the kernel writes to. (3) The daemon: `device_read` takes one of `ShardCell::MAX_READS` (128) slots, first come first served (`read_slot`), plans with the shard (waiting for a commit in flight), reads on the ring (memory and test devices in their own calls, as before), and ends the read with `ShardCell::end_read`, which works during a commit. `Read` and `ReadAt` are served off the connection's loop, so a connection has many reads in flight (before, each connection had one, and a write behind it waited). Past `NodeConfig::read_timeout` (2 s, `READ_TIMEOUT`; `hyperstord --read-timeout-ms`) a read is abandoned and the primary sends `Msg::ReadCopy` to the PG's other acting members in turn, each answering from its own copy, as `Read` or `ReadAt` would, once the writes queued before it are applied; `Unavailable` (retried by clients) if none answers within the timeout. Three timeouts in a row fail the device; a read in time resets the count. `hyperstor_read_timeouts_total` counts reads abandoned and those a member answered. The fault rig's `CommitDelays::set_reads` makes a device's reads slow without holding the reactor. Tests: `hyperstor_engine::shard::tests::freed_extents_wait_for_reads_in_flight`, `held_extents_follow_the_oldest_read`, `nothing_is_deferred_under_a_read`, `reads_in_flight_against_a_model` (20 seeds of 300 writes, deletes and reads begun and ended in any order, against a model; no space lost once all end; fails without the hold); `hyperstor_reactor::file::tests::reads_land_in_the_buffer`; `hyperstor_daemon::shard_cell::tests::reads_past_the_limit_wait_in_order`; `cluster::a_slow_read_holds_no_reactor` (node 0's reads take 1 s: never seen down; fails with the delay holding the reactor); `cluster::a_read_past_the_timeout_is_served_from_a_member` (one timeout serves the read from a member and fails nothing, the third fails the device, its node stays up; fails without the member's answer); `cluster::reads_in_flight_get_whole_writes` (reads of 50 ms through `ReadAt` while the blocks are overwritten 99 times: each gets one write whole; without the hold, a read gets a checksum error). | A read held its reactor for as long as its device took, which a 1 MiB read on the pilot made 100–153 ms. The hold costs one counter per read and frees space at most a read timeout late. | Not measured on the pilot yet. Still on the reactor's thread: reads for recovery, backfill, scrub and repair (`push_of`, `fetch`, deep scrub), which read whole objects; checkpoint slices and deferred writes (stage 3). Under steady reads on a hard disk, overwrites are redirected instead of deferred. An EC or cache pool's read past the timeout gets `Unavailable` (no member holds a copy). A member from before this build answers `ReadCopy` as unknown, and the primary tries the next. |
| I231 | **Pools, devices and volumes managed in the web UI** (19-administration.md §23.7; asked 2026-11-01): each action sits on the page that shows its object, and shows its `hyperstorctl` command, as the others do. Nodes: add a device (node, address, class), a device's weight (a number, or `auto`) and class (a name, or `auto`), and remove it, after its dry run lists the PGs that move and the space after; a removal refused for leaving a pool short offers a forced plan. Pools: create (name, kind, classes prefilled from the devices', copies, min, PGs, domain, autoscale, a cache pool's grace, force), change (only the fields edited are sent: classes or `any`, name, domain, a split to twice the count, autoscale, grace), the devices a pool uses (D180: used or not, added or removed, toggled with `SetPoolDevice`), and delete after a dry run, which lists the pool's volumes and asks to force when there are any. Volumes: expand, starting at twice the size. Buttons follow the roles as the server's policy does (operate for devices, store for pools and volumes); removing a device and deleting a pool need a login of the last 10 minutes, which the API client asks for. Test: `browser::pools_devices_and_volumes_are_managed`; `browser::someone_without_roles_sees_nothing_to_do` checks the new buttons are hidden. |
| I232 | **A held reactor says where the time went** (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-01: finding 3 of the peer silences, and node 0's 170 ms pause): a hold of 100 ms or more (I188) was logged with its length only, so a pause on both of a node's reactors at once (431 ms, then 170 ms) couldn't be told from a slow task. The reactor now times each task poll and each `io_uring_enter`, keeping the longest of each and, for the poll, where the task was spawned (`spawn` and `block_on` are `#[track_caller]`); the stall watcher takes them on each 10 ms tick, and reads its thread's `/proc/thread-self/schedstat` (one `pread` a tick) for its time on a CPU and waiting for one. The log line goes on: `on a CPU N ms, waiting for one N ms, off one N ms; longest poll N ms, of a task spawned at FILE:LINE; longest io_uring_enter N ms`. In `held_nodes_report_no_healthy_peer` the injected 600 ms device waits read as a 600 ms poll, off a CPU, of the backfill task. Tests: `the_longest_poll_is_named_by_where_its_task_was_spawned` (a task sleeping 30 ms in its poll is named by its spawn line; waiting for a timer counts as an enter, not a poll; fails without `#[track_caller]`); `a_hold_says_where_the_time_went` (the line's text, and a sleeping then a spinning thread told apart by schedstat). | A wait for a CPU or both reactors held at once points at the host; a long poll names the task to fix; a long enter, past the 10 ms timer, means the kernel ran a submission inline. Two clock reads a poll cost tens of nanoseconds; the spawn site is a pointer. | A task spawned by a helper is named by the helper's line unless the helper is `#[track_caller]` too. Time off a CPU doesn't say why (a page fault, a blocking call, memory pressure); the host's own tools (`perf sched`, PSI) are the next step when it dominates. |
| I243 | **Pushed objects keep their holes** (09-replication.md §8.5; issue #158, found on the pilot 2026-11-02): every whole-object copy (`push_parts` for backfill and refill, `push_of` for `Fetch`, the first `FetchPart`) read the object densely from block 0, and the receiver wrote it back as one `Write`, so each hole became a mapped block of zeros. For a plain object that changes nothing; a volume's version is read through to its older versions and its clone's parent (08-engine.md §7.2), so a clone served from such a copy read zeros where it had never written, up to its last written block of the chunk. Now `Shard::held_runs` lists the blocks an object holds, and `WireObject::runs` carries them, with the data of those blocks alone; the receiver writes each run where it was, then truncates. An object without holes encodes as before. `runs` is encoded last and only when present, so a node from before it refuses an object with holes as malformed (the push fails and is retried) rather than filling the holes; a node from before whole objects (`Msg::Push`) isn't sent one either. Not changed: `Shard::digest` still counts a block of zeros as a hole, so copies made densely before this match the good ones and aren't repaired or reported. Making the digest tell them apart is the owner's call (scrub then needs a rule for which copy wins). Test: `cluster::a_refilled_clone_keeps_its_holes` (a clone writes one block per chunk while a node is away; after refill every copy holds only that block, and the clone reads its parent's data elsewhere; on main it read 3 blocks of zeros). |

## Networks, first part (HS19)

| # | Decision | Why | To discuss |
|---|---|---|---|
| I233 | **`hyperstor-fabric`, the library half of stages 1–3** (26-networks.md §29.5, §29.6, §29.8): networks and roles (`model`), each reactor's endpoints ranked by closeness (`endpoints::rank`), the choice of a path per I/O (`path::choose`), and the reconciler's decisions (`reconcile::reconcile`, driven by `step` through the `Inputs` and `Store` traits, D203). Not yet wired: the cluster file's networks, endpoints in the map, reactors binding them, the leader's loop. | Each part is pure and tested on its own before the map format and the reactors change; the map and protocol changes come next, one stage at a time. | The order of the wiring: endpoints in the map first (stage 1), or the reconciler's state in the map first (stage 3). |
| I234 | **"Under one switch" means any switch port above both, however deep** (glidex-topology `Host::closeness`). On the pilot's HGX hosts a GPU and its InfiniBand port meet two switch levels up (`nvidia-smi topo`'s `PXB`), and an NVMe drive meets them at the board's top switch; their nearest switches differ. Comparing nearest switches (glidex's `switch` field) would have called every pair "same NUMA node". | Peer-to-peer stays below the root port in both cases, which is what the closeness level stands for. | — |
| I235 | **The reconciler's rules as built** (D209): a `compute` port is lent once it has been idle (other traffic under 5% of the link) for the dwell (60 s) and nothing has changed on it for the dwell; it is revoked at once, whatever the dwell, on traffic, a live claim, `off`, or the link going down, its weight dropping to 0 in the same pass, and returned 30 s later; an exclusive claim or a down link zeroes any port's weight at once; a weight is the bandwidth other traffic leaves (at least a tenth of the link) and moves only past 20%. Other traffic is the port's counters less hyperstor's own. A model test (`reconciler_invariants_hold_under_random_load`, 200 seeds × 300 steps over random roles, traffic, links and claims) checks these after every pass. | Safety (claims, traffic, links) never waits on damping; only lending and weights do, which is what can flap. | Whether 5% is the right idle line: G52. |
| I236 | **Ties in path choice keep the first draw**, so with equal load the choice follows the weights exactly; under load, two choices keep the busiest of 8 paths within 1.5 times the mean (`load_spreads_without_herding`). | Preferring the second draw on ties would skew toward lighter weights for no gain. | — |
| I237 | **What the pilot's GPU hosts show** (read with `cargo run -p hyperstor-fabric --example host`, 2026-11-02; G47): 8 NUMA nodes, 4 per socket; 7 of 8 InfiniBand HDR ports up, all on one IPoIB subnet in datagram mode (MTU 2044) behind one subnet manager; each NVMe drive ranks the 2 ports of its PCIe switch first (one, plus a port on the same socket, where a rail is down); the 100 GbE bond and 1 GbE port sit on socket 1 only. `arp_ignore=1` and `arp_announce=2` are set, but there are no per-source routing rules: seven same-prefix routes, so traffic bound to any IPoIB address leaves by the first. Only the bond's first member carries its RDMA device (`mlx5_bond_0`). | G47's risk is real on these hosts: binding source addresses alone won't spread traffic; the installer must check for the rules (D207) and the host owner add them. | Who adds the per-source rules on glidex hosts: glidex's netd, as host network owner. |
| I238 | **Stage 1's wiring, first part** (D206, D207): the map carries networks (name, `control`/`data`/`both`, `trusted`, how a port matches) and endpoints per device, in format 14 (written only when either is set, so maps without them keep their format and older nodes read them); the cluster file declares them (`network NAME KIND [trusted] subnet IP/LEN | ib PREFIX`, `endpoint DEV NETWORK IP:PORT`), and a running map gains new ones from it without a recompute; the map service's stored head keeps them. `hyperstord --endpoint DEV=ADDR` binds more listeners, each served by the device's own reactor (D39 per endpoint), and `--networks` names the data networks it reaches peers on; the installer derives both from the cluster file (a node's networks are the data networks it has endpoints on). Clients (`Client::set_networks`, `hyperstor-vhost --networks`) and nodes reach a device at its endpoint on their first network that has one, else at its address. Pings, heartbeats and the map protocol stay on devices' addresses and the map service's. | Endpoints from the cluster file need no topology code on the data path, and the cluster file is already every host's shared truth. A sender picks one endpoint per device for now; choosing among several per I/O (D208) is stage 2. | Endpoints are written by hand until the installer proposes them from the topology (§29.5); whether it should write them into the cluster file or into the map through the API. |
| I239 | **`hyperstor-install check` warns about endpoints that need routing by source** (D207, G47; `hyperstor_fabric::routing::unrouted`): for a storage host's node with endpoints in the installed cluster file, each endpoint address on a port that shares its subnet with another port of the host needs a rule `from ADDR` in `ip rule show` (IPv4 and IPv6); the check is `ok` when all have one and `warn` naming those that don't. No line at all on hosts without endpoints. It warns rather than fails, and doesn't add rules: the host's network belongs to its owner (§29.1). | Binding source addresses alone doesn't spread traffic over ports in one subnet, as the pilot's hosts show (I237). | Whether `install` should refuse to bind such endpoints, as §29.5 says, or keep warning; for now it warns. |
| I240 | **A device's endpoints share its requests, first part of stage 2** (D208; `ClusterMap::device_addrs_on`, `Pool::pending`): when a device has several endpoints on a sender's first network that has any, the client and the daemon choose one per request with `hyperstor_fabric::path::choose`, two random choices taking the one with fewer requests outstanding on its connection; weights are all 1 until the reconciler's are in the map, and RTT isn't measured yet. `hyperstor-fabric`'s host-reading parts are behind its default `host` feature, so the client and daemon depend on it without glidex-topology. A daemon test with two endpoints per device sees sessions on all six, and fails when senders take the first endpoint only. | Requests outstanding are what the connection already counts; bytes would weigh large I/O better, and come with the reconciler's weights. | Bytes in flight against requests outstanding, once G53 shows how many warm paths a host affords. |
| I242 | **Copies and moves between pools** (D214; 10-volumes.md §10.6; branch `feat/copy-volume`): `hyperstor_client::copy` has `Client::copy_volume` (a snapshot, or the head under the volume's lease, into a new volume created with the given id and held while it fills; run again, it empties that volume and starts over; on failure or cancel it is deleted) and `Client::move_volume` (under the volume's lease: refused with snapshots or a branch reading through it, as a delete is; data dropped first from lineage `id` of the new pool, copied there at the head epoch under the lease's identity, `m/{id}` written with the old pool, lineage, size and owned branches, then the header switched by compare-and-set asserting the lease and the version read after attaching: pool, lineage `id`, branch 0, no chain or skips, a new incarnation; then the directory entries, the old data and the owned branch records, then `m/{id}`). Leases are taken as a takeover (`Take::TakeOver`): a holder that stopped renewing, such as a former leader running the same operation (blocklisted, D125), is waited out and replaced; one that renews keeps the volume, and the copy or move is refused after about 12 s. Both read 4 MiB steps, 8 at a time, through `read_sourced` and write only runs that aren't holes or zeros. The API's `CopyVolume` and `MoveVolume` check what they can (the volume active, the pool there and not full, a snapshot's existence, the head detached, the id free; for a move, another pool, detached, no snapshots) and start `Work::Copy` and `Work::Move`, which the runner follows, writing `done`/`total` in MiB every second and setting the copy's cancel flag once the row is cancelled. `hyperstorctl copy-volume` and `move-volume`; mgmt's policy puts both in the storage group. | A copy reads a snapshot without a lease: a snapshot deleted during the copy may be merged under it, so the copy checks it is still there at the end and fails otherwise. A cancel after a move's switch is recorded but the move finishes. A copy's new volume can be seen in `volumes` while it fills (held, so not attached by anyone else). A move holds the volume until the old data is deleted, so it can't be moved back before; the time that takes grows with the volume's chunks, not its data. |
| I244 | **Endpoints kept in the map through the API** (D215): `SetEndpoints` replaces a device's endpoints, each on a data network of the map at a usable address, none twice; the epoch moves only on a change. Admin and mgmt may set any device's; a node's certificate only its own devices' (device id / 1000 = its node), refused and audited otherwise; the local socket is root's. `GetStatus` lists the networks and each device's endpoints; `hyperstorctl device endpoints` sets them by hand. mgmt's Cedar schema has the action, in `operate`. | One writer path for endpoints, the leader's, whoever proposes them; the node-only rule keeps a compromised host from redirecting another's traffic. | — |
| I245 | **The installer proposes and keeps its node's endpoints** (D215; `keep_endpoints`, `hyperstor_fabric::endpoints::propose`): on a joined storage host (it has the API's address and its node's certificate; else the administrator's), each run reads the map's data networks, finds each device's PCI function (its block device, or the file system its file is on, followed through md and dm to the first member), takes the two closest up ports per data network at the device's own port number, leaving out its own address, sets them with `SetEndpoints` where the map differs, and passes them to `hyperstord`. `--endpoints off` (saved) leaves the map alone, for endpoints set by hand. On the pilot's GPU host each drive gets the two InfiniBand ports of its PCIe switch, or one and a port on the same socket where a rail is down. Hosts installed from a cluster file (no API certificate) keep using its `endpoint` lines. | Every run keeps endpoints in step with the ports (a new card, a port down at install). | End-to-end, only the API's side is tested (`join`): loopback has no PCI port, so no test host proposes anything; the proposal itself is tested on a copy of an HGX board's layout and was run on the pilot's host. |
| I246 | **The cluster's raw space in `hyperstorctl status` and on the Overview page** (D156; asked 2026-11-02): `status` prints `space   F free of T raw (N% used)`, summed from the devices' reports over the devices in, and says how many in devices haven't reported; the web UI's Overview shows the same sum as a bar. Pools' free space can't be added up, as pools that share devices count the same bytes. Tests: `status_text_sums_the_space_of_devices_in` (an out device doesn't count, one not reported is named), `manage::status_and_tables` (every device reported on an installed cluster), `browser::the_pages_show_the_cluster` (the bar). | A device out of placement takes no new data, so its free bytes aren't space the cluster can use. Raw space answers "how full is the cluster"; `pools` answers "how much more can this pool take". Numbered I246 because open PRs hold the numbers before it. | Raw space is before copies: a cluster of 3-copy pools holds about a third of it. |
| I247 | **Load and drives, as built** (D218–D221; 27-performance.md §30; branch `feat/perf-heat`): `hyperstor_proto::perf` holds the report (`PerfReport`, kind 80, answered `Noted`). In `hyperstord`, `heat::Heat` (a `RefCell` per reactor) counts `Write`, `Read` and `ReadAt` per (pool, PG) with a 10-slot space-saving sketch of (lineage, branch) taken from the chunk name (24 bytes, or 28 with a branch); `write_round` and the read path time each device I/O between `begin` and `end` (reads and commits, the latter a round of writes and its flush, so its time includes the flush). The `perf` task takes the window every 5 s (`HS_PERF_MS` in tests) and sends it with the newest `drive::watch` reading when it changed or every 30 s. `drive::watch` runs a thread per block device: sysfs, then `hyperstor-smart` for an NVMe namespace (`drive::ask_helper`, a 5-s timeout), else a note why there is no SMART. In `hyperstor-meta`, `perf::Perf` keeps the windows (`Admin::Perf` clones it with the published map for the API thread); `Admin::VolumesOf` names (lineage, branch) pairs. The API's four reads are in `api/perf.rs`; mgmt's policy puts them in `read`. `hyperstor-smart` is a binary of its own (`nvme.rs`: the passthrough ioctl, `NVME_IOCTL_ADMIN_CMD`, on the namespace's block device), installed with `hyperstor-smart.socket` and `hyperstor-smart@.service` on storage hosts; `--serve` answers one request on standard input and output. A commit's time includes the batch it carried, so under load it grows with the batch, not only with the drive: compare devices with each other, not with a single-I/O latency. The sketch's counts are upper bounds for the volumes it reports. Volumes of a lineage's branch 0 that have since rolled back to another branch are named by the lineage's volume. |

The thermal rule fires on a drive that ever throttled, since the counters are lifetime: it should compare readings over time once the leader keeps them longer. |

With `--hole-repair` it pulls that copy first if it isn't this device's (`repair_own`), then pushes it to the others (`push_to`, shared with scrub's ordinary repair) and counts `holes_repaired`. Copies with the fewest blocks but different digests count as unresolved and go into the PG's scrub report. A member that doesn't answer `Holds` (from before it) skips the check for that PG. Tests: `cluster::scrub_reports_zeros_where_a_copy_has_holes` and `cluster::scrub_repairs_zeros_where_a_copy_has_holes` (zeros written into a clone's versions behind the primary, on one replica of chunk 0 and on the primary and a replica of chunk 1, so its one good copy is outvoted; reported only, then repaired and read back). |

| I255 | **Per-key assertions and table sizes** (HS11 stage 1; D194 revised, point 190; 25-kv-ranges.md §28.2): `WireOp::AssertKv { key, value }` (operation 15) holds when the object's key–value table has `key` with exactly `value`, or lacks it (`None`, also when the object doesn't exist). The primary decides it with the other assertions, holding the object (I6); replicas never see it. The engine keeps `Object::kv_bytes`, the table's keys and values in bytes, through puts, overwrites, deletes, ranges and replay (rebuilt as a checkpoint is read, never stored), and `ObjStat` reports it.
