| I256 | **The key–value library without splits** (HS11 stage 2; 25-kv-ranges.md §28.1–§28.3, §28.7): `hyperstor-kv`, on `hyperstor-client`. A `Store` (S3's is store 1) keeps its root as `kv` object *store id* (8 bytes, big-endian) in `.meta`; tables get a random 64-bit id, ranges a random 128-bit one (their object's name). `create_table` writes the first range (`serving`, the whole table, epoch 1), then puts the root's `t`, `i` and `m` entries asserting the name absent; on `Conflict` it deletes the range it wrote. `delete_table` removes the name asserting the record, deletes every range the root names, then the `m` and `i` entries. Rows are encoded as §28.2 says (`keys`); `apply` asserts the range's epoch and, on `Conflict`, reads the range again: the same epoch means the caller's assertion failed (`KvError::Conflict`), another means retry. Reads check the epoch that comes back with the keys and retry if it moved, so a range that shrank between routing and reading is never taken for empty. Routing follows the root, then `next` past a range's end, and waits 50 ms on a range that is `pending`, `merging` or frozen for the row; about ten seconds in all before `Exhausted`. Scans read range by range, a row at a time past the limit to know the last row is whole, and return the next page's first row. Tests: `kv_store_matches_a_sorted_map` (1,500 random batches with right and wrong assertions, gets, rows and paged scans with prefixes against a `BTreeMap`, through two stores; ranges split by hand, half without a root entry so clients follow `next`; a write to a frozen half waits for the split and one below it doesn't; tables created, listed, deleted and made again; it fails if the freeze, `next`, the epoch assertion or the move to the next range is left out); `keys` unit tests (order, decoding and prefixes on random rows heavy in 0x00, 0x01, 0x02 and 0xFF). | Random ids need no allocator, as volume ids. Asserting the record when deleting keeps a delete from removing a table made again under the same name. The epoch check on reads is the one rule that makes a stale cache safe, since bounds and state change only with the epoch. The epoch assertion also keeps a write from recreating a deleted table's range: a put creates a missing object, an assertion on an attribute it lacks fails. | The table record has no state: a delete in progress is an `i` entry whose name is gone. Leftover ranges after a crash in `create_table` and half-done deletes are left for stage 3's housekeeping (§28.5). Nothing uses the library yet; the gateway (HS12) will. |
| I257 | **Recovery, backfill, repair and deep scrub read off the reactor** (the rest of D190's reads; I230 left them on the reactor's thread). (1) `ShardCell::read_slot` takes a `ReadKind`: `Client` reads keep their 128 slots (`MAX_READS`), and `Background` reads, the owner's choice of a share of their own, get 16 more (`MAX_BACKGROUND_READS`), each kind first come first served, so that neither waits behind the other. `device_read` takes the kind. (2) Deep scrub (`verify_pg`) reads each object through `device_read`. (3) A push or fetch (`push_parts`, `push_of`, `fetch`, `fetch_part`'s first part) reads its object's data through `read_for_push`: one read of all its blocks (holes cost no I/O), cut to the runs it holds (`cut_runs`), then its attributes and key–value table from the shard, and the frames sent, with no `.await` after the read. A push must hold every write queued before it is sent, since a write's `Repl` goes to members and fill targets when it is queued: a write queued while the read waits would reach the target first and be overwritten by the stale push. So the read watches its object (`Watch`; `enqueue` counts writes to watched objects), and if one was queued meanwhile it is planned again; after three tries (`PUSH_READ_TRIES`) it reads holding the reactor, as before. `hyperstor_push_rereads_total` counts the reads planned again. | A push or a deep scrub reads a whole object, 4 MiB for a volume's chunk, holding the reactor for as long as the device takes: 1 MiB client reads held pilot reactors 100–153 ms before D190 (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-01). Re-reading keeps a push's ordering with no change to the protocol or the target. A share of their own keeps a recovery from crowding clients out, and clients from starving it. | An object written more often than its read takes still reads holding the reactor after three tries (seen in the test, with 300 ms reads); refusing writes to it for one read, as `repair_own`'s exec lock does, would end that. Pushes in flight on a slow device take up to 16 reads' buffers more. Checkpoint slices, deferred writes and `read_layered` in a flatten's copy-up still read in the device's calls. Not measured on the pilot yet. |
| I258 | **Health per network, as built** (D210; 26-networks.md §29.7; branch `feat/network-health`): `peer_watch` pings each peer node on its *paths*: the endpoints of its devices in shared PGs on this node's networks (`ClusterMap::device_paths_on`, down ones included so their return is seen), or its first device's address when it has none there, as before networks. UDP pings, pongs and the TCP fallback (D185) are kept per path; each endpoint's listener also gets a UDP socket that answers pings. A node silent on every path for the peer timeout is reported with `PeerDown`; a path silent while another answers, with `EndpointDown { reporter, addr }` (kind 84, answered `PeerDownOk`). The leader keeps endpoint reports as it keeps peer reports (fresh for half the liveness timeout, from nodes that have heartbeated, about endpoints the map has), and `endpoints_tick` marks an endpoint down on two reporters, damped as nodes are (`flap_hold`, held at least 2 s). `ClusterMap::endpoints_down` travels in map format 15, written only while one is down; `device_addrs_on` and `device_addr_on` skip down endpoints, falling to the next network and then to the address; replaced or removed endpoints lose their mark. `GetStatus` sets `Endpoint.down`. Tests: `hyperstor_map::network_tests::senders_skip_endpoints_down_and_keep_pinging_them`, `hyperstor_meta::tests::two_reports_mark_an_endpoint_down_not_its_node`, `endpoints::a_failed_endpoint_is_down_and_its_node_stays_up` (an endpoint cut by the fault rig, `Partitions::cut_addr`: down, node up, writes with three copies go on, back once healed; both cut: the node is down though its address answers), and `endpoints::a_fenced_holder_is_refused_on_every_network` (§29.11). | Two reporters, as D40: one node's own failed port then reports every peer's endpoint on that network, and marks none. A node with endpoints isn't pinged at its address: D210 counts only the control network's heartbeats and the data networks, and the address may be on the control network. The mark is in the map, so every sender moves at once and the API shows it, at the cost of a map epoch per change, which damping bounds. | A storage node of an older build doesn't know kind 84 and drops the connection it comes on; the map service is upgraded first (as for every new message), and reporters ignore the error. In a cluster of two storage nodes, no endpoint can be marked down, as no node can be on peer reports alone (D40). |
| I259 | **The map service logs openraft's warnings and errors** (#177): hyperstor-meta installed no `tracing` subscriber, so when the pilot's map service committed nothing for 32 minutes during a rolling install (2026-11-03), with the leader waiting in glidex-raftstore's `before_write` → `ensure_linearizable`, openraft's own messages on why the followers didn't accept its entries went nowhere. `raft_log::init` now prints `tracing` events to standard error, filtered by `HS_LOG` (default `warn`). A warning or error from one place (file and line) is printed at most once every 5 s: openraft warns twice per failed heartbeat, 40 times a second at our 50 ms heartbeat, so a member down for a reboot would otherwise write tens of thousands of lines. Debug and trace aren't limited. | A repeat of #177 can be diagnosed from the journal; `HS_LOG=warn,openraft=debug` in a drop-in shows more. | The limit hides repeats of one message for 5 s, a different peer's among them (one place in openraft's code serves every target). |
| I260 | **Splits and merges on each range's primary** (HS11 stage 3; 25-kv-ranges.md §28.4, §28.5, §28.7): `hyperstor_kv::jobs` holds the steps, run through any client: `check` splits a range past `split_bytes` at the row that halves it by bytes (never its first row; a single row isn't split), or merges it with its right neighbour when the two hold less than `merge_bytes`; `resume` finishes or undoes a stopped job from the attributes alone. Every step is one list on one object asserting what the step before left: the epoch, a range's state while copying into it, a new range `pending` until opened, the right range still starting where the left ends. A merge's left range carries `merged` (a new attribute) from its extension until the right range is deleted, and a split's lower range carries `next` until the root names the new one; both are how a stopped job is found and finished. A split job whose shrink fails checks whether the lower range or the root names its new range, and deletes it if neither does (asserting it still as opened). `hyperstord` (`kv.rs`) marks range objects written through `exec`, looks at their size each second, and sweeps the ranges of every PG it is primary of (default every 10 s, `NodeConfig::kv_sweep`; limits in `kv_limits`, 64 MiB and 16 MiB by default), taking up a range found unfinished at the same epoch on two sweeps running; two jobs at a time per reactor, one per range. It reads its copy of a range only once no commit is in flight (`ShardCell::get`): commits leave the reactor (I199), and a first build that read it straight after a sleep panicked the reactor whenever one was, on any cluster, every sweep. Tests: `kv_jobs_finish_or_undo_from_every_step` (a split stopped after each of its six steps and a merge after each of its seven, taken up; settled with each row in one serving range and nothing left over, the rows as written, a store with the old ranges cached reading and writing on; after an undone merge a row deleted stays deleted when the ranges merge after all); `kv_ranges_split_and_merge_on_their_own` (8 KiB and 2 KiB limits, 600 writes while a node is killed and restarted: the table grows to four or more ranges and, when all but five rows are deleted, merges back to one, with commits slowed by 3 ms and every reactor watched for ending; a split stopped before opening and a merge stopped after extending are undone and finished by the daemons). The tests fail if the daemon never takes up, if an open split is undone, or if an undone merge keeps the rows it copied. | Steps that assert their predecessor's result make a stale or duplicated job harmless, so a primary needs no lease on its jobs and failover needs no hand-over. The two-sweep rule keeps a sweep from undoing a job under way in the common case; when it does anyway, the job fails its next step. `merged` makes a merge's last steps findable from the left range, the one whose primary runs it. Other tests' clusters turn jobs off (`KV_OFF`), as they make ranges by hand. | No test reaches the race the copy's state assertion guards (a live job copying into a range a take-up just deleted). The leftover check (an hour, §28.5) isn't built: an R2 created by a job whose split was undone before its step 2, and a first range left by a crash in `create_table`, stay until it is. A merge copies only user keys, so the deletion queue (stage 4) must move or drain a right range's queue before it goes. The middle row is found by reading the whole range through the client, 64 MiB at the default limit. |
| I261 | **The deletion queue and leftover ranges** (HS11 stage 4; 25-kv-ranges.md §28.5, §28.6): `Batch::collect(pool, ns, name)` names objects to delete; a batch's collected objects become one queue entry, `q` + a random 128-bit id → the list, put in the batch's own operation list, so it is written only if the batch's assertions hold, and the id is made once per `apply`, so a batch sent again puts the same entry. `jobs::drain` deletes the objects of up to 64 entries, each before its entry. `hyperstord` drains a range with entries before looking at its size, again each second until the queue is empty. A merge copies from `q` on, carrying the right range's queue; an undone merge leaves copies of its entries in the left range, which delete nothing a second time. Leftovers: `jobs::named` (table alive, root entry at the start, or the `parent` (a new attribute, set on a split's new range) naming it by `next` or as the range it splits into; a range in a job counts as named); `jobs::delete_leftover` deletes it asserting its epoch, then the root's entry for it, and the table's `i` entry once no `m` entry is left. The sweep looks every quarter of `KvJobs::leftover` (default 1 h) and deletes ranges found unnamed on every look for that long, counted in memory. `NodeConfig`'s `kv_limits` and `kv_sweep` became `kv: KvJobs` (`off`, `limits`, `sweep`, `leftover`); tests that make ranges by hand turn jobs `off`. Tests: `kv_queued_objects_are_deleted_after_their_batch` (a failed batch collects nothing; objects deleted and the queue emptied; an entry naming an object already deleted drained; the range's primary killed right after a batch; a split's new range drains its own); `kv_leftover_ranges_are_deleted` (a first range with no root entry, a split's new range left by an undo, both ranges and the root entries of a table whose delete stopped; live tables, one split by hand, keep all ranges and rows); the merge in `kv_jobs_finish_or_undo_from_every_step` carries a queue entry. They fail if drains delete nothing, a merge leaves the queue, every range counts as unnamed, or deleted tables count as alive. | Random entry ids need no counter and no read before a batch; per-batch ids make retries idempotent. Counting the hour on the primary needs no clock in the range object, and a restart only delays a delete. Asserting the epoch keeps a range that changed since it was looked at. | Deletion is at least once: names collected must be the batch's own (S3's data objects are). Killing the primary straight after a batch races the drain, so that test doesn't always reach a drain cut short; the entry-before-objects order is what covers it, and an entry naming deleted objects is tested. The leftover look reads the root two or three times per range a quarter hour. |
| I262 | **S3's object layer** (HS12 stage 1; 14-s3.md §14.6, §14.7): `hyperstor-s3`'s library, on a client's reactor. A bucket is a table of store 1, its record (format byte, tenant, creation time) appended to the table's record (`Table::info`, 25-kv-ranges.md §28.3); names follow S3's rules for general purpose buckets; ListBuckets scans the root's tables and keeps a tenant's. An object is row `o` + name, its index entry in column `l`: written time, size, MD5, part count, CRC32C, content type, user metadata, then the bytes inline (up to 64 KiB) or the object id, chunk size and parts. Chunks are `s3` objects of the data pool named bucket id, object id, part and index, 4 MiB, the last padded to 4 KiB; reads are aligned and cut, all of a range's chunks at once. A PUT over the inline limit first puts row `u` + expiry + id (naming the object) and column `p` + id in the object's row, then writes the chunks one by one, then one batch asserts `p` + id and the `l` it read, deletes `p` + id, puts `l` and queues the old version's chunks (D192), then removes the `u` row. An unknown outcome is settled by reading `l` back. `S3::clean(bucket, now)` gives up uploads expired by `now` (a day for a PUT): one batch asserting `p` + id deletes it and queues the chunks, so it and the completion can't both happen; uploads refuse writes and completion within an hour of expiry. Listing scans `o` + prefix, rolls names up to the delimiter and skips past each common prefix; a page's `next` is the name the next page starts at. Deleting a bucket gives up its expired uploads and needs no row left. For a 5 TiB object the queue would otherwise hold a million names: a queue entry now names a series (prefix and count, numbers big-endian u32), and deleting a table drains each range's queue before deleting it (`jobs::drain_all`, also before a leftover range is deleted), where before it dropped the queue with the range. `Client::read_object` reads a named object. Tests: `s3_objects_match_a_model` (400 random PUTs inline, in one or several chunks, on chunk boundaries, in pieces; given up half-way, aborted, short; GETs of random ranges, HEADs, DELETEs, listings with prefixes, delimiters and pages of 1–5, against a model, through two gateways, with a storage node killed and back; an upload the clean-up gave up can't complete, and a completed one is left alone; in the end every dead chunk is deleted and the devices hold only live objects' chunks; it fails if a PUT or DELETE doesn't queue the old chunks, the completion doesn't assert its upload, the clean-up queues nothing, listings don't skip past a common prefix, or reads don't cut a block's start); `kv_jobs_finish_or_undo_from_every_step` grows a table deleted with a series and a single object queued (fails if the delete doesn't drain); `layout` unit tests. | Rows keep uploads apart from objects and sorted by expiry, so the clean-up reads only what expired. Recording the upload in the object's row makes completion and clean-up one-row batches that assert the same column (D200), with no lock. Series keep queue entries small. The engine reads whole blocks, so the library pads and cuts rather than keeping exact sizes on chunks. | A GET that began before an overwrite or delete fails (`Changed`) once the old chunks are drained, which happens within seconds; S3 lets it finish. Delaying the drain (a time in each queue entry) would fix it: for stage 2. A PUT writes its chunks one at a time; stage 2 measures it. A PUT that begins between a bucket delete's check and the delete loses its chunks if its gateway then dies too. Listing reads whole rows, an upload's parts included. Nothing runs the clean-up yet: gateways will, hourly (stage 2). |
| I263 | **Scrub settings per pool, as built** (D224–D226; 28-scrub.md §31.2–31.3): `PoolInfo::scrub` (`ScrubSettings`: hours, deep every, hole repair, deep rate in MiB/s), in map format 16, written only once a pool's settings differ from the defaults so older nodes keep reading the map. Each device runs a scrubber per pool, which reads the settings before each PG, so a change applies from the next one; the deep rate and hole repair are read where they are used. `hyperstord --scrub-hours` and `--hole-repair` are ignored with a log line, and `NodeConfig::scrub_interval` is now a test hook overriding every pool's interval. Step-up only when weakening needed one RPC per case: mgmt authorizes by RPC name and passes the body through unread, so `SetScrubSettings` refuses a weakening change (`weakens_scrub`, naming what) and `WeakenScrubSettings` (step-up in mgmt's policy) needs a reason, kept in the audit event with the settings before and after. `hyperstorctl scrub set` calls the second when `--reason` is given. Health warns `scrub reduced: …` per pool. Cache pools are refused: they aren't scrubbed. | One place decides how each pool is checked, and weakening it leaves a reason and a warning. | 28-scrub.md's single `SetScrubSettings(…, reason)` is two RPCs. Reports, overdue checks, scrub on demand, scrub after recovery and the UI are stages 2–4. |
| I264 | **The S3 gateway over HTTP** (HS12 stage 2; 14-s3.md §14.2, §14.6, §14.7; D195): `hyperstor-s3`, a binary. Tokio's threads (`--threads`, 2) take HTTPS (rustls, HTTP/1.1; the certificate in `--tls DIR`, `s3.crt` and `s3.key`, read at start), check SigV4 (`sigv4`) and decode the body; reactor threads (`--reactors`, 2), each with its own client and `S3`, run the object layer, handed closures over the reactor's channel and answering on tokio's. Bodies cross through bounded channels (a PUT's in 1 MiB pieces, four at most; a GET's a chunk at a time, the next read ahead). **Signatures:** in the `Authorization` header (with `x-amz-date`, or `Date` in RFC 7231's or Python's `-0000` form) or a presigned query (up to 7 days); the path and query decoded and encoded again as AWS encodes them; any region, the one signed with; 15 minutes of skew; Signature Version 2 and session tokens refused (`NotImplemented`). **Bodies:** a signed SHA-256 is checked as the body streams; `STREAMING-AWS4-HMAC-SHA256-PAYLOAD` (each chunk's signature checked as it arrives) and `STREAMING-UNSIGNED-PAYLOAD-TRAILER` (`aws-chunked`, a checksum after) are decoded (`chunked`); signed trailers are refused. `x-amz-checksum-` CRC32, CRC32C, SHA1 and SHA256, in a header or a trailer, and `Content-MD5` are checked before the object appears: the reactor holds the upload until tokio's side says the body was good, else gives it up. **Requests:** ListBuckets, Create/Head/Delete Bucket, GetBucketLocation (empty: us-east-1), Put/Get/Head/DeleteObject (one-span `Range`, `response-` overrides), ListObjects v1 and v2 (`encoding-type=url`; a continuation token is the next name in hex; v1's marker and StartAfter skip a whole common prefix when they name one). Any other parameter or `x-amz-copy-source`, `If-Match` and `If-None-Match` on a PUT: `501 NotImplemented`. A key reaches its tenant's buckets only (`403`). Errors are S3's XML, `<Error>` in no namespace (botocore reads nothing otherwise); the cluster's own errors are logged and answered `503` without their text, which may name hosts. Object names must be UTF-8 over HTTP. **Until stage 3:** keys from `--keys FILE` (`ID SECRET TENANT` a line, refused unless mode 0600; secrets zeroed when dropped, never logged), new buckets in `--data-pool` and `--index-pool`. **Hourly**, from a random point in the first hour, each gateway gives up every bucket's expired uploads. **The drain waits** (I262's open issue): a queue key is now `q` + due time + id (25-kv-ranges.md §28.6); S3's overwrites and deletes linger 15 minutes (`Config::linger`), uploads given up don't. **Chunks in flight:** a PUT keeps four writes going (`Config::in_flight`). Tests: `s3_gateway_serves_an_sdk` (boto3 against a gateway on a 3-node cluster, keys made for the run, a CA made for it: buckets of two tenants, PUTs inline and in chunks, ranges, HEAD, odd names, every checksum the SDK can make, a bad `Content-MD5`, listings v1 and v2 in pages of 7 with a delimiter, StartAfter, presigned GET and PUT and a changed URL, a wrong secret and key, `NotImplemented`, deletes; CI runs it with boto3 1.43.126 (1.38.46 until 2026-11-08), which sends CRC32 trailers by default, and fails without boto3); `sigv4` tests against AWS's published examples (header, presigned, chunked) and botocore's; `chunked`, `dates`, `gateway`, `keys`, `xml` unit tests; `s3_objects_match_a_model` now reads each replaced version straight after its overwrite, and one 2 s after (past the primaries' looks at the queue; fails if the linger is dropped or the drain ignores due times), and its last check skips copies on devices a PG moved off (strays, dropped whole after their grace: with the linger, deletes now land after the PG has moved). Every check named here was seen to fail with its code broken: the linger, due times, `Content-MD5`, checksums, signatures, tenants, markers on a common prefix. Measured: `s3_put_throughput` (02-benchmarks.md). CI's Python (3.13) refused the test's certificate: its default `VERIFY_X509_STRICT` wants an Authority Key Identifier, which `hyperstor-pki` left out of every certificate a CA signs; it now writes one on CAs and leaves alike, and the SDK test turns the strict check on whatever the Python. | Tokio for HTTP and the reactors for the data path is D195; closures over a channel keep the object layer as it is. Checking the body before the batch that makes it visible means no bad object is ever seen, with nothing to undo. Keys in a file, for now, let SDKs be tested before the map service keeps them (D231). Any signed region: clients disagree on a default and the region only scopes the key. The due time in the key lets the primary find what is due with one range read, nothing scanned. | The linger and in-flight values are guesses: measure on the pilot. The certificate isn't re-read when it changes (mgmt's is, D109). No bucket lookups are cached: each request reads the bucket's record. Content-Encoding, Content-Disposition, Cache-Control and Expires aren't kept. CRC64NVME is refused. HTTP/2 isn't offered. A GET slower than 15 minutes fails part-way when its object is replaced. |
| I265 | **Scrub reports per PG and their health checks, as built** (D227, D228; 28-scrub.md §31.4, §31.5, §31.8): after each PG the primary sends `ScrubReport` with the unresolved count in the body as before and the rest (`ScrubDetail`: start, duration, deep, compared, inconsistent, repaired, damaged, holes, holes repaired, why the hole check was skipped) in the **payload**, which a map service from before ignores. The leader keeps each PG's last light and deep report and how many scrubs running skipped the hole check (`scrubs.rs`), and once a minute writes the changed records, a record for each PG without one (counted from then) and removals for PGs gone to rows `scrub/POOL/PG` of the replicated store's `settings` table, from a thread of its own; a new leader loads them. A scrubber asks `ScrubAges` (new map protocol kinds 85, 86) at each pass, starts at the PG scrubbed longest ago, and scrubs a PG deep when its last deep scrub is `deep_every` intervals old, half an interval early, or never was; without an answer, every `deep_every`th pass from PG 0, as before. Health adds `scrub overdue` (2 × the interval light, 2 × the deep interval deep, counted from the record's start), `holes unchecked in the last two scrubs` and, as an error, `damaged` (the primary's copy still holds damaged objects of the PG after its scrub). `GetScrubStatus`, `ListPgScrubs`, `hyperstorctl scrub status` and `scrub pgs [--overdue]` (both read in mgmt's policy). Tests: `scrubs::tests::*` (records in the store, overdue, unchecked, damaged, health text, changes), `scrub_status_and_health_from_the_records`, `deep_scrub_is_due_by_the_records`, `scrub_detail_rides_in_the_payload_and_may_grow`, `cluster::scrub_reports_are_kept_per_pg` (every PG reported light and deep with counts; `ScrubAges` matches), `meta_group::scrub_records_survive_the_group_restarting`, `manage::scrub_status_and_pgs_are_shown`. | Reports in the payload: the map protocol's decoder refuses a body longer than it knows, so trailing fields (as 28-scrub.md §31.9 assumed) would fail a rolling upgrade. A request of its own rather than ages in the map lease: the lease answers every heartbeat, and the ages are needed once a pass. Deep by each PG's age rather than a pass count: a restart no longer makes the next pass deep everywhere. Records read by the API from the member's own copy: any member answers, and only the last minute is missing. Rows of an existing table rather than a table of their own: a member from before them would fail to apply an entry naming an unknown table, so a rolling upgrade, one host at a time, would break the group. | The leader's last minute of reports is lost with it, and health lags the leader by up to a minute. The week's totals are of each PG's last light and deep report in the 7 days, not every scrub (no history is kept). `damaged` counts the primary's copy only: a replica's damage shows as an inconsistency the majority repairs. The leader metrics `hyperstor_pool_scrub_oldest_seconds` and `_overdue_pgs` aren't built: the map service has no metrics endpoint; `GetScrubStatus` has the numbers. Health's links to the UI wait for stage 4. |
| I266 | **The `slow-device` rule tested end to end** (D222, G55; 27-performance.md §30.7): `NodeConfig::perf_every` sets how often each device reports its load (zero: `HS_PERF_MS`, else 5 s), so the cluster test `the_advisor_names_a_device_slowed_on_purpose` can report every 250 ms. Four devices of one kind are read across 32 chunks; the fault rig's `CommitDelays::set_reads` makes device 2000's reads 20 ms slower, inside the time the device records; the leader's findings (`Meta::perf`, then `perf::findings`) name it, and only it, and drop it once its delay is off. | The unit test of the rule fed it reports by hand; this one goes from the device's timing through `PerfReport` to the leader. The pilot can't show the rule for its two NVMe drives (it needs three of a kind), and a cgroup limit on a pilot node slows live VM disks. | The test's window is 12 reports of 250 ms, not a minute; the rule counts reports, so a minute holds only at the default 5 s. Only reads are tested; commits go through the same timing (`write_round`). Not run on real drives. |
| I267 | **Scrubbing now and after recovery, as built** (D229, D230; 28-scrub.md §31.6, §31.7): the map service sends `ScrubNow` (PGs, deep, cancel; map protocol kinds 87, 88) to each PG's primary device, which queues those it is the clean primary of and scrubs them one at a time beside the pass, 100 ms apart; a PG is never scrubbed twice at once. `StartScrub(pool, pgs, deep)` is an operation (`Work::Scrub`, with its start in Unix seconds): every 2 s the runner asks the map service (`Admin::ScrubNow`) how many of its PGs reported a scrub (deep if asked) since then, and it asks the primaries of the others, each again after 10 minutes (a primary moved, or the scrub was cut short); PGs merged away count as done. Cancelled, the primaries drop the PGs not begun. Scrub after recovery runs on the leader every second: a PG whose PG epoch changed is asked a light scrub once clean (acting = up, `size` copies) for a minute, until a scrub since reports, at most six asks; not for cache pools or pools with scrub off. `hyperstorctl scrub start --pool P [--pg N ...] [--deep] [--wait S]`, a store administrator's write in mgmt. Tests: `cluster::a_scrub_asked_for_runs_now` (every PG deep within seconds, though its turn is a day away), `cluster::recovered_pgs_are_scrubbed` (a node out; every PG that changed is scrubbed after, hole check included), `manage::a_scrub_started_now_finishes_as_an_operation` (the operation ends done, every PG deep, audited; cache pools refused). | The map service asks rather than the node deciding: it sees every PG's members change, including on a node that was down, and keeps the records that say when it's done. Done by the records, not by the nodes' answers: a scrub that ran is what counts, whoever asked. An epoch change as the sign of recovery: every push to a member (backfill, refill, peering after a member is back) follows one; one without pushes costs a light scrub from cached digests. | The recovery list is the leader's, in memory: after a change of leader, PGs that recovered and were clean before it wait for their turn. A deep `StartScrub` of a large pool queues every PG at once on each primary; the queue is in order of PG, not age. A primary from before `ScrubNow` never answers, so the operation waits for the regular pass. |
| I268 | **S3's keys and placements in the map service, and the cluster key** (HS12 stage 3, first part; 14-s3.md §14.2, §14.7; D162, D231–D233): **The cluster key** is 32 random bytes in `/etc/hyperstor/pki/cluster.key` (mode 0600, `hyperstor cluster key v1` and hex): `hyperstor-install` makes it with the root on the first host, a member joining with a meta token gets it in `JoinResponse.cluster_key`, the map service's unit loads it as a credential (`LoadCredential=`, only where the file exists, since a missing credential would stop the unit), and `root-backup` encrypts it with the root (restored by `root-restore`, refused onto a host holding another). `hyperstor_pki::ClusterKey` seals with AES-256-GCM, a random nonce, and the sealed value bound to a context. **Keys** are rows `s3key/<id>` of the replicated store's `settings` table (tenant, description, creation, creator, the secret sealed for `hyperstor s3 key <id>`), **placements** rows `s3place/<name>` (data and index pool names): rows of an existing table, so a member from before them applies every entry (I265's reason). `S3Service`: `CreateS3Key` (id `HS` and 18 base32 characters, secret 40 of URL-safe base64, from `getrandom`; the reply carries the secret once; audited by id), `ListS3Keys`, `RevokeS3Key` (deletes the row), `SetS3Placement` (pools must exist and not be cache pools), `DeleteS3Placement`, `ListS3Placements`, and `GetS3Gateway`, every key with its secret and every placement, for `s3` certificates only (a new `Need::S3`; every other role is refused and audited), answered by any member from its copy. Role `s3` in `hyperstor-pki`; tokens of role `s3` name their gateway (as mgmt's, D122), and its join signs a certificate naming `gateway_ips`, 127.0.0.1 and `localhost`, which can serve HTTPS. mgmt's policy: keys are `Secure` (create with step-up), placements `Store`, their list `Read`, `GetS3Gateway` `Machine`. `hyperstorctl s3 key create\|list\|revoke` and `s3 placement set\|list\|delete`. **The gateway** (`hyperstor-s3 --api ADDR[,ADDR...] --identity DIR`) fetches `GetS3Gateway` at start (serving nothing until a member answers) and every 5 s, swaps the set in whole, and keeps the last one while no member answers; `--keys FILE` with fixed pools stays for running without the API (the in-process SDK test). CreateBucket reads its body (64 KiB at most, checked as a PUT's) for `LocationConstraint`: a placement's name chooses it; none, or the region the request was signed for, is `default`; another is `InvalidLocationConstraint`; no `default` is `503`. Tests: `s3_keys_and_placements_come_from_the_map_service` (an installed cluster: placements refused for a cache pool or a missing pool; a key's secret printed once, absent from lists, the audit, the store's files, a backup and the log; `s3` tokens need a name; an admin certificate refused `GetS3Gateway`; boto3 against the binary with an `s3` certificate signed by the cluster's intermediate: CreateBucket on `default`, `InvalidLocationConstraint` until the placement is set and then created there, objects round-trip, a wrong secret `SignatureDoesNotMatch`, a revoked key `InvalidAccessKeyId` within 15 s, keys kept while the map service is stopped); `root_key_backup_and_restore` grows the cluster key (made 0600, loaded by the unit, encrypted in the backup, restored when lost, refused over another); `s3keys` and `ClusterKey` unit tests; the gateway's `location_constraints_choose_a_placement`. Each was seen to fail with its code broken: any role reading the secrets, a secret kept unsealed, the gateway not refreshing, LocationConstraint ignored, cache pools allowed. | One key, held where the intermediate's is, rather than a secret per purpose: D162 needs it for volumes too, and members already share and protect the intermediate's key the same way. Deleting a revoked key's row, rather than marking it: a gateway's set is replaced whole each fetch, so absence is revocation, and no secret stays sealed once unused. Polling every 5 s rather than a stream: a few keys' worth of bytes per gateway, no stream to keep across leader changes, and revocation within seconds as D231 asks. Keys fetched before serving: a gateway that served with none would refuse every client while it looks like it works. The region as `default`: boto3 and the AWS CLI send their configured region as the LocationConstraint of every CreateBucket outside us-east-1. | `hyperstor-install --s3` and `--role s3`, and an all-in-one cluster running a gateway, are the second part (the test signs the gateway's certificate itself; joins with an `s3` token aren't tested end to end yet). A member joined before the cluster key existed has none: it can't make or serve keys (`no_cluster_key`) until it has one (I269: its installer fetches it from another member); a key made by a member with another key is skipped, logged, by members that can't open it. Placements kept pool names, so a renamed pool lost them (I269: kept by id). The cluster key isn't TPM-sealed or rotated (D162's systemd credential is plain `LoadCredential=` here, as the intermediate's is). The gateway's identity isn't renewed by itself: the installer's daily run will. Secrets cross the API in the clear inside mutual TLS, and live in the gateway's memory, zeroed when dropped. |
| I269 | **Gateways in the installer** (HS12 stage 3, second part; 14-s3.md §14.7; D233): `hyperstor-install --s3` adds `hyperstor-s3.service` (as `hyperstor`, `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE` for port 443, `LimitMEMLOCK=infinity` for its reactors) and `/etc/hyperstor/s3.env` (`--meta`, `--api`, `--identity /etc/hyperstor/pki`, `--tls`, `--listen 0.0.0.0:--s3-port`, default 443); `--s3-tls DIR` names the site's certificate, else the gateway's own serves HTTPS; `--no-s3` stops and removes it, as does `uninstall`. **Its certificate:** on a map service host, `s3/<member>` signed with the other leaves, naming loopback, the member's addresses and this host's names and addresses (`LocalNames`, as mgmt's web certificate), recorded beside it so a change of names issues it again; elsewhere, a join with an `s3` token (`--role s3 --join API --token-file F --address IP`, a host with only a gateway, or `--s3` with a token in a later run on a joined storage host, whose own join spent its token), refused before anything is written unless the certificate is an `s3` one, and renewed by later runs over the API (`renew_leaf`, which `renew_node` became), restarting the gateway. **The all-in-one** runs one unless `--no-s3` was ever given (the choice saved), and sets the `default` placement to its pool `vol` through the API with the administrator's certificate the first time a run finds the map service answering, recorded in `/etc/hyperstor/s3-placement.set` so a placement an administrator later removes stays removed; a real install waits up to 30 s for it after starting the services. The test harness installs on its own ports (`--s3-port`) and starts the gateway from `s3.env` (`Cluster::start_s3`). **Follow-ups to I268, from the owner's review:** placements are kept by pool id (two u32s in the row), set by name and answered with each pool's name now and its id, and the gateway goes by the ids, so a renamed pool keeps them; `.meta` is refused as a placement's pool. A member without the cluster key gets it from another: `CaService.GetClusterKey` answers members' (`meta`) certificates only (`Need::Member`), audited, and the installer, on a member with no `cluster.key`, asks the API it joined through and each listed member's address on its own API port, with its `api` certificate, before making one where the root is; with neither, it warns and says how to fix it. Tests: `s3_keys_and_placements_come_from_the_map_service` now runs the installed gateway (its env, its certificate's role and names, the default placement set by the run after the first; `.meta` refused; a renamed pool's placements listed under its new name and used); `a_gateway_joins_with_an_s3_token` (a mgmt token refused; an s3 token makes `s3/gw2` naming the address, an env with the joined API and the map service's addresses, no map service or node there, a second run with nothing to change; the gateway installed there serves buckets with boto3; a removed default placement stays removed; `--no-s3` removes the unit and keeps it removed). Each was seen to fail with its code broken: any token making a gateway, the default placement set again, a joined gateway not using the API it joined, the all-in-one without a gateway, `--no-s3` leaving it, its certificate of another role; and, for the follow-ups, no fetch from peers, any role reading the cluster key, `.meta` allowed. `map_service_members_join_the_first` checks joined members get the first's cluster key, a member whose key is removed fetches it back on its next run, and an administrator's certificate is refused it. | A join of its own for a storage host's gateway: a token is single-use and decides one identity, and the gateway's certificate must not be the node's (D233: only gateways read S3's secrets). The default placement once: a trial cluster works at once, and a cluster an administrator has shaped is theirs. The gateway's own certificate for HTTPS by default: it already names the host, and clients that trust the cluster's root (as `hyperstorctl` does) need nothing else; a site with public clients brings its own. | A joined gateway's certificate names the one `--address`; DNS names come with the site's certificate. The all-in-one gateway listens on every address, as mgmt does; `--s3-port` moves it. Gateways on hosts with data networks don't take `--networks` yet. The installer finds other members' APIs at their map addresses on its own API port (members are installed with the same port); a first host that lost its key while its peers are unreachable makes a new one, which they'd refuse to open (their keys skipped, logged). Behind a Cloudflare Tunnel (installation guide) rests on G56. A gateway's host isn't checked by `check` (port 443 free, for one). |
| I270 | **Four end-to-end tests that failed on slow CI runners** (test only): (1) `a_returning_node_goes_down_once` counts the map service's log from once the cluster is healthy, so a startup down and up (I198) isn't counted as a second down. (2) `the_installer_drops_a_removed_device` waits for device 1 in the published map before the removal that must be refused: `device add` answers once the leader's map has changed, before it is published, and the installer's check reads the published map (`GetStatus`), so on a slow runner it allowed the removal. (3) `a_full_pool_purges_its_oldest_trash` tries `trash list` and `pools` again when one fails in its waits: listing the trash reads volume headers from the `.meta` pool on the devices the purge is deleting from, and a call passed the CLI's 5-s deadline (three CI runs, commits waiting 100–270 ms). (4) `a_lost_majority_is_recovered_alone` waits for member 1's own store to hold the map (a `map backup` from it succeeds) before stopping members and after `--force-alone`: a leader is elected before its first map is applied, and until then a member makes no tokens (`not_formed`) and refuses `--force-alone` ("the store holds no map yet"). | Each test now waits for the state it checks, as the others do; none of the timeouts grew. | Two were behaviours rather than test races, settled 2026-11-05 (05-decision-log.md points 196, 197): `device add` answered before the map with the device was published, so an `install --device` followed at once by `--remove-device` could miss it; map changes now answer once published (D236, I273). The trash list, and other reads of `.meta`, wait behind a purge's deletes on the same devices; that is left to D46's priority classes (HS10b). |
| I271 | **Scrub in the web UI, and calls that only the leader serves** (28-scrub.md §31.8): a pool's Scrub card (Pools, with the pool's PGs open) shows its settings, edited in place (a change that weakens them takes a reason and goes as `WeakenScrubSettings`, so mgmt asks for a fresh login, D226), its oldest light and deep scrub, the PGs overdue, unchecked, damaged and inconsistent, the last 7 days' totals, and **Scrub now** and **Scrub deep now** (`StartScrub`). Only `scrub start` goes on to the leader: the others refused with `not_leader` stay as they are until each is looked at, since a write that was refused did nothing, but a retry elsewhere should be what the person meant. | The metrics of §31.8 are I272's. A member's API recorded as its listening address, the map's host when it listens on every address: behind NAT or a proxy it may be wrong. A member that never led has no API in the list. | I272 | **Scrub metrics on the API's port, as built** (D234, D235; 28-scrub.md §31.8): `GET /metrics` beside the RPCs. connectrpc's `Server` takes only its own service, so the API's TCP port runs an accept loop of its own (`api/metrics.rs`), as the local socket already did: TLS with a 10 s handshake bound and HTTP/1 header reads bounded at 30 s, as connectrpc's own; each request carries `PeerAddr` and `PeerCerts` as connectrpc's server gives them, and goes to the RPCs unless it is `GET /metrics`. `/metrics` is authorized as `GetMetrics` like a call (rate limits and refusals audited): role `metrics`, or any role that may read; 401 without a certificate, 403 for `host`. A `metrics` certificate is refused every RPC but `RenewCertificate` (and `SetEndpoints` refuses it as it refuses any role but `admin`, `mgmt` and the device's own node). `hyperstorctl cert renew [--force]` renews the certificate of `--tls DIR` with itself once past two thirds of its life, writing each file aside and renaming it over; `RenewCertificate`'s row in the CLI's coverage table names it. The scraper renews its own certificate because nothing else on its host knows about it; `hyperstor-install` renews only the host's own. | The accept loop leaves out connectrpc's connection retirement, HTTP/2 keepalive and panic catching: a handler that panics ends its connection, not the server. A `metrics` certificate not renewed within 30 days stops scraping; `cert renew` from a daily timer is the operator's to set up (the docs show one). |
| I273 | **Map changes answered once published, as built** (D236; I270): `Meta::published_by(epoch)` waits, as the blocklist did, until the published map's epoch reaches the working map's after the change; it fails with `not_leader` if the member stops leading, or with `NOT_PUBLISHED` after 4 leases and 2 s. The admin loop replies through `reply_published` to `SetDeviceIn`, `SetDeviceWeight`, `SetFullRatios`, `AddNode`, `SetPoolDevice`, `DeletePool`, `RemoveDevice`, `FinishRemoval`, `CreatePool`, `SetPool`, `SetScrub`, `SetDeviceClass`, `SetEndpoints`, `AddDevice` and `Drain`: at once when the call failed or nothing waits to be published, else from a task of its own, so other requests go on. The API maps `NOT_PUBLISHED` to `unavailable` with reason `not_published`. The polls that cancelling an operation and finishing a removal ran for the same purpose are gone. | One publish cycle per call (about 10 ms; up to the commit's time when a device losing a role is fenced first) buys an answer callers can act on. Waiting for the epoch after the change also waits for changes made before it, which is what a reader of the published map sees anyway. | A dry run waits for changes still unpublished before it, harmlessly. `Release` (internal, no epoch in its answer) still answers at once. A caller that sees `not_published` must read the map before trying again: the change may still land. |

| I276 | **Fences no leader published are lifted; stuck PGs are in health** (09-replication.md §8.4; found on the pilot 2026-11-06): a leader fences the devices losing a role before it commits the map that moves it. If it loses the lead before committing, the fence stays on the device, which lifts it only on a map at least that new; the next leader starts from the last committed map, below it, and with nothing to change publishes nothing. Every write to those PGs then failed (`PG fenced for a map change`, or `peering with the PG's members` on primaries whose members were fenced) while health said ok. Now each device sends `Stuck { device, pgs }` (map protocol kind 89) about once a second while it holds a fence over 1 s old for an epoch newer than its map, or leads a PG whose peering has failed for over 5 s with a failure in the last 5 s (epoch 0). A leader whose working map is older than such a fence takes that epoch, which the publisher then publishes. The leader keeps the reports for 3 s; `StuckState`/`StuckStateOk` (90, 91) give them to followers, and an older leader's error leaves the rest of its view. `GetStatus` shows each kind as `HEALTH_ERROR`, naming up to 8 PGs and their devices. | Raising the epoch is always safe: an epoch a past leader did commit is in the next leader's map already, so the condition never holds for it, and no device holds a map it never published. Lifting the fence on the device instead, after a time, isn't: a leader still committing would publish after the old role holder went back to serving. Health could not see either state: both are on the devices. | A fence for a pending publish of the current leader is reported too after 1 s (commits can take up to 5 s); raising the epoch is then a no-op, and health shows the PGs waiting, which is true. Peering failures are seen only by requests: a PG nobody writes to isn't listed. Mixed versions: an older leader refuses `Stuck` as unknown, so its devices keep the old behaviour until it is updated. |

Idle times stay in memory. A new port starts at its link's speed where storage may use it, so senders don't leave it until its first rates. **Senders:** `weighted_addrs_on` gives each endpoint its port's weight (1 while unknown), leaves out those of weight 0, and falls back to the device's address when none is left. **Operator:** `ListPorts`, `SetPortRole` (audited; mgmt's `operate`), `hyperstorctl port list`, `port role`, the cluster file's `port` line. | Stage 3's loop, on the data it needs and nothing more; the map carries only what senders act on. | Hyperstor's clients in other processes on a storage host (`hyperstor-vhost`) count as other traffic on its ports, so one could take back a lent port it uses itself: counting across processes would need a shared counter. | I279 | **Raft calls that outlast the heartbeat no longer start over** (#177; I64, I194, I259): openraft 0.9.25 drops an AppendEntries that takes longer than the heartbeat (50 ms here, I64) and sends it again. Two things made such a call never succeed. (1) glidex-raftstore, at the pinned a57ed85, let one append carry up to 300 entries (openraft's default), and a map entry can be megabytes, so a member catching up after a restart was sent appends that could not arrive in 50 ms. (2) The member transport connected, including the mutual-TLS handshake, inside the call, and dropped the connection with a dropped call (I194), so each retry paid a new handshake. On the pilot, the leader logged `timeout after 50ms when AppendEntries` before each stall (2026-11-03: 32 min; 2026-11-06: about 70 s, terms 34074 to 34122). Now: glidex-raftstore is pinned at 9992197, which sends at most 4 entries per append (glidex D24) and gives up a new leader's first write after 4 election timeouts and a commit after 10 instead of waiting for ever; and each member call runs in a task of its own that holds that member's connection until the answer is read, so a dropped call's handshake and answer still finish and the next call uses the connection. A member gets 2 s to connect and 5 s to answer (60 s for a snapshot) before its connection is dropped, so one that stops answering doesn't hold it. | Keeps the 50 ms heartbeat and D48's failover: raising it, the other way to give appends more time, would slow elections past half a lease. Tests: `replica::tests::a_slow_handshake_outlives_the_call_that_started_it` (a 200 ms handshake against 50 ms calls: fails before this change, no call answered in 2 s), `members::a_rolling_restart_of_the_members_keeps_committing`. | Loopback can't show cross-host delays; a rolling install on the pilot is the real check. An append of 4 entries of a whole large map (about 10 MB each at 65,536 PGs) can still take longer than 50 ms; such entries are written when a cluster or a large pool is created. |

| I281 | **Checkpoints and deferred writes go on the ring with the commit round** (stage 3 of D165; 08-engine.md §6.2; I199): each commit round began with the shard's upkeep in its own device calls, on the reactor's thread: a slice of the fuzzy checkpoint (its pages), a whole checkpoint when the log passed three quarters, a `Take` or a stalled checkpoint (pages, directory, a flush, the superblock and a flush), and deferred in-place writes once more than 16,384 were pending (and, in the daemon's committer, more than 4,096). End-to-end logs from CI showed storage reactors held 400–740 ms with about 1 ms on a CPU and none waiting for one, mostly in backfill and the stray deleter, which fill the log fastest; peers then report the device silent and CLI calls pass their 5 s deadline. **Now:** upkeep stages its writes (`staged`, `staged_ckpt`), and `commit_begin` puts them in the round's writes, which the daemon makes on the ring as before; a checkpoint the round completes is a `Seal`: `Round::take_seal` gives its superblock, which the caller writes and flushes only once the round's writes are durable, and `commit_end` then moves the log's head, frees the replaced pages and directory and lifts a stall. Deferred blocks being applied stay readable from `applying` until the round ends, since a read (or a `Take`'s checksum check) of those blocks would otherwise find the old data on the device. A checkpoint abandoned for lack of room drops its staged pages, whose extents it frees. `Shard::commit`, `checkpoint` and `apply_deferred` make the same writes in the shard's own calls, so the crash test and the benchmarks exercise the new order. The daemon's committer no longer applies deferred blocks itself: `set_pending_limit(4096)` has the next round do it. Tests: `commit_rounds_leave_every_write_to_their_writer` (400 rounds through `commit_begin`/`commit_end` on a 64-block log, slices of 256 bytes and a pending limit of 0: no write or flush in the shard's own calls, more than 5 checkpoints, deferred blocks read correctly while their writes wait, and the state after reopening matches a model; without the `applying` lookup it fails); the crash test passes 3,000 seeds. | One round in flight keeps the order: no checkpoint is named before its pages, directory and the deferred writes before its start are durable, as before; the pages are now flushed with their round's records rather than all at the end, which costs nothing more. Upkeep in the round's writes falls under the I/O timeout (30 s, D166), as the round's own writes do. | Whether this is what held the CI reactors is to be seen in CI's end-to-end runs after merge; still on the reactor: a push's read after `PUSH_READ_TRIES` and `read_layered` for clone parents. |
| I282 | **`hyperstorctl --timeout-secs`, and the end-to-end tests give each call 30 s** (decided with the owner, 2026-11-06): about half of CI's end-to-end failures were a `hyperstorctl` call past its fixed 5 s deadline, across 13 tests, and none reproduced on the dev host, even with four tests on one CPU. On CI's runners each commit waits 110–250 ms for its flush ("waited N ms for the device" in the node logs), so a change that commits to the `.meta` pool several times, or a read behind a purge's deletes, takes longer than 5 s. The harness already waited for health ok and the first map before a test's first call. **Now:** `--timeout-secs N` (else `$HYPERSTOR_TIMEOUT`, else 5) sets each call's deadline; 0 or a bad value is a usage error (exit 2); `join` and `cert renew` keep at least their 10 s. The harness sets `HYPERSTOR_TIMEOUT=30` (`CTL_TIMEOUT`) on every `hyperstorctl` it runs, and a failed call's message names the command and how long it took, so the next slow call says which one it was. | Operators on slow or busy disks see the same: a call that gives up may still be made, which the reference page says. A test-only knob would have hidden it; fewer tests at once would have slowed CI and still failed on one slow call. | Whether the calls that passed 5 s were all `.meta` changes is to be seen from the new messages. |
| I283 | **The connection writer joins only small frames.** `hyperstor_rpc::writer` joins queued frames into one send only while it stays within `BATCH_BYTES` (256 KiB, `next_send`); a frame that doesn't fit waits for the next send and goes out on its own, as queued, without a copy. `chan::Receiver::try_recv` takes one queued value. | The writer appended everything queued behind the first frame into one buffer. During a backfill a node queues 4 MiB pushes faster than 1 GbE drains them, so one poll copied tens to hundreds of MB into fresh memory: in I257's pilot run (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-06), seven holds of 100–125 ms, all on a CPU, in the writer task (I232's log names its spawn). Now each poll copies at most 256 KiB, and a push is never copied; small replies still share a send. | A vectored send (`SendMsg` with an `iovec` per frame) would also save the small copies; not needed for the holds seen. |

In CI, nodes went down this way at startup ("no heartbeat for 859 ms") while devices took 1–3 s to open; with a test's own kill, that made two transitions, so `node N flapping` held health at `warn` and `status --wait 60` (`a_returning_node_goes_down_once`) or the 120 s wait for health ok (`a_device_is_added_to_a_running_node`) ran out. **The tests:** `a_returning_node_goes_down_once` accepted a heartbeat-timeout down only when a node's log said "reactor held", which no longer appears since I281; it now accepts either the peers' reports or the timeout, held 2 s. `a_rolling_restart_of_the_members_keeps_committing` (an older flake, "timed out waiting for the restarted member to catch up") now prints the restarted member's token list, the leader's and every member's log when its catch-up wait fails, as it did only for its write. Test: `a_starting_node_is_down_but_not_flapping`. | A node never heard from is down, not flapping: its first heartbeat should bring it up at once. Its downs after that are held and counted as before, so a flapping node is still damped. | A node that heartbeats, then stalls for the timeout while its devices still open, would be counted; I188 has nodes heartbeat only once every device is open, so it shouldn't happen. |
| I285 | **A push whose object is written at every try holds its key, not the reactor** (I257, I281): `read_for_push` reads on the ring and plans again when a write to its object was queued meanwhile; after `PUSH_READ_TRIES` (3) it read the object in the shard's own calls, holding the reactor for a device read. With every read taking 300 ms (`a_push_is_read_again_when_its_object_is_written`), that held a node's reactor 100–410 ms, its peers reported it silent, and a PG was still backfilling at the test's end (on the dev host, 1 run in 6). **Now** it holds the key as a repair does (`exec_locks`, `hold_key`): a client write to it is refused (`unavailable`, "object being repaired"), which the client retries, an operation list on it waits, and the read is made once more on the ring; the hold is dropped when the read returns, before the push is sent with no wait in between. A key already held (a list deciding, a repair) is waited for up to `REPL_TIMEOUT`, after which the push fails and backfill tries again. `hyperstor_push_holds_total` counts the holds; `held_data`, the reactor-holding read, is gone. Test: `a_push_is_read_again_when_its_object_is_written` now also asks that a key was held (9 per run) and that no node was seen down. | On the primary, only client writes and operation lists change an object, and both respect the hold, as `repair_own` already relies on; the rest are replica-side or the repair itself. A write refused for one read is retried within the client's 20 tries. | The last read in the shard's own calls on the reactor, `read_layered` for a flatten, moved to the ring in I286. |
| I286 | **A flatten reads its parents on the ring, holding the chunk** (I257, I285; 10-volumes.md §10.4): the blocks the clone lacks are gathered into runs under one borrow of the shard, and each run is read with `read_layered_begin` as a background read (`device_read`). `decide_and_apply_with` takes a `decide` that may wait; the key stays held (`exec_locks`) meanwhile, so a client write to the chunk is refused (`unavailable`, "object being repaired") and retried, as during a repair, and then wins over the copy. The fence is checked again after `decide`. Other operation lists keep the synchronous `decide_and_apply`. | `flatten` decided its writes in one synchronous call, reading each missing 4 KiB block with the shard's own `read_layered`, so flattening a chunk held the node's reactor for up to a chunk of device reads: the last synchronous device read on the reactor. `writes_racing_a_flatten_win` writes into the copied runs, with reads slowed to 100 ms, until the flatten ends; a flatten that also copied blocks the clone holds fails it. | A write to a chunk being flattened waits for its runs' reads, retried by the client (20 tries, up to 200 ms apart); a chunk is 1–4 MiB. |
| I287 | **Bucket names per tenant, as built (D243, D244; 14-s3.md §14.9), and the pilot's first gateway** (2026-11-07): a bucket's table in store 1 is the tenant's 4 bytes, big-endian, then the name; `S3::bucket`, `buckets` and `delete_bucket` take the tenant, and `buckets` scans the tenant's prefix. A table whose prefix and record disagree on the tenant is reported corrupt. The gateway's only CreateBucket conflict is `BucketAlreadyOwnedByYou` (409, as before for one's own), and another tenant's bucket is 404 `NoSuchBucket` on every call. **On the pilot:** a gateway on one member with `--s3 --s3-port 8848`, placement `default` = data `nvme`, index `vol`; boto3 made a bucket, wrote and read back 1 KiB, 8 MiB and 90 MiB, and deleted them; the test key was revoked and its secret never printed. **Found:** `hyperstorctl s3 …` writes through the local socket fail on a follower with `not_leader`, so the installer's hint and the guide's `sudo hyperstorctl s3 key create` work only on the leader; `--api <leader>:8850` works. **Fixed:** `hyperstorctl` now sends S3's key and placement writes on to the leader when a member refuses them with `not_leader`, as it does `scrub start`; a refused call wrote nothing, so a key is never made twice. | The pilot had no buckets, so the new table names needed no migration; tenant ids are unique in the cluster, so realms (D248) need no further prefix. |
| I288 | **Realms on S3, as built (D248–D250; 20-tenants.md §24.8)** (2026-11-07): rows of the map service's settings table, as S3's keys are: `realm/<name>` (id, description, who and when), `trealm/<tenant>` (a tenant's realm when not `default`), `prealm/<pool id>` (the realms that may use a pool when not every one), and a realm's placements as `s3rplace/<realm id>/<name>` beside `default`'s `s3place/<name>`, so a member from before realms never reads one. `default` is realm 0 and has no row. Checks across rows run in the write's own transaction: a realm is deleted only when no tenant, pool list or placement names it; a pool isn't taken from a realm whose placement uses it; a placement names only pools its realm may use. A new realm takes the highest id plus one. `RealmService` (`CreateRealm`, `ListRealms`, `DeleteRealm`, `SetTenantRealm`, `SetPoolRealms`), `hyperstorctl realm create|list|delete|tenant|pool`, `--realm` on `s3 placement set|delete`, `hyperstor-s3 --realm` and `hyperstor-install --s3-realm`; `GetS3Gateway` answers only the realm's tenants' keys and its placements. In mgmt's roles, creating realms and setting pools' lists are `Store`, deleting a realm `Store` with a fresh login, moving a tenant `Secure` with a fresh login (it decides which gateway takes its keys). **Not yet:** moving a tenant doesn't check that its buckets' pools suit the new realm (D248): the map service can't see buckets until HS15 counts containers per tenant, so the guide says to move a tenant before it has buckets; the expired-upload cleaner of every gateway still walks every realm's buckets. Open for review. | The settings table already carries S3's keys and placements through Raft and backups, and a separate prefix per kind keeps old members' reads unchanged. Deriving a key's realm from its tenant (D248) means a key needs no realm of its own, and moving a tenant moves its keys. |
| I289 | **S3 stage 4a: conditional requests, multi-object delete, checksums kept (D194; 14-s3.md §14.7)** (2026-11-07): `Condition` (any, absent, exists, one of these ETags) is checked against the entry read, which the batch then asserts, so a write or delete can't race the check. A conditional PUT is checked before the body is taken, so a refused one sends nothing to the chunks, and again as it is made visible; a refusal gives the upload up. `If-None-Match` on a write is `*` only, as S3's; `If-Match` and `If-None-Match` together are refused (`501`). Reads follow RFC 9110, section 13.2.2's order, as S3 documents: `If-Match` holding overrides `If-Unmodified-Since`, `If-None-Match` not matching overrides `If-Modified-Since`, at whole seconds. DeleteObjects takes up to 1000 names, needs `Content-MD5` or a checksum (as S3 requires), deletes 16 at a time in the order asked, answers a version id other than `null` with `NoSuchVersion`, and honours a per-name `ETag`. The `x-amz-checksum-*` value a body was checked against is appended to the index entry and given back on the PUT and, with `x-amz-checksum-mode: ENABLED`, on a whole-object GET or HEAD; an object written without one gives back its CRC32C, which every entry keeps. Request bodies are read by a small XML reader of our own (elements, text, entities, CDATA; a DOCTYPE is refused, so no entity expansion), not a dependency. Open for review. | Asserting the entry read is D194 as settled; checking first saves sending a body that will be refused. Giving back CRC32C when no checksum was sent matches S3, which now always keeps one (CRC64NVME there); SDKs check only the algorithms they have. The two bodies to read (DeleteObjects, CompleteMultipartUpload) are small and fixed in shape. |
| I290 | **S3 stage 4b: copy object, as built (D193; 14-s3.md §14.7)** (2026-11-07): `S3::copy` reads the source entry's bytes a few chunks at a time and writes them as a PUT of the destination, so a copy has its own chunks, its own upload record and the PUT's crash safety; reading and writing take turns, not overlapped yet. The source is any bucket of the same tenant (another tenant's is `NoSuchBucket`, D244), up to 5 GiB as S3's CopyObject. Metadata is copied, or the request's with `REPLACE`; copying an object onto itself without `REPLACE` is refused, as S3 does. `x-amz-copy-source-if-*` are read as a GET's conditions but every failure is 412; the destination's `If-Match`/`If-None-Match` apply as on a PUT. The checksum is the source's (the bytes are the same), or `x-amz-checksum-algorithm`'s, computed as the bytes go by. A source replaced during the copy is still read whole while its chunks linger (15 minutes); later, `503 SlowDown`. A multipart source's copy gets a single-part ETag, as S3 gives. Open for review. | D193 as settled: no shared chunks, so deletes stay simple. Taking turns is simplest; overlapping reads and writes can come when copies are measured. |
| I291 | **S3 stage 4c: multipart uploads, as built (D196; 14-s3.md §14.6, §14.7)** (2026-11-07): an upload is a `u` row (with column `m`, when it began) and its pending record, which now also keeps the content type, metadata and checksum the object will have. Each part gets its own object id, so chunks are named (bucket, part's object id, part number, index), and a part uploaded again never writes over the chunks of the one before, which a reader of nothing yet but a collector may still be deleting. A part's column (`p` + upload id + number + object id) is put as being written, in a batch asserting the upload's record, before any chunk; it is marked done, and earlier parts of its number dropped and queued, in a batch asserting the upload again. Completion reads the upload's columns and, in one batch on the object's row, asserts the upload and every part named, puts the entry (data kind 2: each part's object id, number and size; nothing is copied), removes the upload's columns and queues the chunks of the object replaced and of parts not named. Parts still being written at completion or abort are left with the `u` row for the expiry's clean-up, which runs after their writers must stop (the hour's margin), so no chunk is written after its collection. The ETag is D196's; the entry's CRC32C is combined from the parts' (`crc32c_combine`). Checksums: an upload names an algorithm and S3's type; `COMPOSITE` (any algorithm) is the algorithm over the parts' checksums, shown with `-` and the part count, `FULL_OBJECT` (CRC32, CRC32C) is combined from the parts'; a completion's whole-object checksum is checked before the entry is put. UploadPartCopy reads a range of an object and writes it as a part, with the upload's checksum. ListMultipartUploads reads every `u` row of the bucket and checks each upload's record, so it costs as many as are under way; listings of objects read rows whole, parts' columns included. S3's limits: 10,000 parts, 5 GiB each, 5 MiB but the last; uploads expire after 7 days (§14.6). Open for review. | Part object ids keep chunk names unique without a counter to keep consistent across gateways. Leaving parts being written to the expiry trades an upload row kept up to 7 days for never leaking or deleting a chunk under a writer. Combining CRCs gives every multipart object a whole-object checksum without reading it back. |
| I292 | **A member killed during its first start can't open its store** (found by `a_rolling_restart_of_the_members_keeps_committing`, I284's "older flake"; glidex#317): glidex-raftstore creates `map.redb` and `raft/log.redb` in place with `redb::Database::create`. redb 3.1.3 sizes a new file, writes its header without the magic number and flushes, then writes the magic number and flushes again; on open, a file with data and no magic number is refused (`Database error: I/O error: invalid data`) for good. The test killed the third member about 3 ms into its first start, because a leader among the first two was enough for it to go on: 3 failures in 107 local runs on 2 CPUs, and once in CI; the kept file's first 8 bytes were zero and `raft/log.redb` didn't exist. The test now waits until every member's own store holds the map (`formed`) before restarting any, so it restarts running members as it means to. The fix is glidex-raftstore's (glidex#317, fixed by glidex#318): it builds each store file beside it (`<file>.new`), renames it into place and syncs the directory, and deletes a leftover `.new` at start. Every glidex crate is now pinned at glidex `main` bc1fa83, two commits after v0.0.7 (I280), which changed only those two files of the crates hyperstor uses. `a_member_killed_in_its_first_start_starts_again` leaves half-made `map.redb.new` and `raft/log.redb.new` files where a third member will start (as a kill leaves them now), kills its first start after 0, 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 ms, then starts it: it holds the map, catches up with a write, and the `.new` files are gone. With `hyperstor-meta` built at v0.0.7 instead it fails (the `.new` file stays). | The test was meant to test restarts of running members, not creation. Hyperstor adds no workaround of its own: the store files are glidex-raftstore's. | A member left with a half-made store by a build before this pin still needs its `meta/` directory deleted by hand: a store without the magic number never held data, and with no Raft log the member never voted (glidex D18). The kills in the test land in the creation window only sometimes; the leftover files are what it checks every time. With the wait, the test passed 249 of 250 runs on 2 CPUs; the other timed out in the new wait itself (40 s, before any kill, the run right after a release build), for a reason that run didn't show; the test now prints every member's log there too. |
| I293 | **`hyperstor-install` reads every binary before writing any, and restarts services on replaced binaries** (2026-11-07). **Found** rolling #219 out to the pilot: the release bundle lacked `hyperstor-smart`; the installer, writing binaries one at a time, replaced six on one member, then failed on the seventh and restarted nothing. Its next run found the six unchanged and restarted nothing either, so the member's services kept running the old binaries (their `/proc/PID/exe` "(deleted)") until restarted by hand. **Fixed:** every binary is read before any is written, and a missing one fails with "nothing was installed"; and on a real run, each running service whose main process's executable isn't the installed file is restarted with the others, whatever left it so. Socket-activated `hyperstor-smart` keeps no process and isn't checked. | Reading first makes the binaries all-or-nothing; checking the running processes makes a later run finish what an earlier one left, which the installer's convergence promises. |

| I298 | **Per-bucket counts, as built (D257; 25-kv-ranges.md §28.9; 14-s3.md §14.6; HS15 stage a)** (2026-11-07): `Batch::count(old, new)` puts the row's counts in column `0xFF` (left out of `row` and `scan`) and the difference as `c` + random id in the range, made once per `apply` so an attempt sent again puts the same key. `Store::counts` walks a table's ranges by their own `end`, each range's deltas read with its attributes (`jobs::counts_of`), and starts again after 50 ms on a range that is unfinished or doesn't start where expected (up to 200 times, then `Exhausted`). The primary folds a range's deltas once it has 64 (`FOLD_AT`, seen by `hyperstord`'s look at the range after writes and on each sweep): each delta asserted (`AssertKv`) and deleted, their sum put, with the epoch and `serving` asserted. A split's copy sums the `0xFF` columns it moves; the new range opens with that delta, and the lower range's shrink puts its negative beside the `KvDelRange` (counted again from the frozen rows when a split is taken up after its new range opened). A merge puts the right range's sum as `c` + the right range's id, which an undone merge deletes. S3 counts each object row as 1 and its latest entry's size, in the three batches that change `l` (PUT, CompleteMultipartUpload, DELETE); copies go through the PUT's. The bucket record gains `counted` (true for buckets made from now on); the map service's bucket list and show count each bucket (a failed count shows as not known, not as an error), and `S3Bucket` gains `counted`, `objects` and `bytes`, shown in `hyperstorctl s3 bucket list` (`-` when not known) and the UI's Buckets card. Tests: `kv_jobs_finish_or_undo_from_every_step` checks the counts after each split and merge stopped at every step and taken up, and after an undone merge; `kv_ranges_split_and_merge_on_their_own` writes with assertions, as S3 does, so that a write sent again after a node's death conflicts instead of counting twice, checks the counts after growth, jobs left half done and shrinkage, and that the primaries folded (fewer than 128 deltas left of about a thousand); `s3_objects_match_a_model` checks each bucket's counts against the model; the end-to-end test checks a bucket's one object and its bytes in `hyperstorctl`. Each count check was seen to fail with its code broken: the split's negative, the merge's delta, the undo's removal and the recount on take-up; with folding off, the self-splitting test fails too, its table never merging back into one range, since unfolded deltas count in a range's bytes. | One key per write in its own range keeps the batch atomic on one object, so a count can't drift from the rows; the walk by the ranges' own bounds is what makes reading exact while splits run, without a snapshot. | Uploads under way (parts' bytes) aren't counted. A gateway of an earlier build writing to a counted bucket leaves its counts wrong: upgrade every gateway together. Listing every bucket walks every bucket's ranges, one read each; with many buckets that is slow (no cache). A range stuck mid-job, until its primary takes it up (the sweep, 10 s), makes counting its table wait or fail. Open for review. |
| I299 | **Tenants on volumes and the realm checks, as built (D258, D248, D249; 20-tenants.md §24.2, §24.8; HS15 stage b)** (2026-11-07): the volume header gains its tenant, appended after the trash's fields (a header without it reads as 0, none); `create_volume_for` takes one, clones take their volume's, and copies and moves keep it (they copy the header). `CreateVolume` takes `tenant` and is refused (`pool_not_in_realm`, audited) in a pool the tenant's realm may not use; `Volume` answers it; `hyperstorctl create-volume --tenant N`, and `volumes` shows a TENANT column (`-` for none). **Moving a tenant** (`SetTenantRealm`) first reads its containers from the cluster, as D254 reads buckets: its buckets (index and data pools) and its volumes in every data pool, those in the trash too; the realms write then refuses (`pool_not_in_realm`) if the new realm may not use one of their pools, checked against the realms as they are in that write. **Taking a pool from a realm** (`SetPoolRealms`) reads every tenant's and refuses (`pool_in_use`) if a container of a tenant whose realm loses the pool is in it (volumes of tenant 0 are `default`'s). Tests: `realms::tests::containers_stay_in_their_realms_pools`; `headers_round_trip` (a header with a tenant, and one from before); the realm end-to-end test makes a tenant's volume in its realm's pool (another realm's tenant refused), clones it (the clone has the tenant), and is refused moving the tenant for its volume, then for its bucket, and taking the pool from its realm once no placement names it. | Reading containers when asked keeps nothing new in Raft, as for buckets (D254); checking again inside the write makes a concurrent pool-list change safe. | The read and the write aren't one transaction with bucket and volume creation: a bucket or volume made in the moment between them, through a gateway or a call that still sees the old realm, isn't checked. | I300 | **Large payloads are neither copied into frames nor out of them** (HS5b, D41; supersedes I23's "copied into each outgoing frame"). Three copies went: (1) `TcpStream::read_exact` split off the unread part of its buffer at every short read and joined it back, so a frame of N bytes arriving in pieces of P copied about N²/2P bytes (a 4 MiB push in 64 KiB pieces: about 128 MiB); it now reads in place. (2) `read_frame` split the payload off the body, a copy of the payload; a payload of 16 KiB or more (`OWN_READ`) is now read into a buffer of its own. (3) The writer queues `Frame`s: a header and body, and a payload kept as it is (`Frame::owned`, or `Frame::shared` with an `Rc` for the frames to every replica), sent after the header by one `sendmsg` with two iovecs (`TcpStream::write_all2`). Reads answered by a node send their data as owned; replicated writes and `ReplExec` share one payload among the replicas, and the engine takes the one copy left (`Rc::unwrap_or_clone`; none when the payload was small and already copied into the frames). Payloads under 16 KiB are still copied into their frame, which keeps them joinable with other small frames (I283). | `hyperstor-bench cluster mem 1` on one HGX host (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-07): 1 MiB writes +15%, 1 MiB reads +6% (QD1) to +25% (QD4); 4 KiB unchanged, within the run-to-run range (a first comparison that showed QD1 2% slower had its two builds on different commits of main). | Left for HS5b: the client's write payload is still copied into its frame (`Conn::start` takes a slice); the engine copies write data into an aligned buffer (I12) and receive buffers are zeroed before each read; small frames are still joined by copying. Registered buffers and `SEND_ZC` are the next steps. |
