(3) In `an_empty_node_is_refilled_without_flapping` a runner's shared disk took over 2 s for three backfill reads in a row, so D190's rule failed device 1000 and the pools stayed degraded; the end-to-end tests start `hyperstord` with `--read-timeout-ms 10000` (`NODE_ARGS`, beside the 600 ms peer timeout). Tests: the three named. | The purger's rule (one at a time, then two reports) was right; it lost track of its own purge. | I303 | **Roles on a realm, as built (D252, D260, D262–D265; 20-tenants.md §24.8; HS15 stage d)** (2026-11-07): **In mgmt:** a grant is `role` or `role@realm` (`policy::grant`); `policy::reach` gives a call's reach: the cluster when the cluster-wide roles allow it, else the realms whose grants (with the cluster-wide roles) allow it, for the calls of `policy::IN_REALM` only; a refusal is evaluated on every grant at once, so a stale login still asks for step-up. | I304 | **CRC32C at hardware speed: `crc32c` 0.6.9** (HS5b, D41). Every block's CRC (`hyperstor_codec::crc32c`, the engine, the protocol, S3) ran at 5.7 GB/s on an EPYC 7742: 184 µs per MiB, against 27 µs for a memcpy. In 0.6.8 the three-way interleaved loop was compiled without SSE4.2, so each `crc32` instruction was a call to a helper that couldn't be inlined, and the interleaving was lost. 0.6.9 adds `#[target_feature(enable = "sse4.2")]` to the loops (its only code change, read before taking it): 41 µs per MiB (25 GB/s), and the workspace now asks for at least 0.6.9. The same speed came from building with `-C target-feature=+sse4.2`, which would also raise the hardware the binaries need; not needed now. | A replicated write takes CRCs of its data on the client, twice on the primary (checked on arrival, then computed again by the engine) and twice on each replica; reads, on the engine's check and the client's. End to end (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-07): 1 MiB writes +29%, 1 MiB reads +48% to +60%, 4 KiB +1% to +4%. Found by pricing each step of the path, since `perf` isn't open to users on the host. | The engine still computed the CRCs that came with the data (once per node); I306 passes them through. |
| I305 | **Crash tests with a device that lies (#241; 08-engine.md §7.3)** (2026-11-08): `FaultDev::misstep` makes one block's write lost or stored at another block after the device acknowledged it (`WriteFault`); the `damaged_*` crash tests run random batches, a last list (the fence), then damage a block on the media (the log ring, a shard or device superblock, the data region) or let the armed write go wrong, and reopen. The shard must refuse to open, or hold every acknowledged change but the fence's, a block reading as a checksum error and never as other data. Over 300 seeds each: a device superblock copy and data or checkpoint blocks never lost anything (17 checksum errors, 1 refusal: #243); damaged log blocks lost acknowledged lists silently 17 times, lost and misdirected writes 7 and 12 times, every one a write meant for the log ring (#242); a damaged shard superblock opened the shard empty twice (#253). Those four tests and the stricter #243 one are `#[ignore]`d with their issue until fixed; the fixes are D266–D271. | The fault model research names (Ganesan et al., FAST '17; Alagappan et al., FAST '18), against the engine's own model; the fence is the one list whose loss can't be told from a crash. |
| I306 | **The engine keeps the CRCs a write came with** (HS5b, D41). `Op::Write` takes `crcs: Option<Vec<u32>>`, one per 4 KiB block; with them the engine stores those in its index instead of computing them from the data, and refuses a list whose count is wrong (`OpError::Crcs`). A storage node passes them for the three ways data arrives with CRCs, each after `verify_crcs` has checked them against the data: a client's `Write` on the primary, `Repl` on a replica, and a recovery `Push`. Operation lists (`Exec`, `ReplExec`), objects decoded from a fetch, and the engine's own writes pass `None` and are computed as before. Tests: `a_write_keeps_the_crcs_it_came_with` (a write split across many runs of a fragmented shard, a deferred overwrite, reopen; fails when the CRCs are filed against the wrong run), `a_write_whose_crcs_disagree_is_refused_or_caught_on_read` (a wrong count refused; a wrong CRC stored as given and caught on read; fails when the engine recomputes), `data_whose_crcs_disagree_is_refused_at_every_way_in` (`Write`, `Repl` and `Push` with another payload's CRCs are refused with `Checksum`, and the data reads as before); the crash test's random writes now hold a byte of their own in each block, and half carry their CRCs. | Each node read the data twice for one result: once to check the CRCs on arrival, once in the engine to compute the same values (both 4 KiB blocks from a block-aligned offset). Now once: 1 MiB writes +5%, reads and 4 KiB unchanged (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-08). **Integrity:** stronger end to end. Data damaged in memory after the check (a bit flip, a buffer reused too early) used to get a CRC computed from the damaged bytes, which then matched forever; it now keeps the client's CRC, so the next read or scrub finds it and repairs it from a replica. A client still can't store data with CRCs that don't match, since the check on arrival stays. | The risk moves to bookkeeping: a bug that files CRCs under the wrong blocks fails loudly (reads return `Checksum`), but the primary sends the same CRCs to every replica, so it could hit every copy at once, where independently computed CRCs couldn't; the tests above guard the splitting and deferred paths. Next: check on arrival while copying into the engine's aligned buffer (I12), so each node reads the data once. Open for review. |
| I307 | **The other four `manage` flakes on the board** (2026-11-08; branch `fix/manage-flakes-2`). Three were fixed before: `a_returning_node_goes_down_once` and `a_device_is_added_to_a_running_node` failed on commits without I284, which names both, and `snapshots_through_hyperstorctl` ("client-side deadline exceeded" while the map service elected itself) on one without I282's 30 s per call; neither has failed in CI since. The fourth, `a_cache_pool_keeps_its_data_across_splits_and_restarts`, failed with I282 and without I284 at its first read: node 0, its device taking 3.6 s to open, was down from the start ("no heartbeat for 859 ms") when the test made and wrote pool 3, so PGs 3.5 and 3.6 were "written while waiting" and moved, starting empty; node 0 then came up and the data written there was gone. I284 still marks such a node down (only not flapping), so the test now waits for health ok (`status --wait 60`) before making the pool. Test: the one named. | A cache PG written while its node is down loses that data when the node returns (D188, D189): right for the product, wrong for a test that means to check restarts, not a start. | None. |
| I308 | **Damage to the log or a shard superblock refuses the shard (#242, #253; D266–D268; 08-engine.md §7.3)** (2026-11-08): a scan that stops at a bad record reads the rest of the ring for a valid header of the current salt at its own position, and refuses if it finds one; a block of the last group's data that fails its checksum and holds a log record of the current salt refuses too (a misdirected write of the next record, seed 1835 of 3,000, which `first_unwritten` took for a torn group); and when one superblock copy is unreadable, a whole record under another salt where the scan stops refuses (the salt solved from the header checksum, 32 equations over GF(2), and checked against the payload's). A full shard that keeps its salt used to leave the records it dropped readable; they are now erased before the fresh checkpoint. Over 10,000 seeds each, nothing is lost silently: log blocks 418 refusals, lost writes 1,900 checksum errors and 362 refusals, misdirected writes 1,928 and 329, shard superblocks 81 refusals, data blocks 582 and 5; 5,000 seeds of plain crashes refuse none. The look-ahead reads the ring at every open: 0.43 s for a 1 GiB ring with `O_DIRECT`, after reading it into one 16 MiB buffer (1.1 s with a buffer each, 2.7 s in 1 MiB reads; 02-benchmarks.md). | Only the last round can be torn (I199), so a valid record after a bad one, or a record of a salt no superblock names, is proof of damage, not a crash. Refusing loses no data the cluster holds; truncating lost it silently. The superblock mirrors (D267, revised) and the hash chain (D266) come in the incompatible format step. |
| I309 | **The v0.0.8 shard format, and converting to it (#242, #253, #247; D266, D267, D270, D274; 08-engine.md §7.1, §7.3)** (2026-11-08): one incompatible flag, `INCOMPAT_SHARD_V2`, which `format` sets by default (`Features::current`). Shard superblocks get a new magic (`HSSHRD02`) and name the chain value at their head; each is written to its slot and its mirror in the last two blocks of the log ring, in one batch after the round's flush (`Round::take_seal` now returns both writes), and open takes the newest valid of the four copies. Record headers grow to 48 bytes (version 2): the previous record's payload checksum and a word kept zero; a whole record whose `prev` doesn't match refuses the shard. Dense block maps keep their base with them (`BlockMap::Dense(slots, base)`): 0 in the first format, the data start in the second; `Index::rebase` repacks them. `hyperstor_engine::upgrade` opens a device of the first format, then for each shard (`Shard::upgrade`) checkpoints until head and tail meet, shrinks the ring by two blocks, rebases the dense maps, marks every page and checkpoints under a new salt; then writes the flag, copy 1 then copy 0. A crash before the flag opens the first format: its superblocks never decode the new magic, and the pages they name are untouched. The daemon still only opens devices; `device upgrade` (D274) comes next. Crash tests at 3,000 seeds: the second format (default and dense maps), the first format, and conversions during recovery with power lost at random points (2,639 done, 1,701 interrupted), all recovering every acknowledged change; a damaged shard superblock now leaves the shard intact in 3,000 of 3,000 (refused 2.7% before, I308). Converting rewrites the whole index once. | The crash test computed the largest list after reopening; a conversion takes two blocks from the ring, so a list taken before the crash was left out of the candidates: the bound is now read before the crash. A test asserting free space after a burst of writes is now exact about what it means (the held blocks return when the read ends), since checkpoint timing moves with the ring size. |
| I310 | **mgmt follows the leader and serves a proxy (D276, D277; 19-administration.md §23.7)** (2026-11-08): `members.rs` keeps the order of the members' API addresses and `Members::each` calls them in turn until one answers other than `unavailable`; a call served by any but the first asks the member list again at once. People's rows, the API proxy, the audit shipper, the dashboard's status and PG reads, and the realm check all go through it. `front.rs` holds the proxy rules: `Cidr`, `via_proxy`, `client` and `on_cert`. With `--proxy-ca`, rustls's `WebPkiClientVerifier` asks for a client certificate but allows none; a connection that completed with one presented it from that CA. The served certificate's DNS names are re-read with it. The installer passes `--mgmt-hosts`, `--mgmt-proxy-ca`, `--mgmt-trusted-proxies` and `--mgmt-proxy-only`, saved in `install.conf` only once one is set. | On the pilot, each host's mgmt had only its own member (`--api` one address), so on a follower a login, which writes a session, and every change failed. `a_followers_mgmt_reaches_the_leader` claims the first administrator and logs in through a follower's mgmt as the installer configures it; with the member list ignored, the claim never succeeds. `mgmt_serves_a_proxy_with_an_organizations_certificates` serves an organization's chain with a PKCS#8 key, lets its name in, refuses a stranger under `--proxy-only`, takes a proxy by its certificate, refuses another CA's, and believes `CF-Connecting-IP` only from the proxy; with proxies' certificates ignored, or the certificate's names, it fails. | An OIDC login's state (PKCE verifier, nonce) is in the memory of the mgmt that began it (`glidex-auth`'s `OidcState`): behind a load balancer over several hosts, `/auth/oidc/` needs session affinity, or the state could move to the map service (a glidex change). `--proxy-ca` is read at start; a new CA needs a restart. |
| I311 | **Frame headers carry the frame's version, 2, not the protocol's (13-protocol.md §13.1; D261)** (2026-11-08): `Header::encode` wrote `VERSION` in every frame and `Header::decode` refused any other, so raising it to 3 for `Msg::Host` (D261) made every frame of the new build unreadable to older peers, and theirs to it. `FRAME_VERSION` (2) is now written; 2 and 3 are read (builds since D261 wrote 3). `VERSION` (3) is only what `Hello` and `HelloOk` exchange, and `Hello` names `OLDEST_VERSION` (2) as its least. | **Found** rolling main d659f80 out to the pilot (2026-11-08): a follower host, upgraded first; its new node's every connection to the old members was reset at its first frame, so it never had a map, never heartbeat, and stayed down about four minutes, every PG degraded, until the host was rolled back. Reproduced off the pilot with three hosts of 7b9cb2f, each a member and a node, and one host upgraded: the node alone fails, the member alone doesn't. With the fix, all three hosts upgraded in turn, followers first, each node staying up and an I/O test after. `frame_headers_stay_readable_by_older_builds` checks the header's bytes are a version-2 build's. | A rolling install from an older build has no test in CI: it needs the older binaries. The rehearsal used for this (three hosts of the previous build, upgraded in turn) could become an end-to-end test given a release's binaries. |
| I312 | **The backup device superblock (#246, D271; 08-engine.md §7.1)** (2026-11-08): `COMPAT_BACKUP_SUPER` (compatible, bit 0), set by `Features::current`. Formatting leaves the last block out of the regions and writes the superblock there with the two copies; every rewrite (recording the cluster, converting) writes copy 1, copy 0, then the backup, each flushed. `info` reads the backup when neither copy decodes, unless one is of a newer version (that build wrote it, and the backup may be older), and only a backup that has the flag and the device's size. Converting (D274) adds it when the last block is past every region or is the last region's and free (`Shard::give_up_last_block` reserves it and shortens the region, which the rewritten superblock records); otherwise the device goes on without. Blank copies with a shard superblock at block 2 or 3 read as `Corrupt`, so `hyperstord` (which formats blank files) and `format-device` (whose signature check reads `info`) refuse instead of formatting. Tests: both copies damaged, the backup serves; it damaged too, refused; all three wiped, refused, not blank; a conversion adds a backup that serves alone; crash suites at 1,000 seeds pass. | Falling back past a copy of a newer version would have read an older backup of a device a newer build had changed: found by the existing test of a newer version. |
| I313 | **A detach waits up to 2 s for a hypervisor to hang up (#265; D256; 11-frontends.md §11.1)** (2026-11-08): `hyperstor-vhost` checks the disk's connection every 50 ms, on its reactor, for up to `HANGUP_GRACE` (2 s) before answering `busy`. The hang-up shows (`POLLRDHUP`) only once the last copy of the hypervisor's end is closed, and that comes late in two cases: a hypervisor just signalled closes it as its process exits, which takes a large VM a while; and a process forked meanwhile (another test's child, in CI) holds a copy until it execs. Before, an orchestrator that stopped a VM and detached at once could be told `busy` and reach for `force`. The tests' retry helper (`detach_gone`, #237) is gone: the service waits. A detach of a disk whose hypervisor stays connected now answers after 2 s. | The tests had been fixed by retrying; the same race in production needed the wait in the service. |
| I314 | **The daemon's cluster tests run at most 8 clusters at once (#264)** (2026-11-08): `crates/hyperstor-daemon/tests/cluster.rs` takes one of `CLUSTERS_AT_ONCE` (8) places before a cluster starts and frees it when the cluster goes. The test runner starts as many tests as the host has cores; on a 256-core host all 77 ran at once, each several nodes' reactors and a map service on sub-second timeouts, and most full runs failed one of them by timing (a node seen down, a connection reset, a lease late, a PG with two copies), a different test each time, while CI (about 8 at once) passed. With the cap, 4 of 4 full runs pass, in 56 s instead of 28. Copies are also now checked against the map published before `Cluster::stop` (`Cluster::published`): stopping the nodes one by one can take longer than the down timeout, and the map service then moves the first ones' PGs before it stops. | Raising the tests' timeouts would hide real stalls the tests are there to find; the host's parallelism was the change. |
| I315 | **Dependencies brought up to date, and `cargo audit` in CI** (2026-11-08; branch `deps/buf-1.75`): `buf` 1.75.0, protobuf-es 2.17.0 (the generated TypeScript changes only its header), Connect-ES 2.3.0, `cargo update`'s 48 semver-compatible crates, Playwright 1.65.0, `bun` 1.4.4, `boto3` 1.43.126, and `upload-artifact` v7 and `download-artifact` v8 (v4 ran on the deprecated Node 20). A new CI job, `audit`, runs `cargo audit` (0.22.2) against RustSec's database; `.cargo/audit.toml` ignores two advisories, each with its reason: RUSTSEC-2023-0071 (`rsa`, the Marvin attack) and RUSTSEC-2026-0387 (`crypto-bigint` 0.5), both pulled in only by `jsonwebtoken`'s `rust_crypto` backend, through glidex's `glidex-auth` (which only checks an identity provider's tokens with its public keys) and `hyperstor-test` (ES256 tokens signed with a key made per run). | Compatible updates and CI pins in one change, checked by one CI run; an advisory ignored only with a reason, so a new one fails CI. | Since done, the whole list: `redb` 4.5 with the glidex pin (I320; glidex's `b6028568`; this note first said it was still to come, from the pin of the time), the UI's `vite` 8, `tailwindcss` 4 and TypeScript 7 (I325), and `rand` 0.10, `md-5` 0.11 and `base64` 0.23 (2026-11-09; branch `deps/rand-md5-base64`): `md-5` and `base64` in `hyperstor-s3`, `hyperstor-daemon`'s tests and `hyperstor-test` needed no code change; rand 0.10 has `SmallRng` without a feature, and its `random`, `random_range` and the rest are `RngExt`'s, while `Rng` is now the generator's own trait (a bound). `rand` 0.8 stays for `openraft` and `base64` 0.22 for `jsonwebtoken`, `reqwest`, `connectrpc` and `pem`. The two ignores went the same day: the glidex pin moved to `eb1c545e` (glidex's `jsonwebtoken` on `aws_lc_rs`; its other changes since `bc1fa833` are packaging metadata and `glidex-tls` on `sha2` 0.11) and `hyperstor-test` switched to `aws_lc_rs` in the same commit, because `jsonwebtoken` panics on its first signature check when a build has both backends. `rsa`, `p256`, `p384` and `crypto-bigint` are no longer in `Cargo.lock`. |
| I316 | **OIDC logins finish on any mgmt (D278; 19-administration.md §23.9.1)** (2026-11-08): glidex #381 adds `OidcState::begin` and `finish` and makes `Pending` public and serializable, with no `Debug`; `start` and `callback` are built on them. mgmt pins `glidex-auth` alone at #381's merge commit `f891a028`, as `glidex-authd` doesn't depend on it; the others stay at `eb1c545e`. `People::keep_login` and `take_login` keep the row as start time, nonce and the sealed JSON; the row's key is the AEAD's associated data, so a row moved to another state's key won't open. A wrong browser, or none, gets "belongs to another browser" and has used the row up, as `glidex-auth`'s own `take` does. | Answers I310's follow-up. `logins_begun_are_kept_sealed_and_taken_once` shows the row holds no verifier or claim in the clear, opens once and only for its browser, and that old rows are swept. `people_log_in_with_an_identity_provider` begins a login, restarts mgmt (its memory gone), and finishes the login on the new process, then refuses a replay. With the state in memory, as before, the restarted mgmt has none to finish. | Each login now costs mgmt two to three map service writes (keep, delete, plus sweeping). |
| I317 | **The web UI's footer names mgmt's build (19-administration.md §23.7)** (2026-11-08, the owner's request: as glidex's footer): `hyperstor-mgmt`'s `build.rs` reads git at build time (`describe --tags --exact-match`, else the last tag and `rev-list --count` since it, the branch, the commit) and `/auth/whoami` returns it as `build`; `Footer.tsx` links the release (or GitHub's comparison of the release with the commit), and the commit. `HYPERSTOR_BUILD_TAG`, `_RELEASE`, `_BRANCH`, `_COMMIT` and `_REPO` override them for builds outside a checkout. The repository is never read from the checkout's remote, whose URL can carry a credential. | glidex takes its footer from the UI's bundle (Vite at build time); here mgmt's binary carries it, because the pilot's UI files and binaries are staged separately, and "installed" means the binaries. It is only in `/auth/whoami`, so a person must be logged in to see it, as in glidex. `the_build_names_its_commit_and_release` checks the shape; the UI e2e checks the footer's commit link. | No "modified" marker as glidex's: `build.rs` reruns when HEAD, a branch or a tag moves, not on every edit, so it couldn't say so reliably. The other binaries (hyperstord, the members) can be another build during a rolling install; the footer names mgmt's alone. |
| I318 | **`a_token_waits_for_the_first_map` asks again after a full wait** (2026-11-08; branch `fix/members-token-flake`): in CI on 2026-11-08 the token asked for while the second member started waited out the server's 4 s (`FORMING_WAIT`, I275) and was refused `not_formed`: electing and committing the first map took longer on the runner. The test now asks again after such a refusal, for up to 60 s, and checks that each refused call waited at least 3 s, so it still shows the wait rather than an immediate refusal. Test: the one named. | The server's wait is for an operator right after installing; a runner slower than that is the test's problem, not a reason to wait longer for everyone. | None. |
| I319 | **`hyperstorctl device upgrade` (D274; 08-engine.md §7.1)** (2026-11-08): nodes report each device's shard format with its space (`Msg::DeviceFormat`, kind 94, after `DeviceKind`; a map service from before answers it as invalid). The leader keeps the reports out of the map, as it keeps space: a map that carried them would be format 20 the moment the first v0.0.8 node reported, during the rolling upgrade, and nodes before it can't read that. `UpgradeDevices` (Write, `Operate` with step-up in mgmt, audited) puts the devices in the map's `upgrades` (map format 20, written only while one is asked) and is refused while any device asked hasn't reported a format, which only nodes of v0.0.8 or later do: the operator can't make the map unreadable to an older node by mistake. A node whose device the published map asks to convert opens it with `hyperstor_engine::upgrade`; one without room for the conversion's checkpoint stays in the first format and opens as it was, saying so. The leader drops a device from `upgrades` when it reports format 2. Status shows each device's format (`format`, `upgrade_asked`) when the leader answers; `nodes` gains a FORMAT column, blank on another member (a follower's view of the leader is a message older members decode, so it isn't extended). Tests: a rolling restart of four in-process nodes converts every device of the first format and reads back what they held; the map service's refusal; map format 20; and end to end, installed devices report format 2 and `device upgrade --all` says so. | The in-process test first restarted nodes before the request was published and they didn't convert: the API answers once the map is published, so the test now waits for that too. |
| I320 | **glidex pinned at its main `4d1f5b13`, with `redb` 4.5 (D20)** (2026-11-09; branch `deps/glidex-main`): every glidex crate, `glidex-auth` included (it was at `f891a028` for #381), moves to one revision. Since `eb1c545e` glidex has upgraded redb from 3 to 4.5 (glidex #382), trimmed and truncated the Raft log with `retain_in` (#384), and taken `thiserror` 2, `nix` 0.31, `getrandom` 0.4, `base64` 0.23 and `dirs` 7 (#371–#375). `hyperstor-meta` and `hyperstor-bench` move to `redb` 4.5 in the same change: `hyperstor-meta` uses `redb::ReadableTable` on tables from `glidex-raftstore`, so the two must be one redb, and `Cargo.lock` now holds only 4.5.0. No hyperstor code changes. | One redb for the map service's whole store. glidex #382 found that redb 3.1 and 4.5 both write file-format version 3: 4.5 opens files 3.1 wrote, 3.1 opens files 4.5 wrote, and both after an unclean shutdown, so the pilot's members open their stores as they are and rolling back needs nothing. Raft snapshots are glidex's own row stream, so members on either build replicate during a rolling install. | Closes I315's `redb` 4 item. glidex #382 measured point reads +23% and durable commits −11% (p99 +0.3 ms); the map service's commits are not measured on 4.5 here. |
| I321 | **Two checkpoint directory copies, and checkpoint pages scrubbed (#243, D269; 08-engine.md §7.3, 28-scrub.md §31.2)** (2026-11-09): every checkpoint writes its directory twice, in blocks of their own (`Ckpt::copy`); without room for the copy, or past the 200 runs a superblock names, it goes on without one rather than stall. The superblock names the copy after its own checksum (`HSCKCPY1`, the runs, a checksum of their own), so a build from before copies reads the superblock as before and leaves the copy's blocks free; a copy whose blocks something else holds when this build opens the shard again, or whose checksum fails, is read as none. `open` reads the copy when the first fails its checksum or can't be read, and logs it; its fresh checkpoint writes both again. `Shard::scrub_checkpoint_page(i)` reads one page and checks it against the directory; a damaged or unreadable one is marked changed and a checkpoint written at once (one that is running is finished first: its pages were chosen before the mark). `scrub_checkpoint_directory` reads both copies and writes a checkpoint if one is damaged or the checkpoint has no copy (a device's first pass after this build gives every idle shard one). `hyperstord` runs one task per device: each page, then the directory, once per the shortest deep scrub period of the pools that scrub (24 h × 7 by default), spread over it, read on the reactor between commit rounds; finds are logged and counted in `hyperstor_checkpoint_damage_total`. | A damaged directory costs one 4 KiB read of the copy instead of the device. Pages are about 128 KiB, so a pass reads about the index once a week by default, one page every few seconds at most. Naming the copy after the checksum needs no device flag: the superblock says whether a copy exists, and the older build ignores it, as D269's "compatible flag" meant. Crash test (5,000 seeds each): a damaged block of either directory copy, 0 refused; a page damaged and scrubbed before the restart, 0 refused; a page damaged and not scrubbed, refused (1,173) or intact, never silent. | D269 says "behind a compatible flag"; built without a device flag, for the reason given. Recovering only the PGs of a damaged page stays D268's later step. |
| I322 | **Freed space discarded after each checkpoint (#245, D275; 08-engine.md §7.4)** (2026-11-09): the engine queues every extent a commit or a named checkpoint frees (`Discards`: `next` → `sealing` when a checkpoint begins → `ready` once it is named, each merged runs); `Shard::take_discards(max)` hands out the parts of ready runs still free, at most `max` and an eighth of the free space, reserved in the allocator until `discards_done`; `set_discard(true)` right after opening (nothing in the log since the open's checkpoint) queues the whole free space too, so space freed before a crash or by an older build is discarded once. `BlockDev::discard`: `BLKDISCARD` on a block device, `fallocate(PUNCH_HOLE | KEEP_SIZE)` on a file, zeros in memory and on the crash test's device (also in its unflushed cache). `hyperstord` runs one thread per device for discards, in batches of at most 256 MiB with 10 ms between them; a device answering `EOPNOTSUPP` or `ENOTTY` is logged once and left alone; another error retries after 60 s. The per-device setting is `hyperstord --no-discard ID,…|all`. Metrics `hyperstor_discarded_bytes_total` and `hyperstor_discard_queued_bytes`. | The setting is where the drive is: a slow-discarding drive is a property of one host's hardware, and the node's options already name its devices; a setting in the map would need a format, an RPC and the UI for a rare case. A batch of at most an eighth of the free space keeps writes going while it runs (the first daemon test run, with the whole free space in one batch, refused writes with no space). The crash test discards at random points on every run (about 12.5 million blocks over 2,000 seeds, 0 silent losses); discarding queued runs without checking they are still free fails it at seed 0; making freed blocks ready at once, without waiting for a checkpoint, passes, as the reasoning above says. | D275's *why* says a block freed after the last checkpoint might be needed by replay; it isn't (freeing waits for durability, and only unacknowledged commits are dropped). Waiting for the checkpoint is kept as decided: it batches discards into long runs. Not measured: overwrite throughput and p99 with and without discards needs an NVMe drive the engine owns (G58, with G57's host). |
| I323 | **Checksummed writes in operation lists (#244, D279; 13-protocol.md §13.1)** (2026-11-09): `WireOp::WriteCrcs { off, data, crcs }` (tag 16), protocol version 4. `Client::write_op(off, data)` takes the CRCs; the S3 gateway sends each chunk so. On the primary, `exec` checks every `WriteCrcs` (whole blocks, CRCs matching: else `Invalid` or `Checksum`) before deciding, `engine_op` passes the CRCs to `Op::Write` (I306), and the effects go to replicas as they came; `repl_exec` checks them again. `Conn` keeps the version its peer's `HelloOk` answered (`version()`, 0 before), the client records it, and a storage node now greets its peers with `Hello` (instance 0), so `decide_and_apply_with` sends a replica below 4 the list with plain writes (`WireOp::without_crcs`). A primary below 4 refuses the list as malformed (`Invalid`); the client then sends it once more as plain writes. The rig's `faults::Older` can make a node speak version 3 (`set_version`): it answers `HelloOk` so and reads checksummed writes as unknown. | Closes #244 for the data it left: volume writes kept the client's CRCs since I306; S3 objects now do too, from the gateway to every copy's media. `cluster::checksummed_writes_reach_nodes_of_the_protocol_before` (three of four nodes restarted as version 3) fails without the client's retry (malformed list) and without the primary's plain writes to older replicas (the replica refuses). | Flatten's effects (data read back from the engine, checked then) stay plain writes. The decision log holds two points numbered 242 (#248's device, and the OIDC login state), from sessions that ran at once; left as they are. |
| I324 | **A client's write is sent from its own buffer** (HS5b, D41; follows I300). `Client::write` cut the caller's buffer into one piece per chunk and copied each into its frame (`Conn::start` took a slice). A frame's payload can now be a range of a shared buffer (`Frame::part`, `Conn::start_part`), and `TcpStream::write_all2` sends `tail[range]` after the header; the client sends each piece as a range of the caller's buffer, kept in an `Rc` for retries. Pieces under 16 KiB (`OWN_READ`) are still copied, so they can join other small frames (I283). Other requests to a primary still copy their payload (`to_primary` wraps the new `send_primary` with a slice); none of them is large on the data path. Test: `parts_of_one_buffer_reach_the_peer_as_cut` (three parts of one 3 MiB buffer, cut at unaligned offsets, two sent from the buffer and one copied, arrive as cut); `the_writer_delivers_mixed_frames_in_order` now sends its large frames as the tail of a shared buffer after an offset. | `hyperstor-bench cluster mem 1` on one HGX host (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-09): 1 MiB writes +4%, everything else unchanged. | The front ends still copy into the buffer they hand over (ublk from its I/O buffer, vhost from guest memory); sending from those buffers needs them to outlive the request, which registered buffers (I12) would give. |
| I325 | **The web UI on Vite 8, Tailwind CSS 4 and TypeScript 7, as glidex's (19-administration.md §23.7)** (2026-11-09; branch `deps/ui-vite8-tw4-ts7`): `vite` 8.3.6 with `@vitejs/plugin-react` 6.2.1, `tailwindcss` 4.3.5 through `@tailwindcss/postcss` (`autoprefixer` and `tailwind.config.js` go), TypeScript 7.0.2, React 19.3; the `@glidex/ui-kit` copy moves to glidex `4ff7c0b` (#402), whose classes mean the same in Tailwind 3 and 4. Migrated with `@tailwindcss/upgrade` 4.3.5: the configuration is in `index.css` (`@import`, `@source` for the kit, `@custom-variant dark` for the class-based dark mode), and classes are renamed to keep their v3 size (`shadow` → `shadow-sm`, `rounded` → `rounded-sm`, `rounded-sm` → `rounded-xs`). Kept from v3, as glidex did: borders with no color of their own are gray-200, and buttons keep the pointer cursor. The tool also renamed the word "rounded" in a form label; put back. | Glidex moved first (#402 and its dependabot bumps); the kit is shared, and one stack keeps the two UIs' classes and builds alike. The 13 browser tests pass on the new build. | The e2e suite stays on TypeScript 5.8, as glidex's does. Placeholders take Tailwind 4's default color (a little darker); no test looks at colors. |
| I326 | **Cluster tests hold a stopped node's port (#276)** (2026-11-09; tests only): `Cluster::start` binds each node's reactor port first with a socket that sets `SO_REUSEADDR` and never listens, kept for the cluster's lifetime (`held_listener`), and the node's listener binds the same port. When the node stops, its listener closes but the hold keeps the port, so `restart_as` binds it again. Before, the port was free between the stop and the restart, and another cluster in the same test binary (up to 8 at once since #268) could take it for a listener or as an outgoing connection's ephemeral port; `a_read_past_the_timeout_is_served_from_a_member` failed once that way. | On Linux a second socket can bind a port when both set `SO_REUSEADDR` and the first isn't listening, while a bind without it, `bind(0)`'s choice and `connect`'s ephemeral choice all skip a port that is bound (checked on 6.8: 20,000 `bind(0)` calls never landed on a held port). A connection to the stopped node is still refused, as on a real stopped host, which keeping its listener open would not give (the kernel would accept into the backlog). No retry loop, so no new timing in the tests. | Test harness only; nodes in production bind their configured addresses. |
| I327 | **Buffers aren't zeroed before they are overwritten** (HS5b, D41). Two places zeroed memory that was filled right after: the engine's aligned copy of write data (`AlignedBuf::from_slice` allocated zeroed, then copied), once per run on every node; and the receive buffer of every frame's body and large payload (`vec![0; n]`, then a receive), once per hop. `from_slice` now copies into memory it doesn't zero and zeros only the padding after the data; `TcpStream::read_exact_n` receives into a vector's spare capacity and grows its length only by what the kernel wrote, and `read_n` uses it. Tests: `copies_are_aligned_and_padded_with_zeros` (every length and source offset, after freeing a buffer of 0xee of the same size; fails when the padding isn't zeroed), `read_exact_n_fills_from_pieces_and_reports_an_early_end` (3 MiB in pieces of up to 300 KB, then an early end that keeps the 10 bytes that came). **Not done:** checking the CRCs while copying into the aligned buffer (planned after I306). Measured on the host: the check is limited by the CRC instruction, not by reading memory, so fusing it with the copy saved about 15 µs per MiB (684 → 667 µs for allocation, copy and check, on the main thread); it would move the check into the engine's commit loop, which changes the index before it copies, so a failed check would need a rollback, and the primary would forward data before checking it. | The copy without zeroing costs 15 µs per MiB instead of 28 (EPYC 7742, spawned thread). End to end (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-09): 1 MiB writes +4.5%, the rest unchanged. **Found on the way:** on the main thread, glibc serves an allocation of 256 KiB or more aligned to 4 KiB from fresh pages every time, faulting each page in: about 600 µs per MiB, against 28 µs for a `Vec` of the same size or for the same allocation on another thread. The daemon's reactors run on threads of their own (`hs-reactor-…`) and don't pay it; `hyperstor-bench engine` runs its suites on the main thread, so its large reads and writes include it. | Running the engine benchmarks on a thread of their own would make them match the daemon, but would change their numbers against earlier sections. Open for review. |

The test now runs `status --wait 60` first, as the cache pool test does since I307 and `operations`' and `backup`'s tests do. Test: the one named. | A node down for a moment at the start is the product working (I284); the test means to check status's contents, not a start. | None. |

| I330 | **Workstreams tracked on a GitHub project, not in the repository** (2026-11-09; branch `board/github-project`; asked by the owner): the progress board (`spec/board/*.toml`, generated into `spec/23-progress-board.md` by `scripts/board.py`, checked in CI) is gone. Each workstream is an issue on the [Hyperstor project](https://github.com/orgs/CMKL-APEX/projects/1), with Status (*In progress*, *Blocked*, *In review*, *Merged*, *Deployed*), Owner, Spec milestone and Deployed (commit and date) fields. Milestone progress stays in `docs/public/src/status.md` (`scripts/tracking.py`). | The generated page conflicted on nearly every merge while several sessions worked (#273 took four merges of `main` and four CI runs), and "deployed" was free text. | Whether to make the project public, like the repository (it is private, the issues public). |
| I331 | **`an_outvoted_primary_fetches_a_large_table_in_parts` waits for the repaired copy, not the counter (#288)** (2026-11-09): the test waited until the primary's node counted a scrub repair (`scrub_repaired > 0`), then stopped the cluster and compared the copies. That counter counts every repair the node's scrub makes. It failed once in CI (the primary's table had the stray's 1 entry, not 2000), and its captured output has no "repaired this primary's copy" line, which every passing run prints: the count came from an earlier repair, most likely one made while the 20 batches were written (a replica behind on its last batch outvoted twice, 200 ms apart), and the cluster stopped before the primary fetched. It now waits until a lookup through the primary no longer finds the stray's key, then until the repair is counted (the count follows the push to the members, so it can come later than the fetched copy). Under 6 runs at once on prism2, 60 of 60 pass; with only the lookup and an immediate check of the counter, 15 of 60 failed on the counter, which is what shows the order. | The test is the evidence for I182's fetch in parts; a wait on a node-wide count measured the wrong thing. | Whether light scrub should repair copies that differ only because writes were in flight: the 200 ms recheck makes it rare; pushing the primary's copy to a replica that is only behind is harmless (the primary is newest), but it is counted as a repair. Not changed here. |
| I332 | **glidex pinned at its main `5b56b25a` (D20)** (2026-11-09; branch `deps/glidex-main-c03`): every glidex crate moves from `4d1f5b13` (I320) to one revision. What reaches hyperstor: `glidex-raftstore` applies each batch of committed Raft entries in one transaction (glidex D32), and gains checkpointed applies (glidex D31: applies commit without a flush, with a durable checkpoint every 100 entries or 1 s), off unless `RaftSettings::checkpointed_applies` is set, so the map service keeps durable applies; `glidex-auth` moves to `reqwest` 0.13 (glidex #389), whose default TLS is rustls with the platform verifier instead of native-tls, so OpenSSL leaves `Cargo.lock`; `glidex-topology` adds GPU pool discovery, unused here. `hyperstor-meta`'s `settings()` names the new field as `false`; no other code changes. | One glidex for every crate (I320's reason). Batched applies are the same state after each batch, and the map service's watchers are told of each entry in order, so nothing that watches the store sees a difference; the store's file format is unchanged (still redb 4.5). The OIDC client's TLS checks the provider's certificate against the system's roots through rustls; checked in glidex: public sites succeed, a self-signed certificate is refused. | Whether the map service should take checkpointed applies (fewer flushes per committed change, the same durability through the Raft log): to measure with the map service's commit rate, not done here. |
| I333 | **The installer's udev rules move to `61-hyperstor.rules`; under `60-hyperstor.rules` they never matched** (2026-11-10; branch `feat/storage-host-devices`): udev reads rules files in the order of their names across `/usr/lib/udev/rules.d` and `/etc/udev/rules.d`, and `60-hyperstor.rules` sorts before `60-persistent-storage.rules`, which makes the `/dev/disk/by-id/` links our rule matches in `DEVLINKS`; so the disk kept `root:disk` and only the installer's `chown` when it added the device (I207) let `hyperstord` open it, until the next reboot. Found adding an NVMe drive by hand on a new pilot host (Ubuntu 24.04, systemd 255): the node failed with `Permission denied`; `udevadm test` applied no `OWNER` from `60-hyperstor.rules` and applied it from the same line in a `99-` file. Now `UDEV_RULES` is `/etc/udev/rules.d/61-hyperstor.rules`; every install run removes the old file (listed as `removed …`), the new one is written and udev reloaded and triggered as before; uninstall removes both. Tests: `hyperstor_install::tests::udev_rules_come_after_the_by_id_links`, `manage::the_installer_adds_a_device_to_a_node` (the old file removed by a later run). | Renaming keeps the match on the by-id name the operator gave, which `/dev/nvmeXnY` isn't; matching `ID_SERIAL` instead would need its own name per bus. 61 is the first name after the links; nothing later in a stock Ubuntu sets a disk's owner. | Pilot hosts with an NVMe drive added (I207) lose it at their next reboot until they run this installer, which rewrites the rules; check with `udevadm test /sys/block/NAME` that `61-hyperstor.rules` sets `OWNER`. |
| I334 | **`--device` on storage-only hosts, with the node's certificate (D280)** (2026-11-10; branch `feat/storage-host-devices`): `AddDevice` takes a node's certificate for its own node, at an IP one of its devices has in the published map, without a class; other nodes, other IPs and classes are refused with `permission_denied` and audited (`refused`). The installer's `--device` uses the administrator's certificate where `admin.key` is, else `node.key` through the API in `join`, and calls `AddDevice` itself (`api_call`, as for endpoints) instead of running `hyperstorctl`, which only reads `admin.crt`; the leader's address in a follower's refusal and an address already registered are handled as before. Tests: `join::storage_hosts_join_with_tokens` (a joined host's certificate refused for node 1, for `127.0.0.2` and with a class; `install --node 0 --device FILE` on that host adds the device). | One client for both certificates; `hyperstorctl --tls DIR` reads only `admin.*`, and copying a node key under that name is what the tests do, not an operator. The IP is checked against the published map: a node changes its addresses only through a join, and an older map only refuses more. | Before this, the new pilot host's drive was added by hand (`hyperstorctl device add` on a member host, `format-device`, a `devices` line in `install.conf`). Not yet run on a real storage-only host with this build. |
| I338 | **Write data goes to the device from the buffer it arrived in** (HS5b, D41; 2026-11-10; branch `perf/hs5b-aligned-receive`). A replicated 1 MiB write was copied four times on its way to the media: on the primary, `Rc::unwrap_or_clone` cloned the payload while the replicas' frames still held it, then the engine copied it into an aligned buffer; each replica made that aligned copy too. Now a storage node receives a request's payload of 16 KiB or more into a buffer aligned to a block (`rpc::read_request`, `AlignedBuf::for_receive`, `TcpStream::read_exact_into`), the payload is an `IoBuf` (`hyperstor-codec`: shared, sliceable, `Arc`-counted bytes over a `Vec` or an `AlignedBuf`) that the replicas' frames and `Op::Write` share, and the engine queues each run of an aligned payload as a slice of it (`WriteBuf::Shared`), copying only data that doesn't start on a block (small payloads, local writes). An operation list's encoded body is shared with replicas the same way instead of copied for each. `AlignedBuf` moved from the engine to `hyperstor-codec` (the engine re-exports it) so the wire side can receive into it. Two `unsafe` contracts carry it: `AlignedBuf::for_receive` leaves the data uninitialized until the receive has written all of it (a failed receive drops it unread), and `RecvBuf` promises the reactor memory that stays put while the buffer moves. Tests: `slices_share_the_bytes_and_keep_their_alignment`, `read_exact_into_fills_the_callers_buffer_from_pieces` (pieces of 7919 bytes, the rest of the buffer untouched, then an early end), `large_request_payloads_arrive_aligned` (sizes on both sides of 16 KiB, after frames that leave the stream at any offset), `an_aligned_write_goes_to_the_device_from_where_it_arrived` (the round's write is the payload's own memory when aligned, and an aligned copy otherwise; both read back). **Found on the way:** the first version wrapped every frame's payload in an `IoBuf`, replies and empty payloads included, and every engine write in one: an `Arc` allocation each, about ten per 4 KiB write across the client and three nodes, and 4 KiB writes lost 1–2% in two A/B runs. Ordinary frame reads now keep plain vectors, an empty `IoBuf` holds no allocation, and the engine's own buffers aren't wrapped; 4 KiB writes are then within the runs' range. | End to end (02-benchmarks.md, 2026-11-10): 1 MiB writes +9.9%, the rest within the run-to-run range. A received payload takes up to 4 KiB more memory (padding to whole blocks), and a piece of it keeps the whole buffer until the last piece is written. | I12 expected registered buffers to remove the copy; alignment alone does. Registering buffers with io_uring (`IORING_REGISTER_BUFFERS`) saves pinning pages for each I/O, and stays planned until a profile shows it. Open for review. |
